1 / 26

caveman

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/caveman/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the caveman skill be improved? The skill enables ultra-compressed LLM communication across 6 intensity levels (lite, full, ultra, wenyan-lite, wenyan-full, wenyan-ultra), claiming ~75% token reduction. It has rules for dropping filler, auto-clarity exceptions, and boundaries for code/commits. It's a single 130-line SKILL.md with no evals, no references directory, and no bundled scripts. The skill is part of the oh-my-slop skills repo for the pi agent harness. Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a foundational credibility problem: the '~75% token reduction' claim in the description is completely unsubstantiated. There's no eval, no benchmark, no before/after measurement anywhere. It's a marketing number attached to a technical tool, and any agent that takes it at face value is being misled. In practice, the actual reduction depends heavily on the baseline — if the agent already writes reasonably terse responses, the delta is maybe 10-15%, not 75%.

The wenyan variants are a trap. They add 3 full intensity levels that almost no user will ever invoke, yet they consume description tokens and add cognitive load to the intensity table. The examples show wenyan-full mixing English and Chinese characters ("池reuse open connection"), which is neither proper 文言文 nor useful English — it's the worst of both worlds. If the skill can't demonstrate clean classical Chinese, it shouldn't offer the option.

The auto-clarity section is the only genuinely valuable part of this skill, but it's buried at line 40+. The rule 'Drop caveman for security warnings, irreversible actions, multi-step sequences' should be the FIRST rule, not an afterthought. Getting this wrong means a user sees 'rm -rf /data → gone. fast.' instead of a proper warning. That's not token efficiency, that's a liability.

The persistence model ('ACTIVE EVERY RESPONSE. No revert after many turns') is naive. Sessions are long. Context windows fill. A caveman-mode response that was appropriate at turn 3 becomes absurd at turn 47 when the user is asking for a detailed architecture review. The skill needs a decay or re-evaluation mechanism, not a blunt 'stay on until told off' rule.

The description triggers on 'less tokens' and 'be brief' — but those phrases are also used when someone wants a short answer to a simple question, not a persistent communication mode. This will overtrigger and annoy users who wanted a one-line answer, not a session-long caveman persona.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the persona. What problem does this skill actually solve? There are two distinct problems being conflated:

1. **Token economy**: The user wants to reduce token consumption for cost or context-window reasons.

2. **Communication style**: The user wants terse, direct responses without filler.

These are related but not identical. You can reduce tokens by being more selective about what you include (shorter code examples, fewer redundant explanations) without changing your prose style at all. Conversely, you can write caveman-style prose that still uses the same number of tokens as normal prose if the content is complex.

The skill should start by asking: what's the actual constraint? If it's token cost, the real lever is response length and example verbosity, not article-dropping. If it's readability, the real lever is structure and information density, not caveman affect.

The intensity levels reveal this confusion. 'Lite' is just 'write well' — drop filler, be direct. That's not a special mode, that's baseline competence. 'Full' adds article-dropping and fragments, which is a stylistic choice, not a token optimization. 'Ultra' adds abbreviations that sacrifice readability for marginal token savings. The levels aren't measuring the same axis — they're mixing style, compression, and readability into one slider.

The wenyan variants are a separate skill entirely. Classical Chinese compression is a fascinating problem, but it belongs in its own skill with proper linguistic grounding, not bolted onto a caveman mode as an afterthought.

My recommendation: split the token-efficiency advice (which belongs in general agent guidance) from the communication style (which could be a lightweight preference). The skill should be 30 lines, not 130.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a much bigger opportunity than it realizes. 'Compressed communication' is a feature; 'adaptive communication density' is a product.

First, the skill should support domain-specific compression dictionaries. A caveman response about Kubernetes should abbreviate differently than one about React. The current skill has generic abbreviations (DB, auth, config) but no mechanism for domain-specific ones. A `references/` directory with per-domain abbreviation tables would make the ultra mode dramatically more effective.

Second, the skill should measure and report its own compression ratio. After each response, it could optionally output a small metric: 'Tokens: 340 (est. normal: 1,200, reduction: 72%)'. This turns the unsubstantiated 75% claim into a live, verifiable metric. Over time, this data would be incredibly valuable for tuning.

Third, there's an untapped market in multi-agent communication. When agents talk to each other (subagent handoffs, council debates, orchestration), caveman mode should be the default. The skill could include a section on inter-agent compression — a protocol for agent-to-agent communication that's even more aggressive than human-facing caveman.

Fourth, the wenyan variants hint at something bigger: multilingual compression. Different languages have different information density. Japanese, Chinese, and German compress differently. The skill could evolve into a general 'dense communication' framework that selects the most efficient language/register for the content type.

Fifth, there's no eval suite. For a skill that makes a quantitative claim (75% reduction), the absence of evals is a glaring gap. Even a simple before/after comparison on 10 common task types would be more evidence than exists today.

🟡 The Outsider

I just read this skill and I'm confused about several things that are probably obvious to the author but not to a fresh reader.

What does 'ACTIVE EVERY RESPONSE' mean in practice? If I'm in a conversation and the agent suddenly starts talking like a caveman, do I need to explicitly turn it on every session? Or does it persist across sessions? The skill says 'Level persist until changed or session end' — but what defines a session? If I close my terminal and come back, is that a new session? This needs a clear onboarding flow.

The intensity table says ultra uses 'arrows for causality (X → Y)' but the ultra example uses '→' while the full example also uses '→' ('Inline object prop = new ref = re-render'). Wait, that uses '=' not '→'. The examples don't consistently demonstrate the differences between levels. I can't tell what distinguishes full from ultra by reading the examples.

The auto-clarity section says 'Drop caveman for multi-step sequences where fragment order risks misread' but doesn't define what counts as a multi-step sequence. Is it 3 steps? 5? A bullet list? A numbered procedure? I need a concrete heuristic, not a vague guideline.

The 'Boundaries' section says 'Code/commits/PRs: write normal.' But what about code comments? What about commit messages? What about PR descriptions? These are all text associated with code but not code itself. The boundary is fuzzy.

The description says 'Cuts token usage ~75%' but I have no way to verify this. Is this measured? Averaged across what types of responses? For a skill that's about precision and efficiency, this number is suspiciously round and unsupported.

Finally, the skill has no 'When NOT to use this skill' section. Every good skill tells you when to step back. This one doesn't, which means agents will apply it in situations where it's harmful (explanations to beginners, sensitive communications, documentation).

🟠 The Executor

Here's what I'd do Monday morning, in order:

**1. Fix the description.** It's trying to do too much. The current description lists the skill's features (intensity levels, token reduction claim) instead of just triggering conditions. Rewrite to: 'Use when the user wants terse, compressed responses with minimal filler. Supports multiple intensity levels from light compression to maximum abbreviation.' Cut the 75% claim from the description — it belongs in the body with evidence, or it doesn't belong at all.

**2. Add a before/after calibration table.** The single highest-value addition would be a table showing the same response at all 6 intensity levels. Not 6 different examples — the SAME example at all 6 levels. This lets agents calibrate and lets users see exactly what they're getting. Currently the examples are different questions at different levels, which is useless for calibration.

**3. Move auto-clarity to the top.** The safety-critical rule (don't compress warnings and irreversible actions) should be rule #1, not a section buried halfway through. This is a 5-minute edit that prevents the most dangerous failure mode.

**4. Cut or defer the wenyan variants.** They add complexity, they're not properly executed (wenyan-full mixes English and Chinese), and they'll be used by maybe 0.1% of users. Move them to a separate skill or a reference file. This alone would reduce the skill by 20%.

**5. Add one eval.** Just one. Take a standard response, run it through caveman at each level, measure the token count. Put the numbers in the skill. This replaces the 75% claim with actual data.

Total time: about 2 hours. Impact: the skill goes from hand-wavy to credible.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

Response E (The Executor). It's the only one that gives a concrete, sequenced action plan. The before/after calibration table idea is the single most impactful suggestion in the entire debate — it solves the calibration problem, the evidence problem, and the example-quality problem in one move. Every other advisor identified problems; this one gave a build order.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response C (The Expansionist). It goes on a massive feature rampage — compression dictionaries, live metrics, inter-agent protocols, multilingual frameworks — without acknowledging that the core skill can't even substantiate its basic 75% claim. Building a compression measurement system on top of an unmeasured foundation is putting the cart before the horse. The multi-agent communication idea is interesting but belongs in a completely different skill.

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed skill composition — what happens when caveman mode is active AND another skill (like court-jester or documentation-lifecycle) also modifies response style? The skill has no guidance on precedence or conflict resolution. Also, no one questioned whether the skill should have any persistence at all, or if it should be a per-response modifier rather than a session-long mode.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

Response D (The Outsider). It caught the most actionable clarity issues that every other advisor glossed over. The question about what defines a 'session' for persistence purposes is critical — if the agent persists caveman mode across sessions when the user didn't intend it, that's a bad experience. The observation that the examples don't consistently differentiate intensity levels is a direct, fixable flaw. And the missing 'When NOT to use' section is a real gap that could cause harm.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response A (The Contrarian). It's so focused on tearing down the 75% claim that it dismisses the entire skill's value. But a skill doesn't need precise metrics to be useful — 'be more terse and drop filler' is valuable guidance even without a token counter. The Contrarian also calls the wenyan variants 'a trap' when they're clearly marked as optional intensity levels that users can simply not select. Not every feature needs to be for everyone.

What All Missed

No one addressed the interaction between caveman mode and non-English users. The skill is entirely designed around English-language compression (dropping articles, using short English synonyms). For users whose primary language isn't English, the skill's advice may be irrelevant or harmful. The AGENTS.md rules require asking about language preferences for web apps — this skill should at least acknowledge the limitation.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

Response B (The First Principles Thinker). It correctly identifies the core architectural flaw: the skill conflates token economy with communication style. This is the deepest insight in the debate. The observation that 'lite' is just 'write well' and shouldn't be a special mode at all is sharp. The recommendation to split token-efficiency advice from communication style would fundamentally improve the skill's design, even if the final implementation keeps them together for convenience.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response C (The Expansionist). The live compression ratio metric sounds great in theory but would itself consume tokens to display, partially defeating the purpose. The inter-agent compression protocol is a fascinating research idea but wildly out of scope for a 130-line communication style skill. And the multilingual compression framework is a PhD thesis, not a skill improvement. Every suggestion here increases complexity without addressing the skill's actual weaknesses.

What All Missed

No one discussed the skill's testability. The skill-creator skill emphasizes that all skills need evals, yet caveman has none. How do you test that caveman mode actually produces more terse responses? How do you test that auto-clarity fires correctly? The skill should include at least 3-5 test prompts with expected characteristics (e.g., 'response should not contain articles', 'response to a DROP TABLE request should include a full warning'). This is a repo requirement that's completely unaddressed.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

Response E (The Executor). The five-step plan is exactly right, and the prioritization is correct. Fix the description first (it's the trigger mechanism), add calibration data second (it's the credibility foundation), move safety rules third (it's the risk mitigation). The suggestion to cut wenyan variants is pragmatic — they can always be added back as a reference file if demand exists. This is the only response that respects the skill's current scope while making it materially better.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response A (The Contrarian). While the 75% critique is valid, the Contrarian doesn't offer a constructive path forward beyond 'this claim is unsubstantiated.' Okay, so what number should go there? What measurement methodology would be credible? The Contrarian tears down without building up. Also, calling the persistence model 'naive' without proposing an alternative is incomplete — what SHOULD the persistence model be? Per-response? Per-conversation? Time-decayed?

What All Missed

None of the advisors considered the skill's position in the broader skill ecosystem. The oh-my-slop repo has 20+ skills. How does caveman interact with the django skill, the drf skill, the documentation-lifecycle skill? If an agent is using caveman mode AND the documentation-lifecycle skill simultaneously, which style wins? The skill needs a 'Composition' section explaining how it interacts with other active skills, or at minimum a statement that it only affects prose style, not structural requirements from other skills.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

Response D (The Outsider). The questions it raises are the ones that will generate the most user confusion if left unanswered. 'What counts as a multi-step sequence?' is something every agent will wonder the first time auto-clarity should fire. The boundary fuzziness around code comments vs. code itself is a real edge case that will cause inconsistent behavior. And the missing 'When NOT to use' section is a genuine safety gap — applying caveman to a beginner asking for a gentle explanation is a bad outcome that the skill should explicitly prevent.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response B (The First Principles Thinker). While the token-vs-style insight is intellectually correct, the recommendation to 'split into two skills' ignores the practical reality that users want one toggle for terse mode. The theoretical purity of separating concerns doesn't map to user mental models. Users don't think 'I want token optimization' vs. 'I want terse prose' — they think 'stop wasting my time and tokens.' The skill can acknowledge the distinction internally without splitting into two separate skills.

What All Missed

No one addressed the skill's onboarding experience. When a user first activates caveman mode, what happens? Is there an acknowledgment? A brief example of what to expect? The skill jumps straight into rules without any onboarding. A new user who says '/caveman' gets terse responses with no context for why the agent suddenly changed its communication style. A one-line onboarding message ('Caveman mode active. Responses will be compressed. Use /caveman lite|full|ultra to adjust. Use /caveman off to disable.') would significantly improve the experience.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council converges on five points. (1) The 75% token reduction claim is unsubstantiated and needs evidence or removal. (2) The auto-clarity safety rules should be the first thing agents see, not buried mid-skill. (3) The examples are poorly calibrated — different questions at different levels don't help agents or users compare intensity. (4) The wenyan variants add complexity without proper execution and should be deferred to a separate skill or reference file. (5) The description overtriggers by including 'less tokens' and 'be brief' which map to simple short-answer requests, not persistent communication modes.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on fundamental architecture. The First Principles Thinker wants to split the skill into two (token efficiency vs. communication style). The Expansionist wants to expand it into a comprehensive compression platform with metrics, dictionaries, and inter-agent protocols. The Executor wants to fix the existing skill in 2 hours. The Contrarian thinks the credibility problem is fatal without evidence. The Outsider thinks the clarity and onboarding gaps are the primary issue. The chairman sides with the Executor's pragmatic approach as the immediate path, but adopts the First Principles insight as the long-term design direction: the skill should internally separate token-reduction advice from style guidance, even if shipped as one file.

Blind Spots Caught

Three things the entire council missed. First, skill composition: no one addressed how caveman interacts with other active skills that also modify response behavior. Second, testability: the skill has zero evals in a repo that mandates them, and no one proposed a concrete eval strategy. Third, non-English users: the skill's compression rules are entirely English-centric (article-dropping, English synonyms) with no guidance for other languages, which conflicts with the repo's own language-awareness requirements.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill in three phases. Phase 1 (immediate, 2 hours): Rewrite the description to remove the 75% claim and overtriggering phrases. Move auto-clarity to rule #1. Add a before/after calibration table using the same example at all 6 levels. Cut wenyan variants to a separate reference file. Phase 2 (short-term, 1 day): Add 3-5 test prompts as evals. Add a 'When NOT to use' section. Add a 'Composition' section for multi-skill interaction. Add a one-line onboarding acknowledgment. Phase 3 (medium-term): Build a simple token measurement eval to replace the 75% claim with actual data. Consider splitting token-efficiency guidance from style guidance into separate reference files.

First Step

Add a before/after calibration table showing one response at all 6 intensity levels. This single change addresses the biggest credibility gap (no evidence for compression claims), the biggest calibration problem (agents can't distinguish levels), and the biggest example quality problem (current examples aren't comparable). It's a 30-minute edit that transforms the skill from hand-wavy to demonstrable.

2 / 26

codebase-design

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/codebase-design/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the codebase-design skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a foundational problem: it teaches vocabulary without teaching judgment. You define 'depth' as leverage at the interface, but you never give the reader a decision procedure for when deepening is wrong. Everything reads like deepening is always good. It isn't. Deep modules that aggregate unrelated responsibilities are just God objects with a vocabulary upgrade.

The dependency classification in deepening.md is the strongest part, but it has a glaring gap: category 3 (Remote but owned) and category 4 (True external) are structurally identical — both use injected ports with adapters. The distinction is 'owned vs. not owned,' but from the module's perspective, the design is the same. You're splitting on organizational ownership, not structural difference. That's confusing for someone trying to classify their own dependencies.

The 'Design It Twice' pattern is clever but operationally underspecified. You say 'spawn 3+ sub-agents' with different constraints, but you don't say what to do when two agents converge on similar designs (which happens frequently). You also don't handle the case where all four proposals are bad — there's no escape hatch or 'start over' signal.

The rejected framings section is defensive in a way that feels like it's protecting the author's ego rather than helping the reader. 'We use depth-as-leverage instead' — fine, but prove it. Show me a module where Ousterhout's line-ratio says 'deep' and your leverage definition says 'shallow.' Without that counterexample, this is just philosophy.

The glossary forbids 'component,' 'service,' 'API,' and 'boundary' — but doesn't tell me what to say when I'm working in a codebase that already uses those terms. Translation guidance is missing. Am I supposed to refactor everyone's vocabulary?

Overall: strong conceptual core, weak on practical guidance, and dangerously one-sided in advocating depth.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the vocabulary and ask: what problem are we actually solving here? Two things. First, designers need a shared language so they can reason about module shape without talking past each other. Second, designers need heuristics for finding the right seams in existing code. Everything else is secondary.

The glossary nails the first problem. 'Interface means everything a caller must know' — that's the right call. Most interface discussions collapse to type signatures; this definition correctly includes invariants, ordering, error modes, and performance. That's valuable.

But the second problem — finding seams in existing code — is where the skill is incomplete. You give dependency categories and the 'one adapter means hypothetical, two adapters means real' heuristic, but you never give a workflow for actually surveying a codebase. How do I scan a directory tree and identify shallow clusters? What's the entry point? The skill assumes I already know what I want to deepen, but in practice, finding the candidate is half the battle.

The deletion test is the closest thing to a diagnostic tool, but it's purely thought-experiment. Make it operational: look for modules where the public methods mostly delegate to other modules' public methods. That's a shallow module you can detect by static analysis or even grep.

The 'interface is the test surface' principle is profound but under-explained. The implication — that tests which reach past the interface are a design smell — deserves its own section with before/after examples. Right now it's a bullet point that most readers will nod at and ignore.

The 'Design It Twice' process is genuinely good. The parallel sub-agent pattern with forced different constraints is sound engineering judgment. But it's positioned as an advanced technique. The real question is: what's the simplest possible path from 'I have a messy module cluster' to 'I have a deep module with a clean seam'? The skill should front-load that path.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill sits at the intersection of three massive leverage points: code design, AI-assisted development, and onboarding. Right now it only fully serves the first.

For AI-assisted development: the skill should explicitly call out that the vocabulary here makes prompts more precise. 'Make this module deeper' is actionable for an LLM in a way that 'clean this up' isn't. 'Introduce a seam at the adapter for the payment gateway' is the kind of instruction that produces reliable AI output. This skill should tell users: use this vocabulary in your AI prompts, and tell the AI to use it back.

For onboarding: the 'Going deeper' section is a stub. This should be a full onboarding guide — a sequence of exercises someone new to the codebase does to internalize the vocabulary. 'Find the shallowest module in the codebase and explain why it's shallow using the deletion test.' 'Identify three real seams and their adapters.' 'Propose a deepening and classify its dependencies.' Without this, the skill is reference material, not a teaching tool.

The skill should also connect to architectural patterns it currently ignores. Clean Architecture, Hexagonal Architecture, and Ports & Adapters are all doing the same thing this skill does with different names. A mapping table — 'what we call a seam, Clean Architecture calls a port boundary' — would make this skill useful to people coming from those traditions instead of forcing them to learn a new vocabulary that maps 1:1 to concepts they already have.

The 'Design It Twice' pattern could be a product feature: a built-in pi command or extension that automates the sub-agent spawning with pre-built constraint templates. Right now it's a manual process described in prose. Make it executable.

Finally, the skill is missing a gallery of real-world examples. Not contrived Python snippets — real anonymized cases from actual codebases showing before/after deepening with the vocabulary applied. That's what would make this skill stick.

🟡 The Outsider

Reading this cold, I can't figure out when I'm supposed to use this skill. The description says 'use when designing or improving a module's interface' — but that's nearly all software design. The trigger is so broad it's meaningless. Be specific: use this skill when you have a cluster of 3+ modules that feel tangled and you're not sure where to cut. Use it when you're introducing a new dependency and need to decide whether to inject it. Use it when a test suite has 50 tests for a module with 3 public methods — that's a depth problem.

The glossary is well-written but I needed to read it three times to internalize the distinctions. A few things tripped me up:

  • 'Module' is defined as 'anything with an interface and an implementation,' but then in the diagrams, 'module' is the box containing both. So is the module the thing, or the boundary? This is never clarified.
  • 'Implementation' is distinguished from 'Adapter' but the example given (Postgres repo vs. in-memory fake) is confusing. Aren't both adapters? The Postgres repo satisfies an interface and the fake satisfies the same interface — isn't that exactly the adapter concept? Calling one 'implementation' and the other 'adapter' seems arbitrary unless you explain WHY one gets the adapter label.
  • 'Seam' is attributed to Michael Feathers and defined as a location, but then you say 'the seam is where the interface lives' and also 'an adapter sits at a seam.' That's the seam being three things: a location, an interface, and an attachment point. Pick one.

The diagrams are good but too minimal. A 'deep vs. shallow' comparison would be more compelling with a concrete example — even a toy one. Show me a shallow Logger module and a deep Logger module side by side.

The 'Rejected framings' section is the most interesting part of the document, and it's buried at the bottom. Put it earlier — it's the section that tells practitioners who already know other frameworks why THIS vocabulary is better. It's persuasive content that's currently hidden.

Also: the license says 'MIT (adapted from mattpocock/skills)' — if this is a derivative work, is the adaptation substantial enough? Does the license notice need to be more prominent? I'm not a lawyer, but that footnote feels thin for what appears to be a restructured version of someone else's framework.

🟠 The Executor

If I have 30 minutes to improve this skill, here's what I'd do — in order:

1. Add a 'Quick Start' section at the top of SKILL.md. Right now the skill opens with a glossary. Nobody reads a glossary first. Open with a 5-step workflow: (1) Identify a shallow module cluster, (2) Classify its dependencies, (3) Propose a seam, (4) Write two adapters, (5) Test at the interface. Everything else is reference material.

2. Fix the 'implementation vs. adapter' distinction in the glossary. The current definition is confusing. Simpler rule: an adapter is an implementation interchangeable with another at the same seam. If there's only one implementation, just call it the implementation. If there are two or more (prod + test, or two strategies), call them adapters. This is testable and immediately clarifying.

3. Merge the deepening.md dependency categories 3 and 4. They're the same mechanism — injected port, adapter pattern. The question of whether you own the remote service doesn't change the module design. Split on something that matters structurally, like sync vs. async adapters, or collapse them into one category with sub-types.

4. Add one worked example to deepening.md. End-to-end: here's a shallow cluster, here are the dependency classifications, here's the seam, here's the deepened module, here's the before/after test count. Without this, the skill is all theory.

5. Add trigger examples to the frontmatter description. Concrete, not abstract. 'Use when you have a 200-line class with 15 public methods that mostly delegate to other classes' hits harder than 'use when designing interfaces.'

Everything else — the AI prompt guidance, the onboarding exercises, the gallery of real examples — is phase 2. These five changes make the skill usable today.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor (E) is strongest because every suggestion is concrete and sequenced. The 5-step quick start would transform the skill from reference material into a working tool. The fix for the 'implementation vs. adapter' distinction alone resolves the most confusing part of the glossary.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist (C) has the biggest blind spot. Mapping to Clean Architecture and Hexagonal Architecture sounds valuable but would dilute the skill's tight focus. The whole point is a specific vocabulary — adding translation tables to every other architecture framework turns this into a Rosetta Stone instead of a design tool. The AI-prompting angle is genuinely useful though.

What All Missed

All five responses miss that the skill has no guidance for what to do AFTER deepening. Once you've deepened a module, how do you know it worked? What are the success metrics? Fewer tests? Fewer call sites? Faster onboarding for new developers? There's no before/after validation framework anywhere — the skill tells you how to do it but never how to verify the result.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker (B) is strongest for identifying that the skill diagnoses healthy modules but not sick ones. The suggestion to make the deletion test operational — 'find modules whose public methods mostly delegate to other modules' public methods' — is the single most actionable improvement proposed by any advisor. It turns a gut-feel heuristic into a grep-able rule.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian (A) has the biggest blind spot on dependency categories 3 and 4. Yes, they use the same mechanism, but the Executor (E) agrees — and dismissing the distinction as 'organizational, not structural' misses that organizational ownership absolutely changes the adapter design. You control versioning on your own services; you don't on Stripe's API. That's a design-relevant difference even if the seam mechanism is the same.

What All Missed

All five miss that the skill has a discoverability problem for non-Python examples. The testability section is entirely Python (type hints, os.environ). The skill should either explicitly scope itself to Python or add examples in 2-3 other languages. As written, it reads as Python-specific despite claiming to be language-agnostic.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Executor (E) wins on pragmatic impact per unit of effort. Every suggestion can be implemented in under an hour. The trigger examples for the frontmatter description are especially good — the current description is so broad it would trigger on virtually any coding task, causing alert fatigue. Specific, concrete triggers would make the skill fire when it's actually needed.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist (C) overreaches on the 'Design It Twice automation' suggestion. Turning the sub-agent pattern into a pi extension is a large project that would delay shipping the skill improvements everyone else identified. It's a nice-to-have that would consume the time budget needed for the quick start section and the worked example.

What All Missed

All five responses ignore the 'Design It Twice' failure modes. What happens when sub-agents produce convergent designs? What happens when the user can't evaluate four radically different interfaces? The skill says 'be opinionated' in the recommendation step, but gives no rubric for resolving cases where the agent can't form a strong opinion. This is a real-world scenario that needs handling.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker (B) asks the right meta-question: 'what's the simplest path from messy cluster to clean seam?' Every other advisor assumes the reader is already motivated and just needs better tools. B correctly identifies that the skill needs a workflow, not just principles and vocabulary. The gap between 'here are dependency categories' and 'here's your action plan' is where most users will bounce off.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider (D) makes fair points about the glossary but then suggests moving 'Rejected framings' earlier would help. It wouldn't. That section is for committed readers, not newcomers. Novices need concrete examples before they can appreciate why one framing was rejected over another. The Outsider's suggestion would put the most abstract content first.

What All Missed

All five responses treat the skill as if it only applies to greenfield or refactor scenarios. None address how to apply deepening vocabulary during code review. 'This module is shallow — the interface surface is nearly as large as the implementation' would be an incredibly useful code review comment pattern. Adding a code review checklist or comment templates would expand the skill's utility without adding conceptual weight.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Contrarian (A) is strongest for the 'dangerously one-sided' observation. The skill presents depth as universally desirable with no counterexamples of when shallow is appropriate. A library of small, focused utilities SHOULD be shallow — that's the Unix philosophy. The skill would be far more credible with a 'when NOT to deepen' section. The vocabulary is only half the picture; the judgment of when to apply it is the other half.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider (D) focuses heavily on wording-level confusion in the glossary's seam definition but doesn't recognize that this confusion comes from the intentional layering of a concept across abstraction levels. 'Seam as location' and 'seam as interface' aren't contradictory — they're the same concept viewed from outside vs. inside. The skill could clarify this, but the Outsider treats it as a bug rather than a feature of a rich concept.

What All Missed

All five responses miss that the skill says nothing about退化 (regression). What happens when a deep module's requirements grow and the module starts getting fat? How do you know when a deep module has become a God object? There's no guidance on splitting an overgrown deep module, which is the natural lifecycle concern. The skill teaches birth but not adolescence or death.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees the skill needs a workflow, not just vocabulary and principles. Every advisor identified that the gap between 'here are concepts' and 'here's what you do Tuesday morning' is the core weakness. There's also consensus that the frontmatter description is too broad for reliable triggering, and that the implementation/adapter distinction in the glossary needs sharpening. Five different advisors arrived at these same three conclusions from completely different angles.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on dependency categories. The Contrarian and Executor say merge categories 3 and 4 (remote-but-owned vs. true external); Reviewer 2 argues this ignores design-relevant differences in versioning control. The council also clashes on whether to map this skill's vocabulary to other architecture traditions (Expansionist says yes, Reviewer 1 says it dilutes focus). On the 'seam' definition — the Outsider finds it incoherent; Reviewer 5 argues it's intentionally multi-layered.

Blind Spots Caught

Three blind spots escaped unanimous attention but were individually caught: (1) no 'when NOT to deepen' guidance — depth isn't always good; (2) no regression/ lifecycle guidance for when deep modules grow fat; (3) no code review integration — the vocabulary would be powerful in PR comments but the skill never says so. The most critical blind spot is #1: teaching a principle without teaching its limits is how principles become cargo cults.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill around a 5-step workflow front and center. Fix the implementation/adapter distinction. Add a worked example end-to-end. Add a 'when NOT to deepen' section. Sharpen the frontmatter triggers with concrete code smells. Merge dependency categories 3 and 4 or add a structural distinction that matters. Add a code review checklist. Defer the Expansionist's ideas (AI prompting, onboarding exercises, gallery) to a v2 — they're valuable but would delay shipping the core improvements.

First Step

Write the 5-step quick start section and add it to the top of SKILL.md, before the glossary. Everything else orbits around having a workflow entry point. Until there's a workflow, the skill is a dictionary, not a tool.

3 / 26

council

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/council/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the LLM Council skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a structural identity crisis. It's simultaneously a tutorial on council methodology, a sub-agent orchestration manual, and a reference document — but it commits fully to none of these roles. The result is a 250-line document where the actual process (steps 1-6) occupies maybe 40% of the space. The rest is advisor descriptions, prompt templates, and an extended example that feels like a blog post.

The sub-agent prompt templates are the weakest part. They're verbose, hedging, and full of instructions like 'Be direct and specific. Don't hedge.' — which is itself hedging. If you need to tell an agent not to hedge, your framing is wrong. The prompts should be tight, declarative, and assume competence.

The court-jester handoff is over-engineered. A table mapping question types to modes, constraints about 'one mode only,' a prohibition on 'reference copying' — this is process theater. In practice, the Contrarian either loads a mode or doesn't. The elaborate rules add cognitive load without improving output quality.

The 'important notes' section at the bottom is where the actual wisdom lives, but it's buried after the example. The most actionable guidance ('always spawn in parallel,' 'anonymize for peer review,' 'chairman can disagree') should be at the top, not the bottom.

The framing step (Step 1) is the most valuable part of the entire skill, yet it's described in a single paragraph. The 30-second workspace scan is the difference between generic advice and specific, grounded advice. This deserves its own section with a concrete checklist, not a throwaway line.

There's no failure mode guidance. What happens when a sub-agent times out? When an advisor produces a 50-word response instead of 150-300? When the chairman can't reach consensus? The skill assumes everything works perfectly.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the templates, the example, the advisor descriptions. What's the irreducible core of this skill? It's a protocol for structured disagreement. That's it. The value is not in the specific advisors or the peer review format — it's in forcing multiple independent perspectives into a single decision.

From that foundation, the current design has a fundamental flaw: it's too prescriptive about the number five. Why five advisors? Why not three for quick decisions or seven for high-stakes ones? The skill should be parameterized by decision importance. A $10,000 decision deserves more perspectives than a $100 one.

The peer review step is the most interesting design choice, but it's under-examined. The skill says 'anonymize as A-E' but doesn't explain why this matters or when it fails. If all five advisors are the same model with different prompts, anonymization is theater — the reviewers will recognize stylistic patterns. The skill should address this honestly.

The chairman synthesis is the actual product of the council. Everything else is input. Yet the chairman gets the least detailed instructions. The prompt template is a fill-in-the-blanks structure with no guidance on how to weigh conflicting advice, how to handle advisor quality variance, or when to reject the majority.

The skill conflates two different use cases: (1) helping a user make a better decision, and (2) demonstrating that multi-perspective analysis is valuable. Use case (1) wants speed and clarity. Use case (2) wants thoroughness and transparency. The current skill tries to serve both and serves neither well.

The framing step is genuinely brilliant — the 30-second workspace scan is the highest-value addition to the basic council pattern. But it's described as optional ('Don't spend more than 30 seconds on this') when it should be mandatory. A council without context is just an academic exercise.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is a solid foundation for something much bigger. Right now it's a single-question council — ask, debate, synthesize, done. But the real value is in persistent councils that evolve over time.

Where's the council memory? If you council a product decision, then come back two weeks later with new information, you should be able to resume the previous council, update the advisors, and get an updated recommendation. The current skill treats every council as a one-shot event.

Where's the advisor specialization? The five thinking styles are good, but for technical decisions, you might want a 'The Security Engineer' or 'The Performance Specialist' instead of 'The Contrarian.' The skill should support custom advisor rosters for different domains.

Where's the confidence scoring? The chairman's verdict is presented as if all recommendations are equally confident. But sometimes the council is unanimous and high-confidence, and sometimes it's split and uncertain. The output should communicate this.

The skill should add a 'council dashboard' — a running record of past councils, their recommendations, and whether the user followed them. Over time, this creates a feedback loop that improves the council process itself.

The example section is the most engaging part of the skill and should be expanded. Show 3-4 real council transcripts across different domains (product, technical, hiring, strategy). This would teach the process far better than the abstract descriptions.

Finally, the skill should integrate with the other skills in the repo. A council about Django architecture should auto-load the @django skill context. A council about testing should load @tdd. The framing step should include skill discovery.

🟡 The Outsider

I picked this up expecting to understand how to run a council. Here's where I got lost:

First, the description says 'Use when the user asks for multiple independent perspectives on a decision with real stakes.' What counts as 'real stakes'? The skill gives examples ($97 vs $497 course, hiring decisions) but no threshold. If I'm choosing between two database designs, is that 'real stakes'? The boundary is unclear.

The advisor descriptions are thorough but overwhelming. Five advisors, each with a paragraph of description, a thinking style explanation, and a role in the 'natural tensions' framework. That's a lot of context to hold in your head before the actual process even starts. I wanted to skip ahead to 'how do I run this' but couldn't find a quick-start.

The prompt templates are full of meta-instructions that confused me. 'Respond from your perspective. Be direct and specific. Don't hedge or try to be balanced. Lean fully into your assigned angle.' If I'm an advisor agent, I'm already confused about my role. Adding 'don't hedge' to a list of six instructions doesn't help — it makes me more uncertain about what's expected.

The example at the end is the most useful part of the entire skill. I wish it was at the beginning. Show me a worked example first, then explain the theory. The current structure is: theory → process → templates → example. It should be: example → process → theory → templates.

I noticed the skill never explains what happens if I disagree with the council's recommendation. Can I ask for a re-council? Can I request a specific advisor's view in more detail? The skill presents the verdict as final, but real decisions are iterative.

The 'Trigger Ownership' section comparing council to court-jester is helpful but too long. A simple decision tree (multiple perspectives = council, focused attack = court-jester) would be clearer than a paragraph of explanation.

🟠 The Executor

Here's what's wrong and how to fix it, ranked by impact-to-effort ratio:

1. **No quick-start section.** An agent loading this skill faces 250 lines before it can act. The first thing should be a 10-line 'run a council in 60 seconds' guide: frame question → spawn 5 advisors → peer review → synthesize → present. Everything else is reference material.

2. **The framing step is underspecified.** 'Scan the workspace for context' is vague. Give me a checklist: (a) read AGENTS.md, (b) check for memory/ folder, (c) read any files the user referenced, (d) look for recent council transcripts. 30 seconds, four checks, done.

3. **Sub-agent prompts are too long.** The advisor prompt template is 8+ lines of instructions. Cut it to 3: who you are, what to do, how long to respond. The rest is noise that degrades output quality.

4. **No error handling.** What if a sub-agent fails? What if an advisor produces a 20-word response? The skill should specify retry logic and minimum viable response length.

5. **The example is buried.** Move it to the top, or better yet, provide a 'council in one screen' visual that shows the entire flow from question to verdict in a single view.

6. **The court-jester handoff adds complexity without clear value.** In practice, the Contrarian either uses a court-jester mode or doesn't. The elaborate rules (one mode only, no reference copying, don't homogenize) are process theater. Simplify to: 'The Contrarian may optionally load one court-jester mode for depth.'

The core process (frame → 5 advisors → peer review → chairman → verdict) is sound. Everything around it is overhead. Cut the skill to 100 lines: quick-start, process summary, advisor descriptions (one sentence each), chairman template, done. Move everything else to reference files.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor, for the ranked improvement list. The 'impact-to-effort ratio' framing is exactly how a skill should prioritize improvements. The quick-start section idea alone would transform the skill's usability.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist's persistent council idea. The skill treats councils as one-shot events, but real decisions evolve. A lightweight 'council state' file would enable continuity without major complexity.

What All Missed

No one addressed the skill's own testing. The repo mandates evals for every skill, but council has none. How do we know the council process actually produces better decisions than a single well-prompted agent?

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker, for questioning the number five. The skill is prescriptive about five advisors without justifying why. Parameterizing by decision importance is a genuinely novel insight that would make the skill more adaptable.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian's point about failure mode guidance. Sub-agents will fail, time out, and produce short responses. A skill that orchestrates sub-agents without error handling is incomplete.

What All Missed

No one questioned whether the peer review step adds value. If all advisors are the same model, peer review may just amplify shared biases rather than catch blind spots. The skill should address this limitation honestly.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider, for identifying the structure problem. The 'theory first, example last' ordering is a textbook mistake. Leading with a worked example would make the skill immediately usable and reduce the cognitive load of the advisor descriptions.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor's quick-start idea, while good, doesn't go far enough. A 10-line quick-start is useless without a worked example to reference. Quick-start + example should be a single combined section.

What All Missed

No one addressed the skill's relationship to the other skills in the repo. The council skill should be the orchestrator — it should know when to load @django, @drf, @tdd context based on the framed question. This cross-skill integration is completely absent.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Contrarian, for the 'important notes at the bottom' observation. The most actionable guidance (parallel spawning, anonymization, chairman can disagree) is buried after the example. This is a structural problem that's easy to fix and high-impact.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist's council dashboard idea. While interesting, it adds persistence complexity to a skill that's already struggling with basic usability. This should be a separate skill, not bolted onto the council.

What All Missed

No one addressed the word count constraints. Advisors are told 150-300 words, reviewers under 200 words. These are arbitrary. The skill should explain why these limits exist (scannability? token cost?) or remove them and let the chairman handle length.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker, for the 'two different use cases' insight. The skill tries to help users make decisions AND demonstrate multi-perspective analysis. Acknowledging this tension would help the chairman calibrate output — quick and decisive for use case (1), thorough and transparent for use case (2).

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider's point about 'what if I disagree with the verdict.' The skill presents the council as authoritative, but the user should be able to push back, request a specific advisor's full reasoning, or re-council with new information. This interaction model is missing.

What All Missed

No one proposed a 'council quality' metric. How do we know if a council session was good? Did the advisors produce genuinely different perspectives? Did the peer review catch real blind spots? A simple self-assessment (diversity of perspectives, blind spots caught, recommendation clarity) would help users evaluate council quality.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees on three things: (1) the skill needs a quick-start section at the top — no agent should read 250 lines before acting; (2) the framing step is the highest-value part and deserves more emphasis, not less; (3) the example should lead, not follow — show a worked council before explaining the theory. Four of five advisors flagged the lack of error handling for sub-agent failures as a gap.

Where the Council Clashes

The biggest clash is scope. The Expansionist wants persistent councils, advisor specialization, confidence scoring, and cross-skill integration — essentially a council platform. The Executor wants to cut the skill to 100 lines and move everything else to references. The First Principles Thinker wants to parameterize the number of advisors by decision importance, which no other advisor considered. The chairman sides with restraint: fix the foundation first (quick-start, error handling, example-led structure), then consider expansion in a v2.

Blind Spots Caught

Three collective blind spots emerged: (1) No one proposed evals for the council skill itself — in a repo that mandates testing, the council is untestable. (2) No one questioned whether same-model advisors with different prompts produce genuinely different reasoning, or just stylistic variation. (3) No one addressed the interaction model — what happens when the user disagrees with the verdict? Can they re-council? Request more detail from a specific advisor?

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill in three phases. Phase 1 (immediate): Add a 10-line quick-start at the top, move the example to the second position, add a framing checklist, and add sub-agent error handling. Phase 2 (short-term): Add one eval that tests whether the council produces better decisions than a single agent, add a 'when to re-council' section, and trim the advisor descriptions to one sentence each. Phase 3 (future): Consider advisor specialization, persistent councils, and cross-skill integration as a separate skill.

First Step

Add a quick-start section at the very top of SKILL.md (before the description, before the 'when to run' section) that fits in 10 lines: Frame question → Spawn 5 advisors in parallel → Anonymize as A-E → Peer review → Chairman synthesis → Present verdict. Include a one-line error handling note (retry failed agents, minimum 100-word responses). This single change addresses the top concern from 4 of 5 advisors and can be done in under 30 minutes.

4 / 26

court-jester

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/court-jester/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the court-jester skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a fatal identity crisis. It bills itself as the 'default adversarial review skill for planning work' but then defines five distinct reasoning modes, each with its own reference file, output structure, and selection logic. That's not a skill — it's a framework pretending to be a skill. The result is that an agent loading this skill gets 6349 bytes of routing logic and delegation instructions instead of actionable guidance. The actual reasoning lives in the references, but the SKILL.md spends most of its tokens on meta-process (mode selection, escalation rules, output expectations) that could be compressed to a third the size.

The 'Quick Start' section contradicts the 'Core Workflow' section. Quick Start says 'auto-select the strongest critique mode' and 'deliver a one-pass critique.' Core Workflow says 'do not block on interactive mode selection by default.' These are different instructions with different implications. An agent following Quick Start will behave differently from one following Core Workflow.

The description trigger is too broad: 'stress-testing plans, proposals, architecture, strategy, or other high-impact decisions.' This will fire on nearly any planning task, creating noise. The skill needs a narrower trigger — something like 'when the user explicitly asks for adversarial review or devil's-advocate analysis.'

The 'Critical Rules' section is generic process advice that applies to any reasoning task. Rules 1, 5, 6, and 7 are platitudes: 'steelman before critique,' 'drive toward synthesis,' 'say plainly when the plan is weak,' 'concede what survives scrutiny.' These don't teach the agent anything it doesn't already know from the base system prompt. They waste tokens.

There are zero examples. No sample input/output, no demonstration of what a good critique looks like versus a bad one. For a skill whose entire purpose is to change agent behavior, this is a critical omission. The agent has no concrete model to follow.

The 'Output Expectations' section mandates five structural elements for every output. This is rigid and will produce formulaic responses. Some critiques don't need a 'highest-risk issue' section — forcing it creates filler.

Finally, the skill claims ownership of 'adversarial reasoning for plans, proposals, strategies, architectures, and recommendations' but provides no boundary enforcement. What happens when court-jester conflicts with a domain skill? The pairing instruction is vague: 'Pair it with the relevant domain skill when needed.' When is needed? Who decides? This is a coordination gap.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the structure and ask: what problem does this skill actually solve? The problem is that LLMs are sycophantic. They agree with users, validate weak plans, and rarely push back. The skill's job is to make the agent reliably adversarial in a structured way.

From that foundation, the core requirement is simple: the agent must (1) understand what it's critiquing, (2) apply a specific lens of criticism, and (3) produce actionable output. Everything else is scaffolding.

The five-mode taxonomy is the skill's real contribution. It recognizes that 'be adversarial' is underspecified — there are different kinds of adversarial reasoning for different situations. Socratic questioning for vague assumptions, dialectic for tradeoff decisions, pre-mortem for rollout risk, red team for hostile actors, evidence audit for empirical claims. This is the right decomposition.

But the skill buries this strength under process overhead. The mode selection logic is too complex for what it does. The routing guide maps situation types to modes, but the mapping is obvious: if someone asks 'what could go wrong?' → pre-mortem. If someone asks 'is this claim true?' → evidence audit. The elaborate selection guide and the fallback to mode-selection-guide.md add indirection without value.

The reference files are where the actual knowledge lives, and they're decent. But the SKILL.md should be a thin router: here's the situation, here's the mode, here's the reference. Instead, it's a mini-process-document that duplicates what the references already cover.

What's fundamentally missing: a clear statement of what the agent should NOT do. Anti-patterns. The skill tells the agent to 'steelman before critique' but never shows what a strawman critique looks like. It says 'ground every challenge in specific reasoning' but never shows what vague 'what if' filler looks like. Without negative examples, the agent has to infer the boundary between good and bad adversarial reasoning.

The skill also lacks a clear stopping condition. Rule 2 says 'default to one-pass output' but Rule 5 says 'add a second mode only when it exposes a materially different risk.' Who judges 'materially different'? The agent needs a concrete heuristic, not a subjective standard.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a much bigger opportunity than it realizes. Right now it's a critique tool. It should be a decision-quality engine.

Consider: every time the agent runs a court-jester critique, it generates structured adversarial output. That output is valuable beyond the immediate conversation. It could feed into a decision log, a risk register, or a pre-mortem archive. The skill says nothing about persistence, follow-up, or tracking whether the identified risks actually materialized. There's a hidden opportunity to make the skill a feedback loop: critique → decision → outcome → calibration.

The five modes are good but incomplete. Missing modes:

1. **Temporal critique** — How does this decision look in 6 months? 2 years? What's the reversal cost? This is different from pre-mortem (what goes wrong) — it's about optionality and sunk cost.
2. **Stakeholder critique** — Who loses from this decision? Who's not in the room? This is especially important for architecture and product decisions where the user isn't the only affected party.
3. **Opportunity cost critique** — If we do this, what are we NOT doing? The skill focuses on risks of the chosen path but never asks about the road not taken.

The skill could also be a meta-critic. After the agent produces any plan or recommendation, court-jester could run automatically as a second pass. The current framing requires the user to explicitly invoke it. But the highest-value use case is when the user DOESN'T know they need a critique. The skill should define a protocol for auto-triggering: whenever the agent produces a multi-step plan, it should silently run a pre-mortem and attach it as an appendix.

The reference files could include templates — structured output formats that the agent fills in. This would improve consistency and make the output more scannable. Right now the output format is described in prose but never templated.

Finally, the skill has no concept of critique intensity. Sometimes the user wants a light sanity check; sometimes they want a full red team. Adding an intensity parameter (quick / standard / deep) would make the skill more flexible and token-efficient.

🟡 The Outsider

Reading this skill cold, I can't figure out what it actually wants me to DO.

The description says 'use when stress-testing plans, proposals, architecture, strategy.' Okay. But then the Quick Start says 'extract the user's actual thesis, proposal, or plan from context.' What if the user just says 'I'm thinking about switching from PostgreSQL to MongoDB'? Is that a plan? A proposal? Where's the boundary?

The skill uses jargon without defining it. 'Steelman,' 'dialectic synthesis,' 'falsificationism,' 'second-order thinking.' These are philosophy and critical thinking terms that not all users will know. The skill is written for an agent, but the agent serves a user who might want to understand what's happening. If the user asks 'what mode are you using?' the agent should be able to explain it in plain language.

The mode selection process is confusing. There's a table in the SKILL.md, then a reference file for mode selection, then each mode has its own reference file. That's three layers of indirection. An agent loading this skill has to read the SKILL.md, then decide whether to load the mode-selection guide or go directly to a mode reference. The decision tree in the Default Mode Selection section maps situations to modes, but the situations are vague: 'hidden assumptions or vague goals' — what counts as a vague goal?

The 'Critical Rules' section reads like a schoolteacher's rubric. 'Ground every challenge in specific reasoning, failure chains, incentives, or evidence standards.' This is advice about how to think, not instructions for what to do. An agent either can do this or can't — telling it to do so doesn't help.

There's no example of what a complete court-jester interaction looks like. I'd want to see: user input → mode selection → critique output → synthesis. A single end-to-end example would clarify everything.

The 'Content Ownership' section says this skill 'does not own domain-specific implementation guidance.' But then what DOES it own? The skill seems to own the process of critique but not the substance. That's a reasonable division, but it's never stated clearly enough for an agent to act on.

The license file is referenced but irrelevant to the agent's behavior. It's legal metadata that shouldn't be in the skill's operational surface.

🟠 The Executor

The fastest path to improvement: add one complete worked example and cut the Critical Rules section in half.

The single biggest gap is the absence of a concrete example. An agent reading this skill has no model of what good output looks like. Adding one end-to-end example — user says X, agent selects mode Y, produces critique Z with all five output elements — would do more for behavior change than any amount of process description. This is a one-hour fix with outsized impact.

The Critical Rules section has 8 rules. Rules 1, 5, 6, and 7 are generic reasoning advice that the base system prompt already covers. Rules 2, 3, 4, and 8 are specific to this skill. Cut the four generic rules and keep the four specific ones. This saves ~400 tokens per invocation, which matters because this skill is loaded on every planning task.

The Quick Start and Core Workflow sections overlap significantly. Merge them into a single 5-step process. Quick Start steps 1-5 and Core Workflow steps 1-5 cover the same ground with different wording. This is redundant and confusing. One clear workflow is better than two similar ones.

The mode selection logic should be simplified. Instead of routing through mode-selection-guide.md as a fallback, embed the decision table directly in the SKILL.md. The table already exists — just make it the primary selection mechanism and remove the indirection. The mode-selection-guide.md reference file should only be loaded when the agent genuinely can't decide, which is rare.

The Output Expectations section mandates five elements for every critique. This should be flexible: three required elements (steelmanned thesis, challenges, synthesis) and two optional (highest-risk issue, next steps). Not every critique needs a 'highest-risk issue' — sometimes the risks are evenly distributed.

None of these changes require new research or deep restructuring. They're editing passes that can be done in a single session.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

Response E (The Executor). It identifies the highest-leverage, lowest-effort improvements: add a worked example, merge redundant sections, simplify mode selection, make output expectations flexible. Every recommendation is concrete and immediately actionable. It doesn't theorize — it points at specific sections and says cut this, merge that, change this to that.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response C (The Expansionist). While the ideas about temporal critique, stakeholder critique, and opportunity cost are intellectually interesting, they expand the skill's scope without addressing its current weaknesses. The skill doesn't work well enough at its core to justify adding three new modes. 'Build a decision-quality engine' is a vision statement, not an improvement plan.

What All Missed

All five responses ignore the reference files entirely. The SKILL.md is only half the skill — the actual reasoning guidance lives in the six reference files. None of the reviewers evaluated whether those references are well-written, consistent with each other, or aligned with the SKILL.md's instructions. A critique of the skill that ignores 70% of its content is incomplete.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

Response A (The Contrarian). It finds real structural problems: the Quick Start vs. Core Workflow contradiction, the bloated Critical Rules, the absence of examples, the rigid output formula, and the vague domain-pairing instruction. The identity crisis argument — framework pretending to be a skill — is the most incisive observation in the entire debate.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response D (The Outsider). While the outsider perspective is valuable for catching jargon and confusion, it doesn't offer enough substantive critique. 'I can't figure out what it wants me to do' is a valid reaction but not a deep analysis. The outsider should have caught the missing anti-patterns and the lack of negative examples, which are more fundamental than the jargon issue.

What All Missed

None of the responses evaluate the skill's triggering mechanism against real usage patterns. The description field is the skill's API — it determines when the agent loads this skill. No one analyzed whether the current description would cause over-triggering or under-triggering in practice, or how it interacts with similar skills like llm-council.

reviewer 3
Strongest

Response B (The First Principles Thinker). It correctly identifies the core problem (LLM sycophancy) and evaluates the skill against that standard. The observation that the five-mode taxonomy is the skill's real contribution — and that everything else is scaffolding — is exactly right. The critique of the mode selection complexity and the missing anti-patterns are both precise and actionable.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response E (The Executor). While practical, it's too conservative. 'Cut the generic rules and merge the workflows' is fine but doesn't address the deeper structural issues. The Executor's approach would produce a cleaner version of the same skill, not a better skill. Sometimes the fastest path isn't the most impactful one.

What All Missed

All responses treat the skill as a standalone artifact. No one asked: how does this skill interact with llm-council, which is listed as a separate skill in the same repo? The descriptions overlap — both mention plans, proposals, architecture, strategy. An agent might load both, or the wrong one. The skill ecosystem needs boundary definitions.

Finding that kind of internal inconsistency is exactly what a contrarian should do. The criticism of the rigid output formula — 'some critiques don't need a highest-risk issue' — is also spot-on and something the other reviewers missed.

The Expansionist optimizes for theoretical value without considering user experience.

What All Missed

None of the reviewers considered the skill's token budget. This skill loads six reference files, each 5-8KB. In a constrained context window, loading court-jester plus a domain skill plus the user's actual content could push past limits. The skill should have a token budget strategy — which references are essential vs. optional, and how to handle context pressure.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council agrees on three things. First, the absence of a worked example is the single biggest gap — every reviewer mentioned it directly or indirectly. Second, the Critical Rules section is bloated with generic advice that should be cut to the four skill-specific rules. Third, the Quick Start and Core Workflow sections are redundant and should be merged into one clear process. These are the highest-priority fixes.

The council also agrees that the mode selection logic has too much indirection, though reviewers disagree on how much to simplify. And everyone acknowledges that the five-mode taxonomy is the skill's core strength — the improvements should preserve and clarify it, not replace it.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on scope. The Expansionist wants to add three new modes (temporal, stakeholder, opportunity cost) and auto-triggering behavior. The Contrarian and Executor want to shrink and sharpen what exists. The Chairman sides with shrink-and-sharpen: the skill isn't reliable enough at its current scope to justify expansion. Add modes only after the core five work consistently.

The council also clashes on output rigidity. The Executor wants flexible output (3 required + 2 optional elements). The current spec mandates all five. The Chairman sides with flexibility: require thesis, challenges, and synthesis; make highest-risk issue and next steps optional.

On the question of whether the skill's description triggers too broadly, the Contrarian and Outsider say yes, while the Expansionist implicitly wants even broader triggering (auto-trigger). The Chairman agrees with narrowing: the description should emphasize explicit user request for adversarial review, not passive planning tasks.

Blind Spots Caught

Three blind spots caught by peer reviewers: (1) No one evaluated the reference files, which constitute 70% of the skill's content. The SKILL.md can be perfect while the references are broken. (2) No one addressed the absence of evals. This skill has no test cases, no baseline measurements, no way to validate that it improves critique quality. (3) No one analyzed the skill's interaction with llm-council, which has overlapping descriptions and could cause confusion in the skill ecosystem.

The Recommendation

Execute the Executor's plan as the first pass: add one complete worked example, cut generic rules, merge Quick Start and Core Workflow, simplify mode selection, and make output expectations flexible. This is a single-session editing pass that addresses the highest-agreement items.

Then, as a second pass: (a) add negative examples (anti-patterns) showing what bad adversarial reasoning looks like, (b) add evals — at least 3 test cases with expected outputs, (c) review all six reference files for consistency with the revised SKILL.md, and (d) clarify the boundary with llm-council in the description field.

Defer the Expansionist's ideas (new modes, auto-triggering, decision logging) to a future version. The skill needs to walk before it runs.

First Step

Add one complete worked example to the SKILL.md. Show a full end-to-end interaction: user input ('I want to migrate our monolith to microservices'), mode selection (pre-mortem, because it's a migration with rollout risk), critique output (with all five elements), and synthesis. Place it after the Quick Start section. This single addition will do more to improve agent behavior than any other change.
