5 / 26

diagnosing-bugs

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/diagnosing-bugs/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the diagnosing-bugs skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a paper-thin structure that will collapse under real pressure. The core thesis — 'build a feedback loop first' — is correct but the skill treats it as sufficient, when it's actually just the opening move. Here's what will go wrong in practice:

**Phase 1 is where agents will cheat.** The completion criterion says 'you have already run at least once (paste the invocation and its output).' This is the right idea, but there's no enforcement mechanism. An agent that wants to skip ahead will fabricate output or claim it 'already ran' something. The skill needs an explicit injunction: if you cannot paste real terminal output, you are not done with Phase 1. Period.

**The non-deterministic bug section is naive.** 'Loop 100×, raise the rate to 50%' — this is cargo-cult advice. For race conditions, looping the same code path in the same process won't help. You need different strategies: CPU pinning, memory barrier injection, scheduler perturbation. The skill mentions 'inject sleeps' but presents it as a timing tweak rather than a racetam tool. This section needs a concrete race-condition playbook or it's theater.

**Phase 3's hypothesis protocol is flabby.** 'State it plainly, design an experiment, run it, record the result' — this is a generic scientific method. It doesn't help an agent choose BETWEEN hypotheses when three explanations fit the data. There's no guidance on ranking hypotheses by prior probability, by ease of falsification, or by blast radius. Without that, the agent will test hypotheses in whatever order they occur to it, which is random.

**The evals are laughably soft.** Eval 3 says the response should 'propose a bisection strategy' — but accepting 'git bisect' as an answer is barely above baseline. The evals don't test whether the agent can actually construct the script. They test whether the agent can name the concept. That's a vocabulary test, not a skill test.

**The reference file is a table of contents, not a reference.** The feedback-loops.md file is just a reformatted version of the Phase 1 content with cute examples. Nobody will learn anything from it they didn't already get from the SKILL.md. Either make it a genuine deep-dive (real case studies, actual scripts, war stories) or delete it and save the tokens.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the structure and ask: what problem does this skill actually exist to solve? It exists because LLMs are pattern-matching machines that hallucinate root causes. Given a bug report, an unassisted LLM will pick the most statistically plausible explanation, confidently assert it, and propose a fix — without ever verifying. The skill's real job is to install a circuit breaker between 'I have a theory' and 'I apply a fix.'

Starting from that truth, the skill is about 70% right. The feedback-loop-first principle is the correct circuit breaker. But the skill is organized as a linear phase machine (Phase 1 → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5) when the real process is iterative and recursive. In practice, you form a preliminary hypothesis in Phase 1 to decide WHICH loop to build. You loop back to Phase 1 when Phase 3's experiment is inconclusive (the skill admits this in one sentence but buries it). You might skip Phase 4 entirely if Phase 3's experiment is definitive.

The skill should be reframed around the CORE tension: loop quality vs. hypothesis quality. The phases are supporting actors. Restructure as:
1. The invariant: never assert a cause without a loop that can disprove it.
2. Loop construction (the 10 strategies, the tightening protocol).
3. Hypothesis competition (how to rank, test, and decide between competing theories).
4. The fix discipline (regression test before declare victory).

Also, the skill never asks 'is this a bug or expected behavior?' — the very first question in any real debugging session. If the spec says the system should crash on expired cards and it crashes on expired cards, there's no bug. A one-sentence pre-phase checklist ('Does the observed behavior deviate from the spec?') would prevent wasted cycles.

Finally, the skill has no concept of scope escalation. When do you stop debugging and escalate? When do you conclude 'this is a known library bug, upgrade the dependency'? When do you decide 'this requires a core dump analysis I can't do in this environment'? These stop-rules prevent death spirals.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a much bigger opportunity than it realizes. Right now it's a debugging protocol. It could be an entire diagnostic culture. Here's what's hiding inside it:

**Bug taxonomy integration.** The skill treats all bugs as equal, but different bug categories demand fundamentally different approaches. A memory leak needs heap snapshots and growth analysis. A logic error needs input/output diffing. A concurrency bug needs happens-before reasoning. A configuration bug needs environment diffing. Instead of one flat Phase 1 loop-construction list, the skill should route: 'If the symptom is X, start with strategy Y.' This turns the skill from a pamphlet into a diagnostic tree.

**Cross-linking to other skills.** The skill mentions cURL — the webapp-testing or drf skill probably has opinions on this. It mentions Playwright — the webapp-testing skill owns that. The skill should say 'load webapp-testing when the feedback loop involves a browser' rather than vaguely referencing Playwright. Same for profiling: python-async or django skills will have framework-specific profiling advice. Make these explicit handoff points.

**The evals are a goldmine waiting to happen.** Three evals for a discipline skill is the bare minimum. The evals should include: a performance regression scenario (the clue is 'it got slower after the deploy'), a heisenbug that disappears under observation, a bug that turns out to be a dependency issue, and a scenario where the agent's first hypothesis is wrong and it needs to recover. These would turn the evals into genuine training wheels.

**Bug journal template.** The skill is silent on recording what was learned. Every hard bug teaches something the project didn't know. A lightweight 'bug journal' template — symptom, false leads, actual cause, fix, lesson — would turn each debugging session into institutional knowledge. The skill should prompt: after Phase 5, consider writing a one-paragraph entry.

**Integration with version control.** The skill mentions `git bisect` in passing. It should have a concrete subsection: 'When the regression is between commits, here's the exact bisect protocol.' This is one of the most powerful debugging techniques in existence and it gets one line in a reference table.

🟡 The Outsider

I just read this skill with zero context about pi, this project, or its conventions. Here's what confused me:

**What does 'tight' mean?** The word appears constantly — 'tighten the loop,' 'a tight loop,' 'tight and red-capable.' I can infer it means fast+deterministic+sharp, but the skill never defines it in one place. Each mention adds a slightly different nuance. A newcomer will think 'tight' is the goal but won't be able to tell when they've achieved it. Give me a single definition: 'A tight loop runs in under N seconds, produces the same result every time, and asserts on the specific failure — not just completion.'

**What's the difference between Phase 2 'minimise' and Phase 1 'tighten the loop'?** Both involve making the repro smaller. Phase 1 says 'narrow the test scope.' Phase 2 says 'cut inputs, callers, config, data, and steps one at a time.' These sound like the same activity described at different granularity. I need a clear boundary: Phase 1 tightening is about the LOOP (the runner, the harness, the environment), Phase 2 minimising is about the PROBLEM (the inputs, the scenario, the code path). If this distinction matters, say it explicitly. If it doesn't, merge them.

**Phase 4 is marked optional but never says when to skip it.** 'If the hypothesis is about internal state' — what if it's NOT about internal state? Can I always skip Phase 4? The example says 'add instrumentation to make the invisible visible' but then says 'if the bug is performance-related use a profiler.' That's not Phase 4 being optional, that's Phase 4 being mislabeled. Performance bugs absolutely require instrumentation — making Phase 4 'optional' for them is wrong. Either rename it 'Internal State Instrumentation (if needed)' or make it mandatory for all bugs.

**The evals use vague expected_outputs like 'The response follows the diagnosis discipline.'** How would I measure that? The expectations are better (specific bullet items), but the expected_output field would confuse anyone trying to grade these. Make every expected_output measurable: 'Response contains a concrete command (test invocation, curl, or script path) that would fail on this specific bug.'

**The hitl-loop.template.sh contains hard-coded localhost:8000 URLs and 'Submit' button references.** These are examples, yes, but they're not marked as examples. A copy-paste agent will run this against the wrong project. These should be TEMPLATE_VARIABLES like {{BASE_URL}} and {{TRIGGER_ACTION}}.

🟠 The Executor

The single highest-impact improvement: rewrite the Phase 1 completion criterion as an executable checklist that the agent must literally check off before proceeding. Right now it's four bullet points — red-capable, deterministic, fast, agent-runnable — followed by an italic warning. What's actually needed is a hard gate:

```
PHASE 1 GATE — Do not continue until ALL are true:
1. You have constructed a command/script.
2. You have RUN IT and pasted the actual output below this line.
3. The output demonstrates the user's EXACT symptom.
4. You can run it again and get the same result.
5. It completes in under 30 seconds.

If any item above lacks evidence, STOP. Return to loop construction.
```

This is mechanical, enforceable, and impossible to fake without actual terminal output. Everything else is polish.

The second concrete step: add one eval per Phase. Currently there are three evals covering Phases 1, 1 (non-deterministic), and 3 (bisection). Zero coverage for Phase 2 (minimisation), Phase 4 (instrumentation), or Phase 5 (fix discipline and regression test). A failure mode I've seen in debugging skills is agents who reproduce but never minimise — they fix a bug in a 500-line scenario and the fix is equally bloated. Add an eval where the repro is over-complex and the agent must justify each removal step.

The third concrete step: kill the 'optional' label on Phase 4 and rename it. Call it 'Make the Invisible Visible' and make it mandatory with a short-circuit: if your hypothesis is already confirmed by Phase 3's experiment with direct evidence (not inferred), state 'Phase 4 skipped: [evidence].' This forces the explicit judgment call rather than letting agents skip the hard work by default.

These three changes — hard gate, Phase 2/4/5 evals, mandatory Phase 4 with skip justification — would take an afternoon to implement and would transform this from advisory text into an actual enforcement mechanism.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor (E). The Phase 1 Gate proposal is the single most actionable improvement in the entire debate. It converts an advisory checklist into a mechanical enforcement mechanism. The 'paste actual output below this line' requirement is elegant — it makes Phase 1 skipping physically obvious rather than rhetorically discouraged. This alone would prevent the most common failure mode: agents theorizing without a loop.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist (C). The bug taxonomy routing idea is intellectually appealing but operationally premature. A routing tree adds significant complexity and the skill currently covers 90% of bugs with its existing flat structure. Adding categories like 'memory leak → heap snapshots' is better handled by loading a domain-specific skill when the symptom matches. The skill doesn't need to become a diagnostic tree — it needs to become better at saying 'load X skill for X-class bugs.'

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed the skill's onboarding gap: there's no 'quick start' path. A user reading this for the first time sees 5 phases, a reference file, a script template, and 3 evals. There's no 'if you only read one page, read this' summary. Even a 3-sentence overview at the top — 'Build a loop that goes red. Confirm it catches the bug. Hypothesize, experiment, fix, regression-test.' — would dramatically improve first-use clarity. The Contrarian comes closest with the reference-file critique but frames it as 'delete or deepen' rather than 'add a ramp.'

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker (B). The observation that the skill is structured as a linear phase machine when debugging is recursive gets at the deepest flaw in the current design. The admission that 'you form a preliminary hypothesis in Phase 1 to decide which loop to build' is genuinely revelatory — it means the current phase ordering is already wrong in practice, and the skill should acknowledge this explicitly rather than pretending phases are strictly sequential. The 'is this actually a bug?' pre-check is an obvious-in-hindsight addition that would prevent real wasted cycles.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor (E). The hard gate is excellent for Phase 1 but the reviewer's other two suggestions are weaker. Adding 'one eval per Phase' is a quantity-over-quality move — three well-designed evals that test cross-phase reasoning would be more valuable than five evals that each test one phase in isolation. And renaming Phase 4 to 'Make the Invisible Visible' is cosmetic; the real problem is that Phase 4's scope is unclear, not that its name is boring.

What All Missed

Every advisor focused on the debugging process itself, but nobody asked: what does the agent do when the fix is WRONG? There's no 'Phase 5 failed' path. The fix doesn't make the loop go green, or it makes the loop go green but breaks something else. The skill should have an explicit recovery path: 'If the fix doesn't work, return to Phase 3 with the new data. If the fix works but breaks other tests, the hypothesis was incomplete — revisit Phase 2 minimisation.' This is the most common real debugging scenario and the skill is silent on it.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider (D). The observation that 'tight' is never formally defined despite being the skill's most repeated term is the kind of catch that only a fresh reader makes. The Phase 1 vs Phase 2 boundary confusion (tighten vs minimise) is a real structural problem that every insider advisor missed because they've already internalized the distinction. The hard-coded localhost:8000 in the HITL template is a genuine bug that will cause real copy-paste failures. These are the highest-signal observations per word spent.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian (A). The race-condition critique is overstated. 'Looping the same code path in the same process won't help' is true for some race conditions but not all. Many timing bugs in web apps ARE reproducible by hammering an endpoint 100× in parallel, which is exactly what the skill advises. The call for a 'racetam tool' and 'memory barrier injection' is absurd overkill for a general-purpose debugging skill — those are kernel-debugging techniques. The Contrarian correctly identifies that the non-deterministic section is thin but prescribes a solution that's 10x too heavy.

What All Missed

Nobody addressed the skill's relationship to the skill-creator skill. This skill has evals, which means it's meant to be testable and improvable. But there's no guidance on WHEN to re-evaluate: after a debugging session that went poorly? After a fix that turned out to be wrong? The skill should include a 'retrospective trigger': 'If the fix required more than 3 hypothesis cycles, run the evals again to check whether the skill's guidance was adequate.' This closes the improvement loop.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Executor (E) again, but specifically the 'mandatory Phase 4 with skip justification' idea. This is a pattern that should be applied to every optional element in the skill. The current design has too many escape hatches: Phase 4 is optional, Phase 3 says 'if inconclusive, tighten the loop' (which is vague permission to loop forever), and Phase 1's 'when you genuinely cannot build a loop' is a blank check. Making optional elements mandatory-with-justification converts the skill from a suggestion into a discipline. The Executor's approach is the most enforceable pattern in the debate.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist (C). The bug journal idea is nice-to-have, not need-to-have. Institutional knowledge capture is a project-level concern, not a debugging-skill concern. Adding it here dilutes the skill's focus. The cross-linking suggestion is more valuable but the execution would be fragile — skill loading is context-dependent and hard-coding 'load webapp-testing when browser involved' could cause circular loading or context bloat. A softer 'consider loading X' would be safer but then it's just a comment, not a structural improvement.

What All Missed

The skill has no guidance on debugging in production vs. development environments. All the loop-construction strategies assume you have a dev server, a test suite, and a local filesystem. But many of the hardest bugs are production-only: they depend on real data, real traffic, real infrastructure. The skill should have at least a subsection on production debugging: feature flags, canary deployments, log-based reproduction, read-replica testing. Without this, the skill is incomplete for any team that doesn't have perfect staging parity.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker (B). The 'stop-rules' concept is the most important missing piece in the entire skill. Every experienced debugger knows that the hardest part isn't finding the bug — it's knowing when to stop looking and escalate, upgrade, or work around. The skill's implicit assumption is that persistence always pays off, which is false. A 2-hour debugging session that ends with 'upgrade the dependency' is a better outcome than a 2-day session that finds the root cause. Adding explicit stop-rules (time budget, escalation triggers, known-issue lookup) would make the skill dramatically more realistic.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider (D). The request for a formal definition of 'tight' is reasonable but the proposed fix — 'runs in under N seconds' — is too rigid. Some legitimate feedback loops take 2 minutes (full integration test suites, browser-based E2E). The right definition is relative: 'as fast as possible while still exercising the bug.' The Outsider is right that the term is overused and underdefined, but the solution should be a qualitative rubric, not a hard threshold. Also, the HITL template critique is valid but minor — any agent using that template will obviously need to edit the URLs.

What All Missed

Nobody discussed the skill's tone and how it affects agent compliance. The skill uses a lot of capital-B Bold text, italic warnings, and imperative language. This works for human readers but for agents, the signal-to-noise ratio is low — everything is emphasized, so nothing stands out. The skill would benefit from a clear visual hierarchy: MUST (hard requirements), SHOULD (strong recommendations), MAY (optional). The current format treats Phase 1 completion criteria and 'remove temporary instrumentation' with the same emphasis. An agent that treats everything as equally mandatory will be paralyzed; an agent that treats everything as advisory will skip freely.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees on three things: (1) Phase 1's completion criterion needs to be a hard, enforceable gate with evidence requirements — not advisory bullets. (2) The evals are insufficient — they cover only 2 of 5 phases and test concept-naming rather than skill execution. (3) The skill has too many escape hatches (Phase 4 optional, 'genuinely cannot build a loop' as a blank check, Phase 3's vague iteration permission) that agents will abuse to skip hard work. Every advisor identified at least one of these, and the peer reviews confirmed them as the highest-signal findings.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether the skill should expand in scope or contract. The Expansionist wants bug taxonomies, cross-skill linking, bug journals, and git bisect deep-dives — turning the skill into a debugging encyclopedia. The Executor wants ruthless focus: fix the gate, add evals, make optional elements justified. The First Principles Thinker wants structural reframing around the loop-vs-hypothesis tension. The Outsider wants clarity and onboarding improvements. These aren't all compatible — you can't simultaneously simplify the structure and add a routing tree. The chairman sides with focus: fix the core enforcement mechanism first, then expand only if the core is solid.

Blind Spots Caught

Three blind spots caught across reviews: (1) No recovery path when the fix fails or breaks other tests — the skill assumes Phase 5 always succeeds. (2) No production debugging guidance — all strategies assume a local dev environment. (3) No stop-rules or escalation criteria — the skill implicitly assumes infinite persistence is always correct. These are not edge cases; they are common real-world scenarios that the skill currently abandons the user in.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill around three hard gates (Phase 1: loop built and run with pasted output; Phase 3: hypothesis confirmed by experiment with evidence; Phase 5: regression test exists and suite passes). Make Phase 4 mandatory with skip justification. Add explicit recovery paths: if fix fails, return to Phase 3; if fix breaks suite, hypothesis was incomplete. Add stop-rules: time budget, escalation triggers, known-issue lookup. Expand evals to cover all 5 phases with execution-level expectations. Define 'tight' once, clearly, at the top. Add a one-paragraph quick-start summary. Defer bug taxonomies, cross-linking, and bug journals to a future revision — they're valuable but premature before the core enforcement works.

First Step

Rewrite Phase 1's completion criterion as an executable gate with five concrete checkpoints, the last being 'paste the actual terminal output of running the loop below.' This is the Executor's Phase 1 Gate proposal, endorsed by Reviewer 1 as the single highest-impact change. It takes 30 minutes to write, it's immediately testable, and it eliminates the most common failure mode (agents theorizing without a loop). Everything else — evals, recovery paths, stop-rules — depends on this gate working correctly.

6 / 26

django-allauth

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/django-allauth/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the django-allauth skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a serious structural problem: it's a routing table pretending to be a skill. The SKILL.md itself is 90% indirection — it tells the agent to 'read the reference file' for everything. That means the skill adds a layer of overhead without adding judgment. An agent could achieve the same result by just grepping the references directory.

The Critical Rules section is the only part that provides actual decision-making value, and even there, rule #4 ('Prefer the existing extension point') buries the most important allauth insight — the settings → adapter → form → signal → template → view hierarchy — in a single dense paragraph. This ordering is the single most common source of allauth bugs, and it deserves to be prominent, not a footnote.

The 'When Not To Use This Skill' section is too timid. It says 'pair django' for core Django behavior, but doesn't say what 'pair' means operationally. Does the agent load both skills? Read both? The skill gives no guidance on how to resolve conflicts between django and django-allauth advice.

The Task Routing table is useful but incomplete — there's no routing for common compound tasks like 'add Google login to an existing headless API project' which requires socialaccount + headless + provider references simultaneously. Real tasks are multi-surface; the routing assumes they're not.

Finally, the description triggers on 'django-allauth setup' but the skill has no opinion on what a correct setup looks like — it just points to another file. The skill should encode the minimum viable allauth configuration as a concrete checklist, not a reference link.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Let's strip this down. What is an agent actually trying to do when it loads this skill? It has a task involving allauth and needs to know: (1) which allauth subsystem is involved, (2) what the correct extension point is, (3) what the common pitfalls are. The current skill does #1 well via the routing table, does #2 weakly via Critical Rule #4, and does #3 not at all — it delegates #3 to reference files the agent may not read.

The fundamental question: should a skill be a table of contents or a decision engine? Right now it's a TOC. A decision engine would encode the actual decision logic — e.g., 'If the user wants to customize signup, first check ACCOUNT_ADAPTER, then ACCOUNT_FORMS, then ACCOUNT_SIGNUP_FORM_CLASS, then signals, then templates. Do NOT override the view unless all five prior options are exhausted.'

The reference architecture is actually good — 13 focused files is better than one monolithic doc. But the SKILL.md should be the brain, not the index. It should contain the heuristics that let an agent make correct decisions without reading all 13 files.

What's actually missing from a first-principles perspective: the adapter pattern. Allauth's entire customization architecture is adapter-based, and the skill never names this explicitly. An agent that doesn't understand the adapter pattern will constantly reach for view overrides. The skill should teach the pattern, not just mention it in rule #4.

Also missing: the relationship between allauth settings and Django settings. Allauth doesn't exist in a vacuum — ACCOUNT_AUTHENTICATION_METHOD interacts with AUTH_USER_MODEL, email backend settings affect verification flows, etc. The skill should explicitly map these interaction surfaces.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is playing small. Django-allauth is one of the most complex Django packages in existence — it touches auth, sessions, email, OAuth, OIDC, SAML, MFA, social login, and headless APIs. The current skill treats it like a simple library reference. It should be the authoritative integration guide.

Here's what's hiding in plain sight: the skill could include concrete code patterns for the 5 most common allauth integration scenarios. Not references — actual copy-paste-ready patterns for: (1) standard email+password signup with verification, (2) adding Google OAuth to an existing project, (3) headless allauth for a React SPA, (4) MFA with TOTP, (5) social account linking. These are the patterns that 80% of users need, and right now the skill makes them read multiple reference files to assemble each one.

The skill should also cover the allauth + Django ecosystem intersections that cause real pain: allauth with django-allauth's headless mode alongside DRF (they overlap and conflict), allauth with custom user models (the gotchas are numerous), allauth with Celery for async email, allauth in Docker/test environments (the SITE_ID problem is universal).

There's an opportunity to add an anti-patterns section that names the 3-5 most common allauth mistakes: overriding views instead of adapters, not setting DEFAULT_FROM_EMAIL, forgetting SITE_ID in multi-db setups, confusing headless token auth with session auth. These would save more agent turns than any reference file.

The version-notes reference is good but reactive. The skill should proactively flag which allauth features are stable vs. rapidly evolving, so the agent knows when to double-check upstream docs.

🟡 The Outsider

I have no prior allauth context. I just loaded this skill. Here's what I'm confused about:

The description says 'Use when tasks involve django-allauth setup' — but the skill itself doesn't help me set anything up. It sends me to `references/installation-and-wiring.md`. So what did this skill actually give me? A table of contents.

The Critical Rules are the first thing that's actually useful, but they're written as prohibitions ('Do not assume', 'Do not confuse', 'Do not guess') rather than positive guidance. As a new reader, I want to know what TO do, not just what NOT to do. Rule #4 is the most valuable — the extension point hierarchy — but it's written as a negative ('Before suggesting view overrides...') when it should be the lead.

The Task Routing table uses allauth-internal terminology without definitions. What's the difference between 'account' and 'socialaccount'? What does 'headless' mean in this context? What's 'idp mode'? I shouldn't have to read a reference file to understand the routing table that's supposed to help me pick a reference file. Each row needs a one-sentence plain-language description.

The scope says 'django-allauth' but the skill doesn't tell me what version of allauth it covers. The frontmatter says 'latest docs/source snapshot verified on 2026-04-07' — that's 2+ months old. Is that still current? The skill should tell me at the top whether it's confident about the current state of the project.

Also: the reference files total 56KB of markdown. The SKILL.md gives me no guidance on which references are most commonly needed vs. niche. I'm likely to either read too many or miss the right one.

🟠 The Executor

The fastest path to improvement: add a 'Quick Patterns' section with 3-5 copy-paste code blocks for the most common allauth tasks. Right now every task requires the agent to read 2-3 reference files and synthesize an answer. That's 3-5 tool calls per task. Concrete patterns would cut that to 1.

Priority order for patterns: (1) minimal allauth setup with email verification — this is what every project starts with, (2) adding a social provider (Google as the canonical example), (3) headless mode configuration for SPA backends. These three cover probably 70% of real-world usage.

Second fix: restructure the Critical Rules into a numbered decision procedure. Instead of 7 prohibitive rules, give me a flowchart: 'Step 1: Identify the allauth app(s) installed. Step 2: Identify the surface (account/socialaccount/mfa/headless/idp). Step 3: Check the extension point hierarchy. Step 4: Verify provider-specific requirements.' This is actionable; the current rules are not.

Third fix: the Task Routing table needs a 'compound task' row. Most real tasks span multiple surfaces. 'Adding Google login to a headless project' requires socialaccount-core + providers-major + headless. Without this, the agent will read one reference, give an incomplete answer, and the user will have to ask a follow-up.

These three changes — patterns, decision procedure, compound routing — would be the highest-impact improvements and could be done in under an hour. Everything else is refinement.

The insight about the adapter pattern being allauth's central architectural concept, and the skill never naming it explicitly, is the most important observation in the entire debate. This is the kind of structural critique that leads to fundamental improvement.

This means it may fire for tasks where it adds no value (e.g., a pure Django auth question that happens to use allauth's user model) and may not fire for tasks where it's essential (e.g., an OAuth flow question that doesn't mention allauth by name). The trigger scope needs calibration.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees the SKILL.md is overly thin — it functions as a table of contents rather than a decision-making layer. All five advisors identified this core weakness from different angles. There's also agreement that the Critical Rules section is the most valuable part of the current skill but is structured as prohibitions rather than positive guidance. The Task Routing table needs improvement — either with plain-language descriptions, compound-task rows, or both. Finally, the 'When Not To Use' section's 'pair' language is ambiguous and needs operational definition.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether to add concrete code patterns to the SKILL.md. The Expansionist and Executor see patterns as the highest-value addition; the First Principles Thinker and Reviewers 2/4 argue this creates maintenance burden and that patterns belong in version-annotated reference files. The Contrarian wants the skill to encode a minimum viable configuration checklist; others argue this duplicates the installation-and-wiring reference. On the Outsider's complaint about the verification date, Reviewer 5 pushes back that 2 months is fine for a structural guide.

Blind Spots Caught

Three major blind spots caught by peer reviewers: (1) No advisor addressed testing guidance — how to test allauth integrations is completely absent from the SKILL.md's routing. (2) No advisor evaluated the quality of the reference files themselves — the skill is only as good as its references. (3) No advisor addressed inter-skill consistency — how this skill interacts with django, drf, and other skills in the ecosystem. Reviewer 3 also noted the triggering accuracy question is completely unaddressed.

The Recommendation

Restructure the SKILL.md in three phases. Phase 1 (highest impact): Rewrite Critical Rules as a positive decision procedure — a numbered flowchart that walks through subsystem identification, extension point hierarchy, and provider verification. Add plain-language descriptions to the Task Routing table. Define 'pair' operationally in 'When Not To Use.' Phase 2: Add a compound-task routing row for multi-surface tasks. Add explicit testing task routing. Phase 3: Add a 'Common Anti-Patterns' section that names the top 5 allauth mistakes with one-sentence explanations and links to the relevant reference files. Do NOT add hardcoded code patterns to the SKILL.md — keep patterns in reference files with version annotations.

First Step

Rewrite the Critical Rules section from 7 prohibitive rules into a 5-step positive decision procedure: (1) Verify installed allauth apps, (2) Identify the allauth surface, (3) Apply the extension point hierarchy (settings → adapter → form → signal → template → view), (4) Check provider-specific requirements if applicable, (5) Verify cross-surface interactions (Django settings, other installed apps). This single change addresses the core critique from 4 of 5 advisors and can be done without touching any reference files.

django

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/django/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the Django 6.0 skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill is dangerously overconfident about Django 6.0. The target_versions field claims 'Django 6.0, Python 3.12+' but Django 6.0 doesn't exist yet — the latest stable is 5.x. This means the entire skill is either speculative, hallucinated, or written against a pre-release that may change before GA. The 'last_verified: 2026-03-19' date is in the future, which is a red flag for staleness or fabrication. The reference map lists pattern counts (e.g., '25', '24') with no explanation of what those numbers mean — they're meaningless metrics that will rot immediately. The skill also has a redundant REFERENCE.md that duplicates the SKILL.md's own routing table, creating a maintenance burden and a divergence risk. The 'Critical Rules' section mixes genuine gotchas (N+1, auto_now bypass) with opinionated conventions (inline HTML limits, HTMX preference) without distinguishing which are framework requirements vs. project-specific choices. The HTMX/tabler bias baked into 'Backend Partials and HTMX Mindset' is inappropriate for a general Django skill — many Django projects use React, Vue, or plain server-rendered HTML without HTMX. This skill needs to decide if it's a general Django reference or a specific stack guide.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

What problem does this skill actually solve? It claims to be a 'framework reference' but it's really a routing document that points to 13 other files. The SKILL.md itself contains almost no actionable content — it's a table of contents with some rules bolted on. The actual knowledge lives in references/, but the skill provides no guidance on how deep those references go or whether they're authoritative. The 'Critical Rules' section is the only directly useful content, but it's a grab bag without prioritization. Rule 1 (model field verification) is a process rule, Rule 2 (select_related) is a performance rule, Rule 5 (TemplateResponse) is an API choice — they're different categories jumbled together. The skill should be restructured around the actual developer workflow: 'I need to do X' → 'read file Y' → 'watch out for Z'. The current structure forces the agent to read SKILL.md, then read REFERENCE.md (which repeats SKILL.md), then read the actual reference file. That's two unnecessary hops. Collapse REFERENCE.md into SKILL.md, eliminate the routing indirection, and organize the rules by workflow phase (modeling → views → templates → testing) rather than by arbitrary numbering. Also, the 'Django 6.0' framing is premature — either commit to a real version or make the skill version-agnostic with version-specific addenda.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is leaving enormous value on the table. It covers 13 domains but misses critical real-world Django concerns that trip up every production project. There's no reference file for **database migrations** as a standalone topic — migration conflicts, squashing, data migrations, and rollback strategies are all absent. There's no **logging and observability** guidance — Django's logging config is non-trivial and every project needs it. There's no **caching** reference — per-view caching, template fragment caching, low-level cache API, and cache invalidation patterns are core Django. There's no **management commands** reference — writing custom commands, handling arguments, and running them in production is a daily need. There's no **static files and media** deep dive — S3 backends, CDN integration, and the collectstatic pipeline are production necessities. There's no **internationalization (i18n)** reference — translation strings, locale middleware, and timezone handling. There's no **email** reference — sending email, templates, and backend configuration. The skill also has no **troubleshooting guide** — a 'common errors and their fixes' reference would be the most-used file in the entire skill. Finally, the skill should include a **Django + Docker** reference for containerized development and deployment, since that's the dominant deployment pattern today. The current 13-reference structure covers the framework but not the ecosystem.

🟡 The Outsider

Reading this skill as someone unfamiliar with the project, I'm confused by several things. First, the 'scope: django' field in the frontmatter — what does 'scope' mean here? Is this a Pi skill scope? A Django scope? It's never explained. Second, the 'source_basis: production experience' claim is unverifiable — there's no way for me to know if these patterns are battle-tested or just opinions. Third, the pattern counts in the reference map (e.g., '25 patterns', '21 patterns') are completely opaque — patterns of what? Code examples? Gotchas? I have no idea what I'm getting when I open a file. Fourth, the skill references 'django-partials style' without explaining what that is — is that a third-party package? A convention? Fifth, the 'Backend Partials and HTMX Mindset' section says 'Default composition path in Django: templates + includes/partials/macros' but this is not a universal Django default — it's a specific architectural choice. Sixth, there's no 'Getting Started' or 'Prerequisites' section — I don't know what I need to know before using this skill. Is this for beginners? Experts? The skill assumes I know what HTMX is, what django-partials is, and what 'cross-context imports' means, but none of these are explained. A brief 'Who this is for' and 'What you should know first' section would dramatically improve accessibility.

🟠 The Executor

The fastest improvement: delete REFERENCE.md entirely and merge its routing table into SKILL.md. Right now every agent must read SKILL.md → REFERENCE.md → target reference file. That's 3 reads where 1 suffices. Second, fix the version claim — change 'Django 6.0' to 'Django 5.x' or make it version-agnostic. A skill that claims a non-existent version is broken on arrival. Third, add a 'Quick Patterns' section to SKILL.md with the top 5 most-needed code patterns inline (a model with Meta, a CBV with get_queryset, a custom manager, a signal receiver, a test case) so agents can act without reading a reference file for simple tasks. Fourth, add a 'Common Errors' section with the 10 most frequent Django mistakes and their one-line fixes — this alone would prevent more bugs than all the reference files combined. Fifth, add missing reference files for migrations, caching, and management commands — these are daily needs with zero coverage. Sixth, remove the HTMX/tabler bias from the core skill or move it to a companion skill. Each of these is a small, independent change that can be done in under 30 minutes. Start with the version fix and REFERENCE.md deletion — those are zero-effort, high-impact.

Add a maintenance note to the frontmatter specifying update triggers and ownership. Defer the larger restructuring (workflow-phase organization, i18n/email/logging references, eval coverage) to a follow-up pass.

First Step

Fix the version claim in SKILL.md frontmatter from 'Django 6.0' to 'Django 5.x' (or 'Django 5.x+') and update last_verified to today's date. This is a one-line change that eliminates the most critical credibility problem in the entire skill.

docker

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/docker/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the docker skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a serious scope problem. The description claims it covers 'Docker-specific work' but the reference map has 11 reference files — that's not a skill, that's a documentation library. An agent is supposed to 'read only the reference files needed for the task,' but there's no guidance on how to pick efficiently. The routing table has 10 single-domain routes and 5 multi-domain routes, but the criteria overlap heavily. 'Fix daemon connectivity' and 'Diagnose a broken Docker setup' both point to troubleshooting.md — why have both? The 'Critical Rules' section is a mixed bag of actual rules (secrets don't belong in ARG/ENV), advice (prefer multi-stage images), and process steps (boundary first). These are three different categories crammed into one numbered list with equal weight. Rule 11 and 12 are about agent behavior during hypothetical tasks — they belong in a 'Hypothetical Review Mode' section, not in critical rules. Speaking of which, the Hypothetical Review Mode section duplicates rules 11-12 almost verbatim. The 'Output Expectations' section mandates a rigid answer shape (Primary boundary, References used, What is happening, etc.) that will produce robotic, formulaic responses. Real Docker problems don't fit neatly into six labeled boxes. The 'When Not To Use This Skill' section correctly excludes Kubernetes and Helm but then adds 'Generic OCI internals' — that's too vague. What counts as 'generic OCI'? The skill also completely ignores Docker Swarm, which is still used in production. No mention of rootless Docker, which is increasingly common. The description mentions 'Docker Desktop VM behavior' but there's no Desktop-specific reference file. The skill promises coverage it doesn't deliver.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip this down to fundamentals. What is this skill actually for? It exists so that when an agent encounters a Docker problem, it loads the right knowledge and produces a correct, safe answer. That's it. Everything else is scaffolding. So let's ask: what are the core decisions an agent needs to make? First, is this even a Docker problem? The 'When Not To Use This Skill' section answers this, but it's buried at the top. It should be the very first thing — a clear decision gate. Second, which domain does this belong to? The routing table answers this, but it's a flat list. A decision tree would be more effective: Is it a build problem? → Dockerfile/BuildKit. Is it a runtime problem? → networking, storage, or engine. Is it multi-container? → Compose. Third, what's the safest way to fix it? The critical rules try to answer this but mix safety rules with process rules. Separate them. Safety rules (secrets, root access, daemon security) are non-negotiable. Process rules (boundary first, context checks first) are methodology. The reference architecture is actually decent — domain-specific files that can be loaded on demand. But the SKILL.md itself is doing too much work. It should be a thin router: here's how to decide if this is Docker, here's how to pick a reference, here's the safety checklist. The reference files should carry the knowledge, not the skill file. Currently the skill file is ~200 lines of rules and process that could be 50 lines.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is playing it too safe. It's structured as a pure reference router, but Docker problems in the wild are messy and cross-cutting. The skill should embrace this by adding a 'Common Cross-Cutting Scenarios' section with fully worked examples that span multiple references. For instance: 'You're containerizing a Python web app with a database' — walk through Dockerfile authoring, Compose wiring, networking between containers, volume persistence, and healthchecks as one integrated flow. This would make the skill dramatically more useful for the most common real-world task. Second, the skill should add a 'Docker Patterns' reference file covering proven patterns: the init container pattern, the sidecar pattern, the builder pattern for multi-stage builds, the docker-compose.override.yml pattern for dev/prod splits. These are what practitioners actually need. Third, the skill should explicitly address the Docker ↔ CI/CD intersection — GitHub Actions with Docker, GitLab CI runners, building in CI vs locally. This is where most developers hit Docker pain, and the skill is silent on it. Fourth, add a 'Migration Guide' section: moving from docker-compose V1 to V2, from Dockerfile to BuildKit features, from bind mounts to named volumes. These are high-value, frequently-needed transitions. Finally, the skill should have an eval suite — concrete scenarios with expected outputs — so we can measure whether agents actually use it correctly.

🟡 The Outsider

I have no context about this project. Reading this skill cold, here's what confuses me. The description says 'Use this skill for Docker-specific work' but then lists 15+ specific scenarios. Which one is the primary trigger? If I'm an agent trying to decide whether to load this skill, the description is simultaneously too broad ('Docker-specific work') and too long (it goes on for three paragraphs). The first sentence should be the trigger. Everything else is elaboration. The 'Quick Start' section says 'Identify the boundary first: client vs daemon, build vs runtime' — but I don't know what these boundaries mean yet. This skill assumes I already understand Docker's architecture. A newcomer agent would be lost. There's no 'Docker 101' primer — no explanation of what a daemon is, what an image vs container is, what a Dockerfile actually does. The skill says to read reference files, but the SKILL.md itself should give me enough context to choose correctly. The 'Critical Rules' section has 12 items. That's too many to internalize. I'd suggest grouping them: Safety Rules (4 items), Process Rules (4 items), and Agent Behavior Rules (4 items). The routing table uses file names like 'engine-config-contexts.md' — I can't tell from the filename what 'engine' means. More descriptive route labels would help. The 'Output Expectations' section mandates a specific answer format, which is good for consistency but the labels ('Primary boundary', 'Highest-risk footgun') are jargon that not all agents will interpret consistently. Finally, the 'Content Ownership' section at the bottom feels like it was written for maintainers, not for agents using the skill. It should be in a separate maintainer doc or clearly marked as meta-information.

🟠 The Executor

The highest-impact improvement is fixing the description for triggering accuracy. Right now it's three dense paragraphs. Most agents decide whether to load a skill based on the first sentence. The current first sentence is 'Use this skill for Docker-specific work: containerizing an app, writing or fixing a Dockerfile or compose.yaml, troubleshooting docker build or docker compose' — that's okay but it buries the most common tasks after a colon. Lead with the top 3 triggers: Dockerfile, compose.yaml, docker build. Everything else is secondary. Second, the routing table needs priority ordering. Currently it's alphabetical-ish. Reorder by frequency: Dockerfile/images first (most common), then Compose, then BuildKit, then networking, then storage, then the rest. Third, add a 'Quick Fixes' section — the 10 most common Docker problems and their one-line solutions. Things like 'permission denied on bind mount' → 'check UID/GID mapping', 'container exits immediately' → 'check CMD/ENTRYPOINT and docker logs'. This gives agents fast answers without loading reference files. Fourth, the Hypothetical Review Mode section should be merged with Critical Rules 11-12 to eliminate duplication. Fifth, add concrete code examples to the SKILL.md itself — a minimal Dockerfile, a minimal compose.yaml. These serve as templates and reduce the chance of the agent generating broken configs from scratch. Everything else is nice-to-have. These five changes would improve the skill immediately with minimal effort.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

Response E (The Executor) is strongest because it identifies the single most actionable problem — triggering accuracy — and proposes concrete, low-effort fixes. Reordering the routing table by frequency and adding a Quick Fixes section are changes that could be implemented in minutes and would improve every single skill invocation.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response C (The Expansionist) has the biggest blind spot. It proposes adding CI/CD coverage, migration guides, Docker patterns, and an eval suite. Each of these is valid but together they'd triple the skill's size. The skill is already at 11 reference files. Adding more surface area without pruning existing content will make it harder to load and slower to use. Expansion without consolidation is bloat.

What All Missed

All responses missed the testing gap. The skill has no evals/ directory, no test scenarios, no way to verify that an agent actually produces correct Docker guidance after loading it. For a skill this large and this important, the absence of any eval coverage is a critical quality hole. None of the five advisors mentioned it.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

Response B (The First Principles Thinker) cuts through the noise best. Identifying that the SKILL.md should be a thin router — not a knowledge base — is the architectural insight this skill needs. The observation that safety rules, process rules, and agent behavior rules are conflated is precise and actionable.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response A (The Contrarian) is too aggressive on the 'scope problem' critique. Having 11 reference files is fine for a domain as large as Docker. The alternative — cramming everything into one file — would be worse. The Contrarian correctly identifies the rule conflation but throws out the baby with the bathwater on the reference architecture.

What All Missed

All responses missed that the skill has no versioning guidance. Docker changes significantly between versions (Compose V1 vs V2, BuildKit becoming default, Docker Desktop licensing changes). The skill should state which Docker version(s) it targets and note where behavior differs across versions. This is a correctness issue, not just a completeness issue.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

Response D (The Outsider) provides the most valuable perspective because it reveals the curse of knowledge embedded in the skill. The observation that 'engine-config-contexts.md' is an opaque filename for routing, and that the skill assumes Docker architectural knowledge the agent may not have, are critical usability findings. The suggestion to group Critical Rules into Safety/Process/Behavior is simple and would improve scanability.

Response E (The Executor) focuses on quick wins but misses structural problems. Adding a Quick Fixes section is useful but doesn't address the fundamental issue that the SKILL.md is doing too much work that should live in references. Quick Fixes will also rot faster than reference content because they're context-free.

All responses missed the Docker Desktop gap. The description explicitly mentions 'Docker Desktop VM behavior' as a trigger, but there's no Desktop-specific reference file. This means the skill will trigger for Desktop-specific questions it can't actually answer well. Either add a reference or remove Desktop from the description.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

Response A (The Contrarian) is strongest because it's the only one that calls out specific, concrete omissions: no rootless Docker coverage, no Docker Swarm, no Desktop reference file despite Desktop being in the description. These are real gaps that will cause the skill to fail in production. The critique of the Output Expectations producing robotic answers is also sharp and likely correct.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response C (The Expansionist) proposes an integrated 'containerize a Python web app' walkthrough. This is actually the most common Docker task, and the skill provides no end-to-end guidance for it. However, the Expansionist doesn't propose where this lives — in the SKILL.md (making it heavier) or as a new reference file (adding to the 11-file count). Without addressing placement, the proposal is incomplete.

What All Missed

All responses missed the interaction between this skill and other skills in the ecosystem. The global AGENTS.md has extensive Docker rules (sections 9.2, 10, 11) covering Docker Compose port merging, test environments, non-root UIDs, and more. This skill doesn't acknowledge or reference those rules. An agent loading both could get conflicting guidance. The skill should explicitly note where project-level Docker rules (AGENTS.md) take precedence.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

Response B (The First Principles Thinker) and Response D (The Outsider) together form the strongest combined insight. B says 'make the SKILL.md a thin router,' and D says 'the SKILL.md assumes knowledge the agent may not have.' The synthesis is clear: the SKILL.md needs a brief conceptual primer (2-3 sentences per Docker concept) plus a clean routing table, and nothing else. All process rules, output formats, and hypothetical mode instructions should move to a meta-reference or be simplified.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response A (The Contrarian) dismisses the 11-reference architecture but doesn't propose a concrete alternative. 'That's not a skill, that's a documentation library' is a punchline, not a solution. If not 11 reference files, then what? A single monolithic file? Sub-skills? The Contrarian doesn't say.

What All Missed

All responses missed that the skill's 'Output Expectations' section creates a contract that may conflict with the user's actual request. If a user asks 'what's wrong with my Dockerfile?', they don't want a six-part structured response — they want the fix. The mandatory output format should be a default, not a requirement. The skill should say 'use this format unless the user's request implies a different one.' None of the advisors flagged this rigidity issue.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council agrees on three things. First, the Critical Rules section is poorly organized — safety rules, process rules, and agent behavior rules are conflated and should be separated. Second, the Hypothetical Review Mode duplicates Critical Rules 11-12 and should be consolidated. Third, the description is too long and too dense for reliable triggering; the first sentence should carry the primary trigger weight. These are the highest-priority fixes.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether the 11-reference architecture is a strength or a weakness. The Contrarian calls it a scope problem; the Expansionist wants to add more. The First Principles Thinker says the references are fine but the SKILL.md is doing their job. I side with the First Principles Thinker — the reference architecture is sound, the SKILL.md is just too heavy. The council also clashes on whether to expand (Expansionist: add CI/CD, patterns, migration guides) or contract (Executor: streamline, prioritize). Expansion without fixing the structural problems would make a bad situation worse. Fix the foundation first.

Blind Spots Caught

The council collectively missed four things: (1) no eval suite exists to measure skill effectiveness, (2) no version targeting — the skill doesn't state which Docker versions it covers, (3) the Docker Desktop gap — mentioned in the description but has no reference file, and (4) no acknowledgment of the Docker rules in the global AGENTS.md that may overlap or conflict. The eval gap is the most critical blind spot because it means we can't measure whether any improvements actually work.

The Recommendation

Execute a three-phase improvement. Phase 1 (immediate, low effort): Restructure Critical Rules into three labeled groups (Safety, Process, Agent Behavior). Merge Hypothetical Review Mode with the duplicated rules. Rewrite the description's first sentence to lead with the top 3 triggers. Reorder the routing table by frequency. Phase 2 (medium effort): Add a brief Docker concepts primer (daemon, image, container, Dockerfile, Compose) to the SKILL.md so agents without Docker context can still route correctly. Add a 'Quick Fixes' section with the 10 most common Docker problems. Create a Docker Desktop reference file or remove Desktop from the description. Phase 3 (larger investment): Add an evals/ directory with 5-10 scenarios covering the most common Docker tasks. Add a version note stating which Docker versions the skill targets. Add a note about AGENTS.md Docker rule interaction.

First Step

Restructure the Critical Rules section. Split the 12 rules into three clearly labeled groups: Safety Rules (secrets, root access, daemon security, tags vs digests), Process Rules (boundary first, context checks, .dockerignore, mount types, Compose readiness), and Agent Behavior Rules (hypothetical review, file search limits, working from described scenarios). This is a pure reorganization — no new content, no new research — and it immediately improves scanability and resolves the conflation that every advisor identified as a problem.

9 / 26

documentation-lifecycle

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/documentation-lifecycle/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can this skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a fatal design flaw: it reads like a specification document for a documentation department, not a skill an agent will follow under time pressure. The routing matrix is the only genuinely useful thing here — everything else is padding. Rule 0 ('Documents are files, not chat messages') is the single most important line in the entire skill, and it's buried in Critical Rules section 0 where a tired agent will skim past it. I bet agents routinely violate this rule because the skill does nothing structural to prevent it — it just says 'don't do that.' Where's the enforcement? Where's the hard failure mode? The skill also catastrophically underestimates how often agents will need to operate without the reference files existing. What happens when `references/REFERENCE.md` doesn't exist yet? Onboarding a greenfield repo? The skill says 'Read only the smallest reference file that matches' but provides zero guidance for when those files are missing. It assumes a mature doc structure that most projects don't have. The interview guidance is pure theater — 'use dialectic synthesis' and 'steelman the user's goal' — these are inflated terms for 'ask clarifying questions.' The language is inflated throughout: 'documentation theater,' 'polished lie,' 'documentation drift is a bug.' Pick a register and stick with it. Right now it oscillates between academic jargon and blunt pragmatism. The content ownership section at the bottom is entirely redundant with the opening paragraph. Cut 60% of this skill and it gets better.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Let's strip away everything ornamental. What problem are we actually solving? There is a class of agent tasks where software changes and documentation must change with it. The agent needs three things: (1) know WHICH document types exist and when each applies, (2) know WHAT format each document type requires, (3) write the OUTPUT to disk, not to chat. That's it. The skill currently addresses (1) with the routing matrix — adequate but not great. It addresses (2) by delegating to reference files, which is architecturally sound but means the skill body itself teaches nothing about what a good ADR looks like versus a good runbook. It addresses (3) with rule 0, which is phrased as a prohibition rather than a step in a workflow. The real first-principles restructure would be: (a) explicit trigger — when does this skill apply vs. normal documentation writing? (b) a decision tree with concrete handles — not just routing to reference files, but actual in-skill logic for thin cases; (c) a verification step — after writing, confirm it. The current skill optimizes for the case where a mature doc structure already exists. But the hardest documentation problems happen in immature repos where there is no structure. The skill should bootstrap the structure, not assume it. Also, the skill never defines what 'done' looks like for a documentation task. Is it 'file written'? 'File reviewed'? 'Stakeholders notified'? Without a clear completion definition, agents will either over-deliver or under-deliver unpredictably.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a much bigger opportunity than it realizes. Right now it's a routing skill — it tells agents which doc type to use. But the real pain in documentation lifecycle isn't routing; it's synchronization. The skill should own the concept of a 'doc surface inventory' — a living map of every document that references a given code module, API endpoint, or configuration surface. When an agent changes an API contract, the skill should be able to answer 'which docs reference this endpoint?' That's a graph problem, and this skill doesn't even acknowledge it exists. Second, the skill should integrate with git. Documentation drift is currently defined as a state ('doc is stale') but the real detection mechanism is temporal — when did this doc last get updated relative to the code it describes? A simple heuristic (doc last-updated vs. code last-touched) would catch drift automatically. Third, the skill should handle documentation generation from code — not full auto-doc, but scaffolding. When you add a new endpoint, the skill should generate a reference-doc skeleton with the known facts (path, method, parameters) pre-filled. This turns documentation from a writing task into a verification task, which is far more reliable. Fourth, the skill should address documentation for AI agents specifically — when docs are consumed by both humans and agents, the requirements differ. Machine-readable contracts (OpenAPI, JSON Schema) need different treatment than prose tutorials. The skill ignores this entirely.

🟡 The Outsider

I have no context about this project and I'm reading this skill for the first time. Here's what confuses me: The skill references seven files in a `references/` directory but I have no idea if those files exist, what format they're in, or how to get them. The skill says 'Read only the smallest reference file that matches' — but what if I don't know what's in those files? What if the reference files are themselves long and I need to know which section applies? There's no guidance on how to navigate the reference files themselves. Also, the skill uses the word 'canonical' repeatedly without defining it. Canonical according to whom? Who decides which document is authoritative? The skill assumes there's already a governance structure for documentation, but if I'm a new agent on a new project, there isn't one. The routing matrix has seven rows but the classification step 1 lists seven task types — are these the same seven? They don't match exactly. 'Vague or disputed change request' maps to 'Vague request with missing constraints' — close but not identical. 'Operational / deployment / recovery change' maps to 'Deployment, migration, rollback, incident response' — again close. This near-match is worse than a clear mismatch because it creates false confidence. The skill also never explains what to do when multiple doc types need updating. The routing matrix picks a PRIMARY doc type, but real changes often touch three or four doc types. The skill says 'Update existing authoritative docs before creating new ones' but doesn't say which order when multiple docs need updating.

🟠 The Executor

Here's what I'd do in the next 30 minutes to make this skill actually work better: First, move rule 0 ('Documents are files, not chat messages') to the very first line of the skill body, before the routing matrix. It's the single point of failure for this entire skill and it's buried at critical rule 0. Second, add a one-line verification step at the end of the default workflow: 'Run `ls` or `git diff` to confirm the file was written.' Agents forget to verify; make it a step, not a suggestion. Third, add a fallback for missing reference files. Right now the skill says 'Read the smallest reference file that matches' but if `references/` doesn't exist, the agent stalls. Add: 'If the reference file does not exist, create it using the template in the routing matrix row.' Fourth, cut the interview and synthesis guidance section by half. 'Steelman the user's goal' and 'dialectic synthesis' are not actionable instructions — they're academic vocabulary. Replace with: 'Restate the goal in one sentence. Ask the minimum questions needed to remove ambiguity. If there's a real tradeoff, name it and ask the user to pick.' Fifth, add a concrete example. The entire skill has zero examples of input → routing → output. One worked example (vague request → spec interview → feature spec written to disk) would do more for agent compliance than all the Socratic questioning guidance combined.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor — it identifies the single highest-impact change (moving rule 0 to the top) and provides a concrete, immediately actionable improvement plan. Every suggestion is implementable in under five minutes and addresses a real failure mode rather than a theoretical concern.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist — while the ideas about doc surface inventory and git integration are intellectually interesting, they describe a product, not a skill improvement. The skill is a prompt, not a codebase. Suggesting graph-based doc tracking is scope creep that would make the skill unmaintainable.

What All Missed

None of the reviewers addressed the skill's triggering mechanism. The description field is the only thing that controls when this skill gets loaded, and it's missing key triggers like 'update README,' 'write ADR,' or 'document this change.' The skill could be perfect internally and still never trigger when needed.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker — correctly identifies that the skill assumes a mature doc structure and fails for greenfield repos. This is the most structurally important observation. The restructure proposal (trigger → decision tree → verification) is cleaner than the current design.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian — the claim that 'interview guidance is pure theater' is wrong. The interview section explicitly says 'If there is no real conflict, do not perform fake adversarial theater.' It already guards against its own abuse. Dismissing the entire section because of some jargon is throwing out the baby with the bathwater.

What All Missed

No one discussed how this skill interacts with other skills. The skill says 'Pair it with the relevant framework or product skill once the documentation contract is clear' — but what does that handoff look like? Is there a contract between skills? This is a gap that will cause real confusion in multi-skill workflows.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider — the observation that the routing matrix rows and the classification step items are near-identical but not quite matched is a genuine defect that every other reviewer walked past. This kind of near-match causes agents to make wrong routing decisions. The outsider perspective also correctly identifies the 'canonical' terminology as undefined jargon.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor — while the suggestions are practical, they're incremental. The Executor optimizes the current structure without questioning whether the structure itself is right. Moving rule 0 to the top is good, but it doesn't fix the fundamental problem that the skill is a routing doc pretending to be a workflow.

What All Missed

No one addressed the skill's total lack of error handling. What should the agent do when it writes a file and the write fails? When the reference file is corrupted? When two agents are updating docs concurrently? The skill has zero failure-mode guidance.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Contrarian — the observation that rule 0 is buried is correct and important. The critique of inflated language ('documentation theater,' 'polished lie') is also valid — the skill should pick a consistent register. The 60% cut recommendation is aggressive but directionally right; the skill is over-written for what it needs to accomplish.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist — the doc surface inventory idea assumes infrastructure that doesn't exist. The skill runs inside an agent prompt. It can't maintain a graph database of doc-to-code references. This is a tooling problem, not a skill problem, and conflating the two weakens the review.

What All Missed

No one discussed the skill's audience. Is this skill for junior agents who need hand-holding, or senior agents who need a checklist? The current skill tries to serve both and succeeds at neither. A junior agent needs examples; a senior agent needs a routing table. The skill gives both groups less than they need.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Executor — the suggestion to add a worked example is the single highest-leverage improvement identified by any reviewer. The entire skill is abstract with zero concrete demonstrations. One input → routing → output example would improve agent compliance more than all the structural rewrites combined.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker — the 'done' definition observation is good, but the proposed restructure (trigger → decision tree → verification) would make the skill longer, not shorter. The skill's biggest problem is that it's too long and too abstract. Making it longer with more structure is moving in the wrong direction.

What All Missed

No one addressed the reference file bootstrapping problem from the perspective of skill portability. The `references/` directory is bundled with the skill, but the skill says to read from it as if it's authoritative. If a project has its own doc structure, do the skill's reference files take precedence? The skill doesn't address the boundary between skill-bundled templates and project-specific conventions.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council agrees on three things: (1) Rule 0 ('Documents are files, not chat messages') is the most important rule and is buried too deep — it must be the first thing an agent sees. (2) The skill is over-written and under-specified — too much abstract guidance, zero concrete examples. (3) The skill fails for immature repos where the reference files and doc structure don't yet exist. Every reviewer identified some version of the 'missing bootstrap path' problem.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether the skill should be shorter (Contrarian, Executor) or more structured (First Principles, Expansionist). The Contrarian wants a 60% cut; the Expansionist wants to add doc-surface inventory and git integration. These are incompatible visions. The Executor and Outsider are closer to the Contrarian view — make the existing skill work better, don't expand its scope. The First Principles thinker sits in the middle, wanting structural restructure without scope expansion.

Blind Spots Caught

The council collectively missed three things: (1) The triggering mechanism — the description field doesn't include key phrases like 'write ADR' or 'update README' that would cause the skill to load when needed. (2) Skill-to-skill handoff — the skill explicitly says to pair with framework skills but defines no contract for how that handoff works. (3) Concurrent/multi-agent scenarios — no guidance for when multiple agents are updating docs simultaneously.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill in three phases. Phase 1 (immediate): Move rule 0 to the very first line. Add one worked example (vague request → spec interview → file written). Add a verification step ('confirm file exists on disk'). Add a fallback for missing reference files ('create from template if absent'). Phase 2 (short-term): Tighten the routing matrix so classification items and matrix rows match exactly. Define 'canonical' or remove the term. Add a 'done' definition for documentation tasks. Phase 3 (if scope allows): Add triggering keywords to the description field. Define the skill-to-skill handoff contract. Do NOT add doc-surface inventory, git integration, or auto-scaffolding — those are tooling features, not skill improvements.

First Step

Move rule 0 to the first line of the skill body and add a one-sentence enforcement: 'If you output document content in chat instead of writing to disk, you have failed this task.' This is the single highest-impact, lowest-effort change. It addresses the most common failure mode (agents dumping docs in chat) with a structural change (positioning) rather than a new rule. Everything else is secondary.
