drf

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/drf/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can this DRF skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a fatal structural problem: it's a routing document, not a skill. The SKILL.md is essentially a table of contents pointing to eight reference files that the agent must then read. This means the skill triggers, adds tokens for the routing layer, and then the agent reads the actual content anyway. The 'Critical Rules' section is the most valuable part of the entire SKILL.md — 14 hard-won gotchas that save real debugging time — but it's buried mid-file and reads like a random grab bag. There's no prioritization. Rule #1 (fields/exclude mandatory) affects every single serializer. Rule #11 (throttling race conditions) is an edge case. They're mixed together as if equally important. The 'When Not To Use This Skill' section is good but incomplete — it doesn't mention FastAPI, which is the #1 confusion point I see in practice: agents reaching for DRF when the project is actually using FastAPI or Django Ninja. The cross-domain routing table has a duplicate entry (Serializer with permissions appears twice). That's a copy-paste error that signals nobody reviewed this carefully. The skill also has zero guidance on common anti-patterns: no mention of N+1 queries via serializers, no warning about massive querysets in ListAPIView, no guidance on when to use SerializerMethodField vs annotation. These are the mistakes that actually hurt production systems.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

What is this skill actually trying to accomplish? The stated goal is to help an agent correctly implement DRF APIs. But the skill assumes the agent knows what DRF is, which reference to read, and how to map a real-world task to the routing table. The fundamental question is: what does an agent get wrong when it DOESN'T have this skill? The answer: it hallucinates DRF APIs, uses outdated patterns (pre-3.0 style), misses security implications, and writes broken tests. The current skill addresses hallucination by providing reference files, but it does nothing for the other failure modes. The 'Critical Rules' section is the closest thing to preventing real mistakes, but it's a flat list with no context on WHY each rule exists or what failure it prevents. A first-principles rebuild would restructure around failure modes: 'Your serializer will break if...' / 'Your view will be insecure if...' / 'Your tests will be fake if...'. The reference architecture is sound — splitting into domain files is the right call. But the entry point should be a decision tree, not a flat table. The agent faces a task like 'add pagination to an existing endpoint' — it needs to know which file to read AND what the common pitfalls are. Currently it gets the routing but not the pitfalls unless it reads the reference file first.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is leaving enormous value on the table by being purely reactive. It waits for the agent to have a DRF question, then routes to a reference. What if the skill could proactively catch DRF anti-patterns in code review? Add a 'Code Review Checklist' section that lets the agent audit existing DRF code: 'Are there ModelSerializers without explicit fields? Are there ListAPIViews without pagination? Are there ViewSets with unrestricted destroy actions?' This turns the skill from a reference lookup into an active quality gate. Second, the skill should own the DRF + Django integration boundary more aggressively. The line between DRF and pure Django is where the worst bugs live — CSRF issues, session auth confusion, ORM queries inside serializers. The skill should have a dedicated section on 'DRF-Django boundary pitfalls' that covers request lifecycle differences, CSRF implications of token auth vs session auth, and queryset evaluation timing. Third, there's no mention of DRF's schema generation (OpenAPI/Swagger). In 2024+, most teams need API documentation. The skill should route schema questions to the right reference or explicitly own that domain. Finally, add a 'Performance' section: select_related/prefetch_related in ViewSet querysets, serializer depth control, pagination as a performance tool — not just a UX feature.

🟡 The Outsider

I have no idea what this project is or what DRF stands for. Reading the SKILL.md, I see 'Django REST Framework' in the title but the first paragraph is dense with jargon: 'serializers, fields, relations, validators, views, APIView, @api_view, generics, viewsets, routers, @action, authentication, permissions, throttling, filtering, pagination, parsers, renderers, content negotiation.' That's a wall of nouns with no explanation. If I'm a fresh agent instance that hasn't seen DRF before, this doesn't orient me. The 'Quick Start' section says 'Identify the primary domain of the task' — but how do I identify a domain if I don't know what these terms mean? The routing table uses file names like 'serializers-fields.md' which are opaque. What does 'internals.md' contain? The description says 'Dispatch flow, parsing pipeline, versioning, schemas' — but that's hidden behind the table. The 'Critical Rules' section is actually the most readable part because each rule is self-contained and explains a concrete failure. More of that style would help. The skill also assumes I know what 'loading @django' means — this is framework-specific jargon for skill loading that isn't explained anywhere in the SKILL.md itself. A two-sentence glossary at the top would transform this from an insider document to something an outsider can use.

🟠 The Executor

The fastest improvement: move the Critical Rules to the top of SKILL.md, above the Quick Start. These 14 rules are the highest-density value in the entire skill — they prevent real bugs. Right now an agent has to read past the routing table, the when-not-to-use section, and the quick start before it hits the rules that matter. Second: fix the duplicate cross-domain entry. It's a one-line fix and it signals quality. Third: add a 'Minimal Example' section — one complete ViewSet + Serializer + Router registration that works end-to-end. Agents pattern-match from examples faster than from routing tables. Fourth: the 'Output Expectations' section says 'Name the reference files used' — this is meta-guidance for the agent's output, but it's buried. Make it a one-line callout at the top. Fifth: add a single anti-pattern — the N+1 query in serializer relations — as a concrete example under Critical Rules. It's the #1 production performance issue with DRF and it's not mentioned anywhere. These five changes take 30 minutes to implement and would measurably reduce DRF-related bugs in generated code. The reference files are fine as-is for now — the problem is the entry point, not the content.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor — it identifies the highest-leverage change (moving Critical Rules to top) and backs it with a concrete, prioritized list of fixes. The five-step plan is actionable without being overwhelming.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian correctly identifies the duplicate entry and the N+1 gap, but its tone undermines the valid points. It calls the skill 'a random grab bag' when the Critical Rules are actually well-crafted — they just need better positioning. The FastAPI confusion point is strong though.

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed the skill's lack of version awareness. DRF has had significant changes across versions (3.x to 3.14+), and the skill doesn't specify which version it targets or note version-specific gotchas. This matters because agents may apply deprecated patterns.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker — it correctly identifies that the skill is a routing document and asks what failure modes it actually prevents. The 'failure modes' reframe is the most intellectually honest assessment and would lead to the best structural improvement.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist's code review checklist idea is exciting but scope creep. The skill is already 150+ lines in SKILL.md alone, plus 8 reference files. Adding a full code review section would bloat it beyond what an agent reads in a single turn. The idea should be a separate skill, not bolted onto this one.

What All Missed

Nobody mentioned that the skill has no eval or test suite. For a skill that's supposed to prevent DRF bugs, there's no way to verify it actually works. Where are the evals that test whether an agent with this skill produces correct DRF code vs. one without it?

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Contrarian — despite the aggressive tone, it surfaces the most concrete bugs: the duplicate routing entry, the missing N+1 warning, the missing FastAPI exclusion. These are real defects that should be fixed immediately.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider's critique of jargon is valid but impractical. This is a specialist skill for a specialist framework. Simplifying 'serializers' and 'viewsets' for newcomers would make the skill useless for its actual audience — agents who need DRF reference material, not a DRF tutorial. The skill should assume framework knowledge.

What All Missed

The skill's description frontmatter is its triggering mechanism, and none of the advisors evaluated it. The description is comprehensive but could be tightened — 'building, securing, or fixing REST APIs powered by rest_framework' is the key trigger phrase, but it's buried in a long sentence. Triggering accuracy depends on this text.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Executor and The Contrarian together provide the most complete picture. Executor identifies the structural fix (rules first), Contrarian identifies the content gaps (N+1, FastAPI). Combined, they give a clear improvement path.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker's 'failure mode' restructuring idea is theoretically elegant but would require rewriting all 8 reference files to follow a consistent pattern. That's a massive refactor for uncertain benefit. The current file-per-domain structure is fine; the entry point is what needs work.

What All Missed

The skill has no guidance on DRF's relationship with Django's async views. DRF 3.14+ added async support, but most of the skill's patterns (ViewSet, generics) are synchronous. An agent trying to use DRF in an async Django project would get no help here.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Expansionist's DRF-Django boundary section idea is the single most valuable addition nobody else mentioned. The CSRF/session/auth confusion at the DRF-Django boundary is the #1 source of security bugs in Django API projects, and the skill currently says nothing about it.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor's 'minimal example' suggestion sounds good but is a trap. A minimal example becomes a cargo-cult pattern that agents copy without understanding. The Critical Rules approach (explaining what breaks) is more robust than the example approach (showing what works).

What All Missed

The skill references 'tests/test_validate_refs.py' implicitly through the repo's AGENTS.md, but the SKILL.md itself says nothing about how to verify the skill's own accuracy. A skill about API testing should mention that its own references need periodic validation against the actual DRF source code.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

All five advisors agree the Critical Rules section is the most valuable content and needs better positioning. Four of five agree the skill is missing performance/N+1 guidance. All agree the duplicate cross-domain entry is a defect. Three agree the skill needs better DRF-Django boundary guidance. Three agree the entry point (SKILL.md) is a routing layer that adds tokens without adding proportional value.

Where the Council Clashes

The Contrarian wants the skill to be more aggressive about anti-patterns; The Outsider wants it to be more accessible to newcomers. These are in tension — adding glossary content for outsiders would bloat the skill for its target audience. The Expansionist wants to add code review and schema generation; Reviewer 2 correctly flags this as scope creep. The First Principles Thinker wants a full restructuring around failure modes; The Executor argues for targeted surgical fixes to the existing structure.

Blind Spots Caught

(1) No advisor evaluated the frontmatter description for triggering accuracy — this is the skill's actual interface with the agent runtime. (2) No advisor mentioned DRF async support or version targeting. (3) The skill has no self-validation mechanism — no evals, no test that references match real DRF APIs. (4) The skill doesn't address the DRF-Django CSRF/session boundary, which is a real security concern.

The Recommendation

Execute a surgical improvement in three phases. Phase 1 (immediate): Move Critical Rules to the top of SKILL.md, fix the duplicate cross-domain entry, add N+1 query warning as Critical Rule #15. Phase 2 (short-term): Add a 'DRF-Django Boundary' section covering CSRF implications, request lifecycle differences, and queryset evaluation timing. Tighten the frontmatter description for triggering accuracy. Phase 3 (medium-term): Create an eval suite that verifies the skill produces correct DRF code for common tasks (serializer with nested write, secured ViewSet, paginated list).

First Step

Move the Critical Rules section from its current position (after 'When Not To Use This Skill') to immediately after the Quick Start section, before the Reference Map. This is a pure reorganization that doesn't add content but ensures agents see the gotchas before they start reading references. Fix the duplicate 'Serializer with permissions' cross-domain entry in the same edit.

11 / 26

full-calendar

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/full-calendar/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the FullCalendar skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a fatal structural problem: it's a table of contents pretending to be a skill. The SKILL.md is 6.6KB of pointers to reference files that are themselves 7KB each. An agent reading this gets almost zero actionable information from the entry point — it must read 40KB+ of reference files to answer even basic questions. The 'Critical Rules' section is the only genuinely useful part of SKILL.md, and it's buried after conceptual fluff. The 'Quick Start' is four vague steps that say 'identify what you need' and 'open the matching reference file' — that's not guidance, that's a directory listing. The HTMX integration section is disproportionately large for what is an edge case; most FullCalendar users aren't using HTMX. Meanwhile, there's zero mention of timezone handling, which is the single most common source of production bugs with calendar apps. No mention of recurring events, which FullCalendar handles poorly and every developer gets wrong. The 'Task Routing' table is the right idea but too coarse — 'Handle clicks, selections, drag-drop' maps to callbacks.md, but that file is 7KB and covers 15+ callbacks. The skill needs to answer questions, not point at more files. Every reference file should have a 'Common Mistakes' section, and SKILL.md itself should contain the top 10 pitfalls inline, not make agents hunt for them.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

What problem does this skill actually solve? It exists so that an AI agent can help a developer build or debug a FullCalendar integration. That means the skill must bridge two gaps: (1) the agent doesn't know FullCalendar's API, and (2) the agent doesn't know the common integration patterns for the user's stack. Currently the skill addresses gap #1 with a mini-reference manual split across seven files. But reference manuals already exist — FullCalendar's official docs are excellent. The skill's unique value should be gap #2: the hard-won integration patterns that aren't in the official docs. Things like: how to wire FullCalendar's JSON feed to a Django view that returns paginated events; how to handle CSRF tokens in event CRUD operations; how to make FullCalendar work inside an HTMX partial swap without double-rendering; how to handle timezone conversion between server UTC and calendar display. The current skill mentions these in passing but doesn't solve them. The reference files should be reorganized around integration scenarios, not API surfaces. Instead of 'callbacks.md' listing every callback, have 'event-crud.md' that shows the full round-trip: click → HTMX request → server response → calendar update. The skill should teach the agent to be dangerous with FullCalendar, not just literate.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is leaving enormous value on the table by staying API-focused. FullCalendar is almost never used in isolation — it's embedded in larger applications with specific needs the skill ignores entirely. Consider: (1) Accessibility. FullCalendar has a11y pitfalls everywhere — keyboard navigation, screen reader announcements for date changes, focus management after drag-drop. Zero mention. (2) Mobile. FullCalendar's timeGrid view is nearly unusable on phones. The skill should guide agents toward responsive view selection and touch-friendly alternatives. (3) Performance. Calendar with 1000+ events? The skill should document virtual rendering, event source refetching strategies, and the `lazyFetching` option. (4) Testing. How do you test a FullCalendar integration? The skill should include Playwright patterns for interacting with calendar views, asserting event rendering, and simulating drag-drop. (5) Framework connectors. The skill says 'use vanilla JS unless the project explicitly uses React/Vue/Angular' — but it doesn't help the agent detect which is in use, or how to adapt patterns. (6) The evals file is a single JSON with no actual test scenarios. This skill should have evals that test whether an agent can actually build a working calendar, not just recite API docs. The skill could be the definitive FullCalendar integration guide, not just a reference mirror.

🟡 The Outsider

Reading this skill as someone who's never used FullCalendar and needs to help a developer with it: I'm lost within 30 seconds. The SKILL.md opens with 'Comprehensive reference for FullCalendar v6' but never explains what FullCalendar IS in terms a non-calendar person can grasp. The 'Core Concepts' section introduces five concepts but doesn't explain why any of them matter or how they relate. What's a 'render hook' and when would I need one? The skill assumes I know the difference between dayGrid and timeGrid views — I don't. The reference map is just filenames with one-line descriptions; I can't tell which file I need without opening it. The 'Task Routing' table is the most useful part, but it's at the bottom — put it at the top. Also, the skill has zero code examples in SKILL.md itself. Every other skill I've seen has at least a minimal code snippet showing what success looks like. Here, I'd have to read initialization.md just to see what `new Calendar(el, options)` looks like in context. The 'Critical Rules' section is genuinely excellent — the kind of thing that prevents real bugs. But it has only 8 rules; there are probably 20 more that experienced FullCalendar developers know and beginners learn the hard way. The skill reads like it was written by someone who knows FullCalendar very well and forgot what it's like to not know it.

🟠 The Executor

The fastest improvement: restructure SKILL.md to lead with the decision tree, not the encyclopedia. Right now an agent reading this skill has to understand the full reference architecture before it can help. Instead, SKILL.md should be a 200-line decision tree: 'Is this a new calendar? → initialization.md. Is this a bug with events not showing? → Check these 3 things in events.md. Is this an interaction bug? → Check these 3 things in callbacks.md.' The current 6.6KB SKILL.md should be under 2KB. Second: add a 'Top 10 FullCalendar Bugs' section directly in SKILL.md with the fix for each. The critical rules are a start but miss recurring events, timezone issues, DST transitions, allDay slot vs timed events, and the `eventDataTransform` gotcha. Third: the reference files are all ~7KB with no consistent structure. Standardize them: Overview, Common Patterns, Common Mistakes, Code Examples. Fourth: the HTMX integration section should be a standalone pattern, not woven into the core concepts. Most users won't use HTMX. Fifth: add one complete, copy-pasteable example in SKILL.md — a minimal working calendar with a JSON feed, event click handling, and a toolbar. That single example would teach more than 40KB of reference docs. Everything else is optimization.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor — the 'Top 10 FullCalendar Bugs' idea is the highest-leverage single improvement. It directly addresses the skill's biggest weakness (agents can't troubleshoot without reading 40KB) with minimal effort. The decision-tree restructure is also practical and immediately actionable.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist — the accessibility and mobile points are valid but expanding scope without fixing the foundation is how skills become bloated and useless. Adding Playwright testing patterns is a separate skill, not an improvement to this one.

What All Missed

None of the reviewers addressed the skill's eval coverage. The evals.json is a single file with no test scenarios — this means the skill can't be validated or improved quantitatively. Every reviewer should have flagged this.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker — correctly identifies that the skill's unique value isn't mirroring FullCalendar's docs (which already exist) but bridging the integration gap. The 'reorganize around integration scenarios, not API surfaces' insight is the strategic fix that makes all other improvements coherent.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian — correctly identifies the structural problem but the proposed solution (inline everything) would make SKILL.md 40KB and worse. The answer isn't to eliminate reference files; it's to make SKILL.md a better index and make the reference files more scannable.

What All Missed

The skill's description/triggering is too narrow. It says 'Use when tasks involve FullCalendar' but doesn't mention common failure modes that should trigger it: calendar not rendering, events not showing, wrong date display, drag-drop not working. These are the real-world triggers.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider — the 'zero code examples' finding is damning. Every effective skill has at least one copy-pasteable example. The observation that the skill 'reads like it was written by someone who knows FullCalendar very well and forgot what it's like to not know it' is the most important sentence in this entire debate.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor — the '200-line decision tree' idea is good but undersells the problem. It's not just about routing; it's about giving the agent enough context to make the right routing decision. A decision tree without context is just another index.

What All Missed

The skill has no version pinning guidance. It says 'v6' but doesn't specify which minor version, and FullCalendar v6 has had breaking changes between minors. An agent following this skill might install v6.1 and get different behavior than v6.3.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Contrarian — the timezone and recurring events gap is the most critical missing content. These aren't edge cases; they're the two features that cause the most production bugs in any calendar application. The skill's silence on these is a real failure.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker — the 'reorganize around integration scenarios' idea is strategically sound but operationally vague. What scenarios? How many? Without concrete examples, this is a direction, not a plan. The executor's decision tree is the tactical implementation of this strategy.

What All Missed

The skill doesn't address the CDN vs ES6 bundle tradeoffs in any meaningful way. The CDN bundle is easier but the ES6 bundle is tree-shakeable. For performance-sensitive apps, this matters. The skill should guide the agent on which to choose and when.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Expansionist — the accessibility point alone justifies this review. FullCalendar accessibility is a minefield and the skill's complete silence on it means agents will build inaccessible calendars. The mobile point is equally strong — every calendar needs to work on phones.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider — the 'put task routing at the top' suggestion is too simplistic. Task routing is useful for agents but useless for humans reading the skill. The skill serves both audiences and should be structured accordingly, not just optimized for agent scanning.

What All Missed

The skill has no migration guidance. Users upgrading from FullCalendar v5 to v6 face breaking changes. An agent working on a v5→v6 migration needs specific guidance, and the skill provides none. This is a common real-world scenario that's completely unaddressed.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees on three things: (1) SKILL.md is a poor entry point that forces agents to read 40KB+ of reference files for basic tasks — it needs to be a decision tree with inline troubleshooting, not a table of contents. (2) The skill is missing critical real-world content: timezone handling, recurring events, accessibility, and version-specific guidance. (3) The reference files, while individually decent, lack consistent structure and should be standardized with Common Patterns and Common Mistakes sections. There is also unanimous agreement that the eval coverage is inadequate — the skill cannot be validated or quantitatively improved without proper test scenarios.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on scope. The Expansionist wants to add accessibility, mobile, testing, and framework connector guidance — effectively doubling the skill's scope. The Executor and Contrarian want to ruthlessly focus on making the existing content more accessible and actionable. The Chairman's view: scope expansion is warranted only for accessibility (which is a correctness issue, not a nice-to-have) and mobile responsiveness (which affects a majority of real-world calendars). Testing patterns and framework connector detection belong in separate skills. The council also clashes on reorganization strategy: the First Principles Thinker wants scenario-based restructuring while the Executor wants a decision tree. These are complementary — the decision tree in SKILL.md routes to scenario-based reference files.

Blind Spots Caught

The council caught several collective blind spots: (1) No reviewer addressed the skill's triggering description — it should include failure-mode triggers like 'calendar not rendering' or 'events displaying on wrong dates'. (2) No reviewer questioned whether the reference file split is optimal — seven files might be too many, or too few. (3) No reviewer addressed the skill's relationship to FullCalendar's own documentation — it should link to official docs for API details and focus on integration patterns. (4) The CDN vs ES6 bundle tradeoff was only mentioned by one reviewer.

The Recommendation

Restructure in three phases. Phase 1 (immediate): Rewrite SKILL.md as a decision-tree index with inline 'Top 10 Bugs' section, one complete copy-pasteable example, and a version-pinning note. Move the Critical Rules to the top. Phase 2 (short-term): Standardize all reference files with consistent structure (Overview, Patterns, Mistakes, Examples). Add timezone and recurring events guidance to events.md. Add accessibility and mobile guidance to views.md and display.md. Phase 3 (medium-term): Add proper eval scenarios that test whether an agent can build, debug, and integrate a calendar. Expand the skill description to include failure-mode triggers. Add migration guidance for v5→v6.

First Step

Rewrite SKILL.md to lead with a decision-tree routing table, inline the top 10 FullCalendar bugs with fixes, and add one complete minimal working example (CDN + JSON feed + event click). This single change would make the skill 10x more effective without touching any reference file. Estimated effort: 2-3 hours.

12 / 26

handoff

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/handoff/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the handoff skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill is dangerously naive about what actually goes wrong during agent handoffs. The entire premise is that a fresh agent will read a markdown file and 'continue the work' — but there's zero guidance on what happens when the handoff is ambiguous, contradictory, or incomplete. The skill assumes the agent writing the handoff is competent and honest about state. What about when the previous agent was wrong about what's 'done'? There's no mechanism for that.

Rule 4 says 'keep it under 500 words' but provides no enforcement or even a soft check. An agent could easily dump 2000 words of context and call it a handoff. The word limit is decorative.

The 'Suggested skills' section in the template is the most valuable part of the entire skill, yet it gets one line in the template and zero guidance on HOW to choose which skills to suggest. Should the next agent load every skill that touches the domain? Only the ones the previous agent loaded? This is left completely unspecified.

The evals are laughably weak. Eval 1 tests whether the agent can follow a template — that's a formatting check, not a behavioral test. Eval 2 tests tailoring, but the 'expected output' is so vague ('tailors the handoff toward payment integration') that any output would pass. There's no test for what happens when there ARE no artifacts to reference, or when the git state is dirty with merge conflicts, or when sensitive data is accidentally included.

The `disable-model-invocation: true` in frontmatter means this skill is invisible to the model during normal operation. That's a critical design flaw — the skill that's supposed to help agents transition between sessions can't be triggered by the model itself. The user has to explicitly invoke it. If the user forgets, no handoff gets written.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the markdown and the rules. What problem are we actually solving? We're solving the context loss problem: when one agent session ends, the next session starts with zero knowledge of what happened before. The handoff document is a serialization format for agent working memory.

Viewed this way, the current skill has a fundamental structural issue: it conflates two different concerns. The first concern is WHAT to capture (goal, state, next steps). The second concern is HOW to capture it (format, length, redaction). These should be separated. The 'Structure' section defines the schema. The 'Rules' section defines quality constraints. But they're interleaved in a way that makes it hard to evolve either independently.

The schema itself is missing critical fields. There's no 'Decisions made' section — the previous agent may have made architectural choices that the next agent needs to know about. There's no 'Known failures' section — tests that are known to be broken, workarounds in place, things that LOOK done but aren't. There's no 'Environment' section — what's installed, what versions, what's configured.

The 'save to temp, not workspace' rule is correct in principle but underspecified. What if the temp directory doesn't exist? What if the OS is Windows and the path conventions differ? The skill should specify a fallback chain: `$TMPDIR` → `/tmp` → `~/.cache/handoffs/` → prompt the user.

The real question is: what's the minimum viable handoff that prevents the next agent from making a catastrophic mistake? That's the bar. Not 'summarize everything' — 'prevent the next agent from destroying work or going in the wrong direction.' The skill should be optimized for that.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is thinking too small. A handoff document is just one form of inter-session communication. The skill should expand to cover a spectrum of handoff types:

1. **Quick handoff** — 3 bullets, under 100 words. For when you're just pausing for lunch.
2. **Standard handoff** — the current template. For normal session transitions.
3. **Deep handoff** — includes environment state, dependency graphs, test results, architectural decisions. For complex multi-session projects.
4. **Delegation handoff** — specifically for handing work to a DIFFERENT agent type (e.g., from a generalist to a specialist). Includes explicit capability requirements.

The skill should also address the REVERSE direction: what should the next agent DO when receiving a handoff? There should be a 'Receiving a handoff' section that tells the incoming agent to verify the claimed git state, check that referenced files exist, and validate that 'done' items are actually done. This turns the handoff from a passive document into a protocol.

Another expansion: the skill should mention integration with the SwampCastle persistent memory system. If the project uses SwampCastle, the handoff should reference stored memories rather than re-summarizing everything. This would make handoffs dramatically more powerful.

Finally, the skill should suggest a naming convention for handoff files that includes the date, project, and session type: `handoff-2026-06-18-oh-my-slop-standard.md`. This makes handoffs discoverable and sortable.

🟡 The Outsider

Reading this skill cold, I have no idea when I'm supposed to use it. The description says 'Use when switching sessions, resuming work later, or delegating to a fresh agent' — but those are three very different scenarios with very different information needs. Switching sessions implies the same user, same project, same context. Delegating to a fresh agent might mean a different person, different expertise level, different tooling. The skill treats them as identical.

The 'When to use' section is buried at the bottom. For a skill that's supposed to be invoked at session end, the trigger conditions should be at the TOP, in the description field, where the model actually looks. The current description is too generic — it doesn't differentiate from a simple 'summarize this conversation' request.

The template uses placeholder text like '<date>' and '<skill-name> — <reason>' but never shows a concrete example. I've never seen a handoff document before. Show me one. A complete, filled-out example would be worth more than all the rules combined.

The 'Redact sensitive information' rule lists 'API keys, passwords, tokens, personally identifiable information' but doesn't mention environment variables, `.env` files, database connection strings, or config files that might contain secrets. An agent summarizing work might casually mention 'we set DATABASE_URL to postgres://user:pass@host/db' and leak credentials.

Also: 'Save to temp, not workspace' — what if the user WANTS it in the workspace? What if they want to version-control the handoff? The skill presents this as absolute, but it should be a default with an override path.

The evals don't test the most common failure mode: the agent writes a handoff that's technically correct but useless. 'Goal: build a web app. Done: started project. Next: continue. State: main branch.' This passes all the structural checks and is completely worthless.

🟠 The Executor

The single highest-impact improvement: add a concrete, filled-out example of a handoff document. Right now the skill shows an empty template with `<date>` placeholders. An agent reading this has never seen what a GOOD handoff looks like. One complete example — with realistic goal text, actual git references, real file paths, genuine next steps — would improve output quality more than any rule change.

Second priority: fix the evals. Eval 1 should provide a specific conversation history and require the agent to produce a handoff that references specific files and commits mentioned in that history. Eval 2 should include a scenario where the conversation contains an API key, and the eval checks that the key is absent from the output. Without these, the evals don't actually validate anything.

Third: move the 'When to use' section above the 'Structure' section. The trigger conditions are the first thing an agent needs to know. Currently they're at the bottom, after the template and rules. That's backwards.

Fourth: add a 'Decisions' field to the template. The most common thing that gets lost between sessions isn't what was done — it's WHY it was done that way. The next agent needs to know about decisions made, alternatives considered, and reasons for the chosen path.

These four changes can be made in under 30 minutes and would materially improve every handoff this skill produces. Everything else is optimization.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor — it correctly identifies that the concrete example is the single highest-leverage fix. The skill shows an empty template but never a filled one, which is like teaching someone to write essays by showing them a blank page. The Executor also correctly calls out that the evals don't test for secret redaction, which is the most dangerous real-world failure mode.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist — proposing four handoff types (quick, standard, deep, delegation) is over-engineering a skill that's already struggling with its basic form. The skill should nail ONE type before branching. The SwampCastle integration idea is good in theory but adds a dependency that not all projects use.

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed the `disable-model-invocation: true` frontmatter field, which means the model can't trigger this skill on its own. This is arguably the biggest structural problem — the skill that's most needed at session end is the one the model is least likely to invoke automatically. Someone needs to question whether this field should be removed or whether the description needs to be aggressive enough to compensate.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker — correctly identifies that the skill conflates schema with quality constraints and that the schema is missing critical fields like 'Decisions made' and 'Known failures.' The insight that the skill should be optimized for 'preventing the next agent from destroying work' rather than 'summarizing everything' reframes the entire problem correctly.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian — while the criticism of the evals is valid, the claim that `disable-model-invocation: true` is a 'critical design flaw' is wrong. That field exists to prevent the model from invoking handoff generation as a tool call during normal conversation, which would be disruptive. The skill is meant to be user-invoked. The Contrarian misunderstands the field's purpose.

What All Missed

No one addressed the fact that the skill has no guidance on what to do when the handoff ITSELF is the problem — when the previous agent's handoff is wrong, misleading, or missing. There should be guidance for the receiving agent on how to validate the handoff's claims before trusting them.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider — the observation that the skill never shows a concrete example is devastating and correct. The outsider perspective also catches the most practical issue: the 'save to temp, not workspace' rule is presented as absolute when users might legitimately want version-controlled handoffs. The point about secret redaction missing environment variables and connection strings is a genuine security gap.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist — the naming convention suggestion (`handoff-2026-06-18-oh-my-slop-standard.md`) adds complexity with minimal benefit. The skill already says to save to temp; file naming in temp directories is rarely important. This is the kind of over-engineering that makes skills harder to follow without improving outcomes.

What All Missed

All advisors focused on the handoff document itself but none addressed the TIMING of when the handoff is written. Should it be written at the very end of the session, potentially rushed? Should the agent signal 5 minutes before session end that a handoff is needed? The skill is silent on when during the session the handoff should be produced.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Executor — the four concrete, prioritized improvements (add example, fix evals, reorder sections, add Decisions field) are exactly right. The Executor is the only advisor who gives a clear sequencing of what to do first, second, third. The call to add secret-redaction testing to evals is the most important eval improvement anyone suggested.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker — while the 'minimum viable handoff' framing is intellectually appealing, the suggestion to add 'Environment' and 'Known failures' fields risks bloating the template beyond the 500-word limit the skill already struggles to enforce. More fields ≠ better handoff. The skill needs fewer, better-utilized fields, not more.

What All Missed

No one discussed what happens when there are MULTIPLE handoffs — a project that spans several sessions. Should each handoff reference the previous one? Should there be a 'handoff chain'? The skill assumes a single handoff between two sessions, but real projects often involve many transitions.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Contrarian — the critique of the evals is the most technically precise of all advisors. Eval 1 tests template-following, not behavior. Eval 2's expected output is so vague that any response would pass. These are real, measurable quality problems. The Contrarian is also right that the 500-word limit is decorative without any enforcement mechanism.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider — while the request for a concrete example is valid, the Outsider's criticism that the three use cases (switching sessions, resuming later, delegating) are 'very different scenarios' overstates the difference. At the information level, all three need the same core data: what's done, what's next, what's the state. The template handles all three adequately. Splitting them would add complexity without benefit.

What All Missed

None of the advisors considered the failure mode where the agent writes the handoff to temp but the temp directory gets cleaned before the next session starts. There's no guidance on what to do if the handoff file is lost, no suggestion to also provide a copy to the user verbally, and no fallback strategy. The skill assumes the handoff will survive, which is not guaranteed.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council overwhelmingly agrees on three points: (1) the skill needs a concrete, filled-out example of a handoff document — the empty template is insufficient, (2) the evals are weak and need to test actual behavior including secret redaction, and (3) the template is missing a 'Decisions made' or 'Why' section, which is the most common thing lost between sessions. There is also broad agreement that the 'When to use' section should be moved higher in the document.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether `disable-model-invocation: true` is a flaw (Contrarian) or intentional design (Reviewer 2). The council also clashes on whether to expand the skill with multiple handoff types (Expansionist) or keep it focused on nailing one form (Contrarian, Reviewer 1). The Expansionist's proposal for four handoff types is the most divisive idea — half the reviewers see it as over-engineering, half see it as the natural evolution of the skill.

Blind Spots Caught

The entire council missed two critical issues: (1) no guidance for the RECEIVING agent on how to validate a handoff's claims before trusting them, and (2) no discussion of handoff chains for multi-session projects. Additionally, no one addressed the `disable-model-invocation: true` field's implications for discoverability — if the model can't invoke it, the description must be extremely precise to ensure the user triggers it at the right time.

The Recommendation

Make four changes in priority order: (1) Add a complete, realistic example handoff document — this alone will improve output quality more than any other change. (2) Add a 'Decisions' section to the template explaining key choices and their rationale. (3) Rewrite the evals to test actual behavior: provide a conversation history with an API key and verify it's redacted; provide a specific git state and verify it's accurately reflected. (4) Move 'When to use' above 'Structure' and make the description more specific about the three trigger scenarios. Defer the Expansionist's multi-type proposal — get the base form right first.

First Step

Add a concrete example handoff document to the skill. Take a realistic scenario — a Django project mid-feature, with specific commits, files, and next steps — and show what a good handoff looks like when fully populated. This is a 10-minute change that will have more impact than any rule modification.

13 / 26

htmx

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/htmx/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the HTMX skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a serious identity crisis. It claims to be a 'skill' but is really just a table of contents for reference docs. The SKILL.md itself contains zero executable knowledge — it's a signpost saying 'go read the real docs elsewhere.' That's not a skill, it's a bookmark.

The 'Critical Rules' section is the only part that actually teaches anything, and it's riddled with half-truths. Rule 2 says 'most attributes inherit to children' but doesn't explain what 'most' means or why that matters in practice. The non-inherited list is useful but buried without context — when would I actually hit this? Give me the failure mode, not the spec.

Rule 4 ('Form values auto-included') is misleading. It says 'non-GET requests automatically include the closest enclosing form's values' — but what about elements outside forms? What about `hx-include`? This creates a false sense of understanding that will bite someone at 2 AM.

The 'Development Constraints' section is opinionated to the point of being wrong. 'JavaScript requires explicit justification' — fine in principle, but the legitimate cases list is arbitrary. What about Canvas? WebRTC? File API? The list is a random sampling, not a principled boundary.

The 'Common Pitfalls' section has exactly two items. Two. For a library with this surface area? Where's the pitfall about `hx-boost` breaking script initialization? Where's the OOB swap confusion? Where's the indicator class race condition? These are in the gotchas reference, but the SKILL.md should surface the top 5-7 pitfalls that actually burn people.

The description trigger is also too narrow: 'hx-* attributes, HTMX AJAX requests, swap strategies, server-sent events, WebSockets, or hypermedia-driven UIs.' It misses the most common real-world trigger: 'I need to make this page dynamic without a SPA framework.' Nobody thinks 'I need HTMX' — they think 'I need this button to fetch something without a full reload.'

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the structure and ask: what problem does this skill actually solve? It exists so that when an agent encounters an HTMX task, it knows where to look and what rules to follow. That's it. So let's evaluate it against that purpose.

The core job is routing: given a task, find the right reference file. The Task Routing section does this adequately. But routing assumes the agent already knows it's in HTMX territory. The real failure mode is the agent NOT loading this skill because the task description didn't match the trigger. The description needs to cover the intent-space, not the keyword-space.

Now, what's the actual knowledge an agent needs that it can't get from the HTMX docs directly? Three things: (1) project-specific conventions (the HTML-first preference order, the JS justification rule), (2) the curated 'gotchas' that the team has learned, and (3) the reference map for quick lookup. The skill does #1 and #3 well. #2 is critically underdeveloped — two pitfalls is a joke.

The 'Critical Rules' section is trying to be a contract but reads like a spec summary. A contract would say: 'The server MUST return HTML fragments. The agent MUST verify the HX-Request header is handled. The agent MUST NOT use JSON responses unless explicitly asked.' Instead we get descriptive statements that don't bind behavior.

The reference architecture is actually good — 7 focused files is the right granularity. But the SKILL.md doesn't tell the agent WHEN to read multiple references vs. one. A task like 'build infinite scroll with error handling' touches patterns, requests, events, AND gotchas. The agent needs to know it should read multiple files.

Bottom line: this skill is a good index but a poor teacher. It needs to shift from describing HTMX to prescribing how to use HTMX correctly in this project's context.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is leaving enormous value on the table by being purely reactive and reference-oriented. Here's what's hiding in plain sight:

First, the skill should include a 'decision tree' for when HTMX is the right tool vs. when it's not. Right now it assumes HTMX is always the answer. A simple flowchart — 'Is this a page-level navigation? → hx-boost. Is this a small DOM update? → hx-get/hx-post. Is this real-time bidirectional? → consider WS extension or bail to vanilla JS. Is this a complex client-side state machine? → HTMX is wrong tool' — would prevent more bugs than any reference doc.

Second, there's no testing guidance. HTMX has specific testing patterns — how do you write Playwright tests for hx-trigger='changed'? How do you test OOB swaps? How do you mock HTMX requests in unit tests? This is a massive gap. The skill is telling agents to build HTMX features but giving them zero guidance on how to verify those features work.

Third, the skill should include a 'pattern selection' guide. The patterns reference has 773 lines of examples, but the SKILL.md doesn't help the agent choose. A simple table — 'If you need X, use pattern Y from patterns.md' — would dramatically reduce the time from task to implementation.

Fourth, there's no migration guidance. Teams don't adopt HTMX in greenfield projects — they add it to existing jQuery/Vue/React codebases. A section on incremental adoption patterns (progressive enhancement of existing forms, replacing jQuery $.ajax calls one at a time) would make this skill useful for the most common real-world scenario.

Fifth, the skill should reference the Hyperscript relationship more explicitly. The Development Constraints mention Hyperscript as step 3 in the tool preference, but there's no guidance on where HTMX ends and Hyperscript begins. A clear boundary definition with examples would prevent the common anti-pattern of writing Hyperscript when plain HTMX attributes would suffice, and vice versa.

Finally, version targeting: it says 'HTMX 2.x' but 2.x has breaking changes from 1.x. The skill should call out the specific 2.x changes that matter (e.g., changed default swap behavior, new attribute names) so agents working on upgraded codebases don't use 1.x patterns.

🟡 The Outsider

I just read this skill and I'm confused about several things that I suspect the authors consider 'obvious':

What is the relationship between this skill and the reference files? The SKILL.md says 'Read only the reference file(s) needed for the task' — but how do I know which files I need? The Task Routing section helps, but it's organized by HTMX concept, not by the kind of task I'm actually trying to do. If I'm told 'add a live search to the navbar,' I don't think 'I need the requests reference.' I think 'I need to know how to do live search.' The routing should be task-oriented, not concept-oriented.

What does 'scope: htmx' mean? Is this a pi skill scope? A project scope? It's in the frontmatter but never explained. Same with 'source_basis: official docs' — does this mean the reference files are copied from official docs? Summarized? Augmented with project-specific additions? If I find a discrepancy between the HTMX official docs and this skill, which do I trust?

The 'Quick Start' says 'Validate that server responses return HTML fragments, not JSON' — but it doesn't say HOW to validate this. Do I check the response content-type? Do I inspect the network tab? Do I write a test? For a skill that's supposed to guide implementation, this is hand-waving.

The 'CSS lifecycle classes' rule says 'HTMX adds/removes CSS classes during requests — use for transitions and indicators' but doesn't name the classes. I had to go read the attributes reference to find out they're `htmx-request`, `htmx-swapping`, `htmx-settling`, etc. The SKILL.md should list the actual class names — this is exactly the kind of thing an agent needs at the top level.

The 'data-prefix supported' rule is buried as rule 8. This is actually important for teams that use HTML validators — it should be more prominent, maybe even in Quick Start.

The frontmatter description is the trigger for loading this skill. It lists 'hx-* attributes, HTMX AJAX requests, swap strategies, server-sent events, WebSockets, or hypermedia-driven UIs.' But what if someone says 'make this form submit without reloading the page'? That's the most common HTMX use case and it doesn't match any of those phrases. The trigger should include intent-based phrases like 'partial page update,' 'dynamic content without reload,' 'AJAX form submission,' etc.

🟠 The Executor

Forget philosophy. Here's what's broken and what to fix first, ordered by impact:

**Fix 1: Expand Common Pitfalls from 2 to at least 7 items.** Two pitfalls for a library this complex is negligent. The gotchas reference has 419 lines — distill the top 5-7 into the SKILL.md. The highest-impact ones: HX-Request header detection, OOB swap gotchas, hx-boost breaking scripts, indicator class timing, hx-trigger event confusion, and the inheritance surprise. This is the single highest-ROI change.

**Fix 2: Add a 'Testing HTMX' section.** Even 3-4 lines would transform this skill. Cover: (a) Playwright can intercept HTMX requests via `page.wait_for_response()`, (b) test OOB swaps by checking the target element independently, (c) use `hx-trigger='changed'` with `page.fill()` not `page.type()` for reliable test behavior. This is the most requested HTMX dev topic and it's completely absent.

**Fix 3: Rewrite the description trigger.** Current trigger misses the most common real-world phrases. Add: 'partial page update,' 'AJAX form submission,' 'dynamic content without reload,' 'progressive enhancement,' 'no-JS-framework dynamic UI.' This alone will increase skill activation rate by an estimated 30-40%.

**Fix 4: Add concrete CSS class names to the lifecycle rule.** Rule 8 says 'CSS lifecycle classes' but doesn't name them. Add: `htmx-request` (on the element or body during request), `htmx-swapping` (during swap), `htmx-settling` (after swap). This takes 10 seconds to add and prevents a lookup.

**Fix 5: Add a 'When NOT to use HTMX' callout.** One short section listing cases where HTMX is the wrong tool: complex client-side state, real-time bidirectional communication (use WS directly), file uploads with progress (use native Fetch + FormData), and pages that need SEO without server rendering. This prevents more anti-patterns than any positive guidance.

Do these five things and the skill goes from 'adequate index' to 'actually useful.' Everything else is nice-to-have.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

Response E (The Executor) is strongest because it's immediately actionable. Every item is concrete, scoped, and ordered by impact. 'Expand pitfalls from 2 to 7' is the kind of specific, measurable improvement that can be done in one session. The testing section addition addresses a real gap that every HTMX developer hits.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response C (The Expansionist) has the biggest blind spot. The migration guidance and decision tree ideas are valuable but represent a massive scope expansion. Turning this skill into a decision tree + migration guide + pattern selector + testing guide would triple its size and turn a focused reference into a book. Not everything valuable belongs in the SKILL.md — some of these belong in the reference files.

What All Missed

All responses missed the accessibility dimension. HTMX has significant a11y implications — dynamic content updates need `aria-live` regions, focus management after swaps, and proper announcement of loading states. None of the five advisors mentioned this, and the SKILL.md is completely silent on it. This is a serious omission for a skill that guides UI implementation.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

Response D (The Outsider) is strongest because it identifies the most fundamental structural problem: the Task Routing section is organized by library concept instead of developer intent. 'Adding HTMX behavior to elements' is not how developers think — they think 'I want to make this form submit inline.' Reorganizing routing by task type (forms, navigation, search, modals, validation) would make the entire skill more usable.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response A (The Contrarian) has the biggest blind spot. Dismissing the skill as 'just a bookmark' ignores that this is exactly what a reference skill should be. Not every skill needs to be a tutorial. The index pattern is valid — the problem is the index quality, not the index concept. The Contrarian throws out the baby with the bathwater.

What All Missed

All responses missed that the skill has no guidance on HTMX + Django integration specifics, which is the primary stack this repo targets (evidenced by the django and django-htmx-tabler-knowledge skills). There's no mention of Django template partials, Django's CSRF token handling with HTMX, or the `HX-Request` header pattern in Django views. This is a glaring omission for a skill in a Django-centric project.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The distinction between descriptive rules ('HTMX expects HTML responses') and prescriptive contracts ('The server MUST return HTML fragments') is the difference between documentation that's read and documentation that's followed. This reframing would improve every section.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response E (The Executor) has the biggest blind spot. Its pragmatism is valuable but it's purely incremental. Fixing pitfalls count and adding CSS class names won't address the structural issues — the skill will still be a concept-organized index with no task-oriented routing, no a11y guidance, and no Django integration. The Executor optimizes the local maximum without questioning the global one.

What All Missed

All responses missed the version targeting problem. The skill says 'HTMX 2.x' but doesn't specify which 2.x minor, and HTMX 2.0 had breaking changes from 2.0.0-rc versions. The `last_verified` date is March 2026 — is this against 2.0.4? 2.1.0? An agent working on a project pinned to a specific minor version needs to know if the skill's guidance applies to their version. This is especially critical for the extensions reference, which changes between minor versions.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

Response A (The Contrarian) is strongest because it's the only one that challenges the fundamental design. The 'two pitfalls' criticism is devastating and correct. The JavaScript justification list being arbitrary is a real problem — it will either be ignored (too vague) or followed blindly (too rigid). And the trigger description criticism is the most important structural fix needed. A skill that doesn't load is a skill that doesn't exist.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response C (The Expansionist) has the biggest blind spot. Adding migration guidance, decision trees, and pattern selectors would bloat the SKILL.md beyond its purpose. The SKILL.md should be the concise entry point — these expansions belong in the reference files (patterns.md, gotchas.md) or in a new reference file. The Expansionist doesn't respect the one-page constraint of a good SKILL.md.

What All Missed

All responses missed the error handling gap. The skill has no guidance on what happens when HTMX requests fail. How should the agent handle 4xx/5xx responses? Should it use `hx-target` to swap error messages into a specific element? Should it use the `htmx:responseError` event? What about network failures? This is critical for production-quality HTMX implementations and it's completely absent from both the SKILL.md and (based on the reference file names) likely underdeveloped in the references too.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

Response D (The Outsider) is strongest because it catches the 'curse of knowledge' problems that insiders always miss. The 'scope: htmx' confusion, the unexplained 'source_basis,' the unnamed CSS classes — these are exactly the things that make a skill unusable by an agent that doesn't already know HTMX. The Outsider's confusion is a feature, not a bug: it reveals where the skill assumes knowledge it shouldn't.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response B (The First Principles Thinker) has the biggest blind spot. Its prescription to make rules into contracts ('MUST return HTML fragments') sounds good but would make the skill read like a legal document. Skills need to be scannable and practical. Prescriptive contracts work for linting rules, not for guidance documents. The First Principles Thinker optimizes for correctness at the cost of usability.

What All Missed

All responses missed the indicator/loading state guidance gap. The CSS lifecycle classes rule mentions transitions and indicators but gives no practical pattern. How do you show a spinner on the specific button that was clicked? How do you disable the button during request? How do you handle multiple concurrent requests on the same page? This is one of the most common HTMX implementation tasks and the skill provides zero guidance. The patterns reference likely covers it, but the SKILL.md should at least point to the specific pattern.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees on three things: (1) the Common Pitfalls section is critically underdeveloped at only two items and must be expanded, (2) the description trigger is too narrow and misses intent-based phrases that would cause the skill to load for the most common HTMX tasks, and (3) the skill provides zero testing guidance, which is a major gap for a skill that guides UI implementation. There is also strong consensus (4 of 5) that the CSS lifecycle rule should name the actual CSS classes (`htmx-request`, `htmx-swapping`, `htmx-settling`) inline rather than forcing a reference lookup.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether the skill's index/reference architecture is fundamentally sound or fundamentally broken. The Contrarian and First Principles Thinker argue the SKILL.md should contain more prescriptive, contract-like knowledge rather than being a signpost. The Executor and Outsider accept the index model but want it executed better. The Expansionist wants to dramatically expand scope while Reviewer 4 argues the SKILL.md must stay concise and expansions belong in reference files. There's also tension between the Executor's incremental pragmatism ('fix these 5 things now') and the structural rethinking proposed by the First Principles Thinker and Outsider.

Blind Spots Caught

The entire council missed three significant gaps: (1) accessibility — no mention of `aria-live` regions, focus management, or screen reader announcements for dynamic content, (2) Django integration specifics — despite this being a Django-centric project, there's no guidance on Django template partials, CSRF handling with HTMX, or the `HX-Request` header pattern in Django views, and (3) error handling — no guidance on handling failed HTMX requests, network errors, or user-facing error display patterns. These are not minor omissions; they represent the difference between a toy implementation and a production-quality one.

The Recommendation

Execute a two-phase improvement. Phase 1 (immediate, high-impact): Expand Common Pitfalls to 7-8 items distilled from gotchas.md. Rewrite the description trigger to include intent-based phrases. Add CSS class names to the lifecycle rule. Add a brief Testing HTMX section (3-4 lines pointing to specific Playwright patterns). Add a 'When NOT to use HTMX' callout. Phase 2 (structural): Reorganize Task Routing by task type instead of library concept. Add a Django integration subsection. Add an accessibility subsection covering `aria-live` and focus management. Add error handling guidance. Consider adding a new reference file for testing patterns if the content exceeds what fits in the SKILL.md.

First Step

Expand the Common Pitfalls section from 2 to 7-8 items by distilling the highest-impact gotchas from gotchas.md. This is the single highest-ROI change — it takes one reference read, addresses the most universal criticism from all five advisors, and transforms the weakest section of the SKILL.md into one of the strongest. Everything else builds on this foundation.
