14 / 26

http-status-codes

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/http-status-codes/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the http-status-codes skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a serious structural problem: it's a reference manual pretending to be a decision-making tool. The SKILL.md frontmatter description is a wall of specific code pairs that reads like a keyword-stuffed trigger list rather than a clear statement of purpose. An agent scanning available skills will either miss it (because the description is overwhelming) or trigger it for any HTTP-adjacent task regardless of whether status code selection is the actual need.

The 'Critical Rules' section in SKILL.md duplicates what the reference files already say. Rule 2 (401 vs 403) is repeated almost verbatim in decision-rules.md and client-errors.md. Rule 3 (400 vs 422) appears in three places. This isn't reinforcement — it's bloat that makes maintenance harder and creates drift risk.

The evals are the weakest part. Eval 1 is a laundry-list exercise that rewards pattern matching, not understanding. It asks for 13 mappings in one shot — any agent that parrots the reference files will pass without demonstrating judgment. Eval 4 is better but still mechanical. None of the evals test whether the agent can handle genuinely ambiguous scenarios where the 'right' code depends on API design philosophy, not RFC quoting.

The skill also has a blind spot: it never addresses the 207 Multi-Status code, which is critical for batch/bulk API operations — a pattern that's increasingly common. The rare-and-nonstandard file mentions WebDAV codes but skips 207 entirely. That's a real gap.

Finally, the 'Content Ownership' section at the bottom of SKILL.md is defensive and negative — four 'does not own' items and only three 'owns' items. This reads like scope-lawyering, not guidance. An agent doesn't need to know what the skill doesn't own; it needs to know what to do.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the structure and ask: what problem does this skill actually solve? The answer is: an agent is writing or reviewing HTTP API code and picks the wrong status code because it defaults to 400 for everything and 500 for all server failures. That's the core failure mode.

So the skill needs to answer exactly three questions: (1) What code should I return? (2) What headers must accompany it? (3) What should the client do? Everything else is supporting material.

Currently, the skill buries the most important insight — that 400 is a fallback, not a default — in the Critical Rules section, but the real teaching happens in the decision-rules.md file. The SKILL.md should be a thin router that gets the agent to the right reference file in under 10 seconds. Instead, it's 10 rules, a reference map, a task routing table, and a content ownership disclaimer. That's too much cognitive overhead for what should be a quick lookup.

The reference files themselves are well-organized around the three-question framework (code → headers → client action), but they're inconsistent. decision-rules.md uses comparison tables brilliantly. client-errors.md uses narrative prose with examples. server-errors.md uses a retry matrix. rare-and-nonstandard.md is a flat table. The agent gets a different mental model depending on which file it reads. Pick one pattern and use it everywhere.

The evals test recall, not reasoning. A truly effective eval would present a scenario where two codes are defensible and ask the agent to argue for one while acknowledging the tradeoff. Or present a real-world API with mixed conventions and ask the agent to identify inconsistencies and propose a coherent policy. The current evals are answerable by copy-pasting from the reference files.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is solid on individual code semantics but misses the bigger picture of how status codes operate in real API ecosystems. Here's what could make it genuinely powerful:

First, add an API design patterns section. Real-world API work isn't about individual endpoints — it's about consistency across a surface. How do you handle status codes in a paginated collection? What about batch operations where some items succeed and some fail (207 Multi-Status)? What about long-running operations with polling (202 + 200 vs 202 + 303)? What about webhook delivery status vs HTTP response status? These are the scenarios where agents actually need guidance, and they're completely absent.

Second, add a 'status code versioning' guide. When you evolve an API from v1 to v2, you sometimes need to change the status codes returned by existing endpoints. How do you communicate that? When is it a breaking change? When can you tighten from 400 to 422 without breaking clients? This is a real concern that the skill's brief mention of 'compatibility' in decision-rules.md doesn't adequately cover.

Third, the skill should address the OpenAPI/Swagger dimension. Many agents work with OpenAPI specs where status codes are declared as part of the schema. A section on how status codes map to OpenAPI response definitions — and how to document them with the right response schemas — would bridge the gap between HTTP semantics and API specification work.

Fourth, the skill could own client-side error handling patterns more assertively. The evals touch on this (Eval 3), but the reference files are entirely server-facing. A developer reading this skill to understand what 401 means would also benefit from knowing: 'your client should attempt a token refresh, then retry once, then surface a login prompt.' That end-to-end view would make the skill indispensable rather than merely correct.

🟡 The Outsider

I have no context about this project. Reading the SKILL.md cold, here's what confuses me:

The description mentions '400 vs 422, 401 vs 403, 404 vs 410' — but I don't know what these numbers mean yet. The description assumes I already know HTTP status codes well enough to recognize that these pairs are confusing. If I'm an agent that doesn't know HTTP codes, this description doesn't tell me what the skill does. It tells me what it distinguishes. That's a subtle but important difference.

The 'Quick Start' section says 'start with decision-rules.md' for choosing codes, but then the 'Task Routing' section below it has a different, more detailed routing table. Why are there two routing systems? Which one do I follow? The Quick Start says to read the 'smallest reference file needed' but the Task Routing table sends me to REFERENCE.md if I'm 'unsure where to start' — which is exactly the state I'm in when I read the Quick Start.

The examples in the reference files are good but inconsistent. decision-rules.md has a nice table format with 'Use it when', 'Real-world example', and 'What the client should do' columns. But client-errors.md uses a flatter structure with prose examples. When I read about 401 in decision-rules.md, I get a table row. When I read about 401 in client-errors.md, I get a paragraph. The information overlaps but isn't presented the same way. This makes me wonder which file is authoritative.

The 'Content Ownership' section says the skill doesn't own 'framework-specific exception wiring or middleware setup.' But if I'm working in Django REST Framework and I want to know which exception class maps to which status code, that's exactly what I need. The skill tells me what code to use but not how to implement it in my framework. That's fine, but it should point me to the right skill (like 'drf') instead of just saying 'not my problem.'

The evals.json file has an interesting structure with per-evaluation expectations, but I notice none of the evals test what happens when the agent encounters a code the skill doesn't cover. There's no 'what would you do with a 418 response?' or 'how do you handle a 207 in a batch API?' — the skill's gaps aren't tested.

🟠 The Executor

The highest-impact improvement is fixing the SKILL.md description. It's currently 137 words with a comma-separated list of code pairs that looks like a search engine optimization exercise. Cut it to 40 words that clearly state when to use this skill. The specific code pairs should live in the reference files, not the description. A clean description would be: 'Use when choosing, reviewing, or explaining HTTP response status codes for APIs, SDKs, or client error handling. Covers code selection, required headers, and client retry semantics.'

Second priority: consolidate the routing. Remove either the Quick Start or the Task Routing table — having both is redundant and confusing. The Task Routing table is more detailed, so keep that and drop Quick Start.

Third: add 207 Multi-Status to client-errors.md. It's the most commonly needed code that's missing. Bulk APIs are everywhere, and agents will encounter 207 in the wild. One paragraph, one example, done.

Fourth: rewrite Eval 1. It's too long and tests too many things at once. Split it into two evals: one for basic code selection (malformed JSON, missing token, validation error) and one for the trickier distinctions (409 vs 412 vs 428, 429 vs 503). This gives better signal on where the agent actually struggles.

Fifth: add a 'Common Mistakes' callout box in SKILL.md with the top 3 anti-patterns: (1) returning 200 with an error body, (2) using 400 as a catch-all, (3) returning 500 for upstream failures. These are the highest-frequency real-world errors and they should be immediately visible, not buried in the Red Flags section of decision-rules.md.

Everything else — OpenAPI mapping, versioning guidance, client SDK patterns — is scope creep for a reference skill. Get the core right first.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor (E) cuts through the noise. The description bloat, the dual routing, the missing 207 — these are concrete, fixable problems with clear priority ordering. The suggestion to add a 'Common Mistakes' callout box is the single highest-leverage change: it puts the most important anti-patterns where agents will actually see them.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist (C) has the most ambitious vision but the weakest grounding. Suggesting OpenAPI mapping, API versioning, and end-to-end client handling patterns would triple the skill's scope. The skill is a reference tool, not an API design guide. These suggestions belong in a separate skill or an extension.

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed the inconsistency in example formatting across reference files. decision-rules.md uses structured tables; client-errors.md uses prose with HTTP examples; server-errors.md uses a retry matrix; rare-and-nonstandard.md uses a flat table. An agent reading different files gets different presentation patterns, which increases cognitive load. The skill should standardize on one format — ideally the table format from decision-rules.md, which is the most scannable.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker (B) correctly identifies that the skill's core job is answering three questions: what code, what headers, what should the client do. This framework exposes the real structural problem — the reference files use different organizational patterns, so the agent can't build a consistent mental model. Standardizing around the three-question framework would improve every reference file.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian (A) is right about eval quality but wrong about the Content Ownership section. Telling agents what a skill doesn't own is genuinely useful — it prevents the agent from asking this skill to configure Django middleware or design project-specific compatibility policies. The negative framing is fine; agents need boundaries, not just capabilities.

What All Missed

No one mentioned that the skill has no guidance on status codes for streaming responses (like SSE or chunked transfer). 100 Continue is mentioned in rare-and-nonstandard.md, but the broader question of 'what status code does a streaming endpoint return if the stream fails mid-flight?' is unaddressed. This is increasingly relevant for AI/LLM APIs.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Executor (E) again — the five-step improvement plan is actionable and correctly prioritized. The suggestion to split Eval 1 into two focused evals is particularly good. The current Eval 1 is a kitchen-sink that doesn't discriminate between an agent that understands the basics and one that only handles the easy cases.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider (D) raises valid UX concerns but overweights the 'cold read' problem. Skills aren't meant to be read cold by agents unfamiliar with the domain — they're triggered by task context. The description's code pairs are trigger keywords, not educational content. That said, the dual routing (Quick Start + Task Routing) is genuinely confusing and should be fixed.

What All Missed

None of the advisors questioned whether the reference file structure is optimal. There are 6 files (5 references + 1 index) for what is fundamentally a lookup task. Could the content be consolidated into 3 files without losing the 'read only what you need' benefit? Fewer files means less navigation overhead and less risk of cross-file inconsistency.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker (B) and The Executor (E) together identify the two most critical issues: inconsistent reference file structure and bloated SKILL.md. If I had to pick one, I'd go with B's insight about the three-question framework because it's the diagnostic that leads to the prescription. Once you see that every reference file should answer 'what code, what headers, what should the client do,' the restructuring becomes obvious.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist (C) suggests adding client-side error handling patterns 'more assertively' but the skill already does this — every decision-rules table has a 'What the client should do' column, and Eval 3 tests exactly this. The skill is already more client-aware than C gives it credit for. The gap isn't client-side guidance; it's that the guidance is scattered across files instead of consolidated.

What All Missed

No one addressed the skill's handling of content negotiation failures. 406 Not Acceptable is mentioned briefly in client-errors.md's 'Advanced' table, but there's no guidance on when to use 406 vs just returning JSON regardless of the Accept header (which is what most real APIs do). This is a common design decision point that agents face.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Contrarian (A) delivers the harshest and most useful critique: the evals test pattern matching, not judgment. Eval 1 is especially guilty — it's a straight lookup exercise. The suggestion to test ambiguous scenarios where two codes are defensible would dramatically improve eval quality. An agent that can argue for 409 vs 422 for a duplicate entry, with tradeoffs, understands HTTP semantics better than one that memorizes the 'correct' answer.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor (E) is too conservative. Suggesting that OpenAPI mapping and client SDK patterns are 'scope creep' ignores that this skill is already doing more than pure reference — it includes client guidance, retry matrices, and compatibility notes. Adding a 'Related Skills' section pointing to drf, litestar, or webapp-testing would cost two lines and prevent agents from operating in a vacuum.

What All Missed

None of the advisors mentioned that the skill doesn't address HEAD requests and their status code implications. A HEAD request should return the same status code as GET but without a body — this trips up agents who design separate HEAD handlers. A single note in success-redirects.md would suffice.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council broadly agrees on three points: (1) The SKILL.md description is bloated and should be trimmed to a clear, concise purpose statement. (2) The dual routing system (Quick Start + Task Routing) is redundant and confusing — consolidate into one. (3) The evals need improvement — Eval 1 is too broad and tests recall over reasoning. There's also agreement that 207 Multi-Status is a notable omission from the reference files.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on scope. The Expansionist wants to add OpenAPI mapping, API versioning, versioning guidance, and end-to-end client SDK patterns. The Executor calls this scope creep and wants to keep the skill focused on code selection. The Contrarian and Outsider disagree on whether the Content Ownership section is useful boundary-setting or defensive scope-lawyering. The First Principles Thinker and Executor disagree on whether the reference file count should be reduced — B implies consolidation would help, while E's fixes assume the current file structure.

Blind Spots Caught

The entire council missed three things: (1) inconsistent presentation format across reference files (tables vs prose vs matrices), which forces agents to re-learn the structure for each file; (2) no mention of HEAD request semantics or streaming response status codes; (3) no guidance on content negotiation decisions (406 vs ignoring Accept header), which is a real design choice agents face. Reviewer 1 caught the format inconsistency, and Reviewer 2 caught the streaming gap, but no advisor raised these.

The Recommendation

Execute the Executor's five-step plan as the immediate improvement: trim the description, consolidate routing, add 207, split Eval 1, and add a Common Mistakes callout. Then apply the First Principles Thinker's three-question framework to standardize all reference files into a consistent table format (code → when to use → required headers → client action). Finally, replace Eval 1 with two focused evals and add one new eval that tests ambiguous scenarios where multiple codes are defensible. Defer OpenAPI mapping, versioning, and SDK patterns to a future skill or a 'Related Skills' section.

First Step

Rewrite the SKILL.md description to a concise 40-word purpose statement, remove the Quick Start section (keep Task Routing), and add a 'Common Mistakes' callout box with the top 3 anti-patterns. This is a 30-minute change that improves every agent interaction with the skill.

15 / 26

hyperscript

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/hyperscript/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the hyperscript skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a fundamental structural problem: it's a reference document pretending to be a skill. The SKILL.md itself is 90% routing table and 10% actual guidance. The 'Critical Rules' section is the only genuinely valuable part because it tells you what TO DO and what NOT TO do. Everything else is 'go read this other file' — which is fine for a knowledge base but mediocre for a skill.

The 'When NOT to Use Hyperscript' section is weak. Rule 3 says 'if the solution would be mostly js ... end blocks, skip hyperscript' — but where's the threshold? 50%? 80%? An agent will hit a case that's 40% JS interop and have no idea what to do. Give concrete heuristics or an example.

The setup essentials section burbs the `_hyperscript.browserInit()` call inside a build/import subsection. That's a critical step that deserves its own callout for npm/bundler users, because forgetting it is the #1 gotcha.

The HTMX Companion section is the best part of this skill but it's too short. The single example `on htmx:beforeRequest add @disabled then on htmx:afterRequest remove @disabled` is the most common pattern — and it's buried in the middle of a paragraph. This should be prominent, with at least 3 more real-world HTMX+hyperscript patterns.

The description trigger says 'whenever the user is building, debugging, or reviewing hyperscript code' but the skill itself barely covers debugging at all. There's zero troubleshooting guidance. What are the common errors? What does a parse failure look like? How do you debug a handler that fires but does the wrong thing? This is a glaring omission.

Finally, the `target_versions` field says 0.9.x but `last_verified` is April 2026. The version pinning in the CDN example is 0.9.14. What if 0.9.15 changes something? The skill should advise checking the current version rather than hardcoding a specific one, or at minimum explain when to update the pinned version.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

What is hyperscript actually for? It's an event-driven, element-scoped scripting language that lets you write behavioral logic directly on DOM nodes without a build step. That's the core value proposition. Every structural decision in this skill should flow from that.

Currently the skill organizes information by technical category (syntax, DOM, events, advanced). But the user's mental model isn't organized that way — it's organized by task: 'I want this button to do X when clicked,' 'I want to show a loading state,' 'I want to validate this form.' The reference map at the bottom is accurate but it's a lookup table, not a task guide.

The Quick Start section attempts task orientation but collapses into 'identify the domain → open reference file → implement → validate.' That's a generic process, not hyperscript-specific guidance. What would actually help: start with the most common hyperscript tasks users actually face, and for each one, show the minimal pattern.

The Critical Rules section is closest to first-prices thinking because it captures invariants — things that are always true regardless of the specific task. These rules are load-bearing. Rule about math parenthesization alone will save hours of debugging. Rule about attribute storage being string-based is equally important. But Rule 5 about JS interop is mushy: 'use js ... end for cases where hyperscript genuinely cannot express the logic.' That's circular. What CAN'T hyperscript express? Tell me concretely: complex algorithmic logic, external library calls, anything requiring precise type coercion beyond the built-in conversions.

The reference files are treated as opaque black boxes in the SKILL.md. The skill should extract the single most important gotcha from each reference file and surface it inline. If I'm looking at dom-and-commands.md, what's the one thing that will bite me? The skill should tell me upfront.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill massively undersells hyperscript by framing it as 'event handlers and DOM glue.' That's the minimum viable pitch, but hyperscript can do much more — and the skill should showcase that power.

First, behaviors are barely covered. The `behavior` keyword is one of hyperscript's most distinctive features: reusable, named behavioral mixins that you install on elements. This is a powerful abstraction that no other front-end tool offers in quite the same way. The SKILL.md mentions behaviors exactly once in Critical Rules #9 and once in the reference map. There should be a dedicated section showing how to define and install behaviors, with at least two examples: a simple one (auto-focusing an input) and a composable one (a toggle group).

Second, the `worker` feature is mentioned only as a footnote about separate bundles. Web workers are genuinely useful for offloading computation without leaving the hyperscript mental model. A real example — like performing a calculation in a worker and updating the DOM on completion — would make this skill dramatically more useful.

Third, hyperscript's string-based templating with `put`, `set`, and `fetch` is practically a mini reactive framework. The skill could show a pattern where an element fetches HTML, puts it into the DOM, and adds event handlers — all without leaving hyperscript. This is the kind of example that makes someone say 'I didn't know it could do that.'

Fourth, the HTMX integration section should be expanded to show a full request lifecycle: click handler → disable button → add loading class → htmx request fires → hyperscript handles afterSwap → remove loading class → re-enable button → show toast notification. This end-to-end pattern is what real applications need.

Fifth, the skill should have a 'Common Patterns' section with copy-pasteable snippets for: accordion toggle, tab group, form validation feedback, modal open/close, and optimistic UI updates. These are the patterns that prove hyperscript's value in practice.

🟡 The Outsider

I've never heard of hyperscript before reading this skill. Here's what confused me:

The very first thing I see after the frontmatter is 'Quick Start' which tells me to 'identify the domain of the task' and 'open the matching file from references/.' I don't know what the domains are. I don't know what's in those reference files. This is telling me to look things up before I even understand what the tool does.

The description uses terms like 'natural-language command syntax' and 'localized DOM/event behavior' without explaining what those mean in practice. Show me what hyperserscript code looks like in the first 30 seconds. I need a concrete 'before/after' — show me the plain JS version and the hyperscript version side by side so I can see the difference.

The 'Setup Essentials' section has a code block labeled 'CDN install' and another 'Build/import.' I don't know which one I need. A beginner doesn't know the difference between a CDN install and a build/import. Just tell me: if you're adding this to an HTML file, use this; if you're using npm/webpack/vite, use this. Don't make me figure out the category.

Critical Rules #2 about queue semantics lists five options. I have no idea what 'queue last' means versus 'queue none.' An example would help: 'If a user clicks a button three times rapidly and the handler makes an HTTP request, queue last means only the third click fires.' Without concrete scenarios, these are just words.

The skill mentions `processNode()` twice but never shows me what 'dynamically inserted DOM' looks like. When would I be inserting DOM manually? I might not even know I need this function. A code example showing the problem (innerHTML assignment that doesn't trigger hyperscript) would make this click instantly.

Finally, the HTMX Companion section is the part I understand best because it relates to something I might actually do (disable buttons during requests). But the section heading says 'Guidance' and then the content is mostly telling me what NOT to do. Give me a clear DO section with working code.

🟠 The Executor

The highest-impact improvement is also the simplest: add a 'Debugging & Troubleshooting' section. Right now there's zero debugging guidance. Every user will eventually hit a handler that doesn't fire, fires twice, or throws a cryptic parse error. The skill must answer: what does a hyperscript error look like in the console? How do you inspect the compiled behavior? What are the top 5 parse errors and their fixes? This is the single biggest gap.

Second: create a 'Patterns' section with 5-8 copy-pasteable code examples for the most common use cases. Not links to reference files — actual inline code. The patterns should be: (1) click toggle, (2) disable button during HTMX request, (3) show/hide with transition, (4) fetch and render, (5) form validation feedback. Drop-in code that works. This alone would make the skill 3x more useful.

Third: fix the CDN version pinning. Change the hardcoded `@0.9.14` to a note that says 'pin the latest 0.9.x version for production; check the hyperscript releases page for current.' Stale version pins in skills erode trust.

Fourth: the 'When NOT to Use' section needs one concrete example per bullet. Not abstract advice — a code snippet that shows the anti-pattern. Show me the hyperscript code that's doing too much, then show me the JavaScript replacement. That's how an agent learns to recognize the boundary.

These four changes take maybe 2 hours to implement and would close the skill's biggest gaps. Everything else — expanded behaviors coverage, worker examples, pattern libraries — is phase two. Start with debugging and patterns. Those are the things that prevent real failures.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor. The debugging section recommendation is the highest-leverage fix — it addresses a gap that causes real pain for every hyperscript user, and it's specific, actionable, and fast to implement. The patterns section with inline copy-pasteable code would transform the skill from a lookup table into something genuinely useful at the moment of implementation.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian. Much of the criticism is valid but the response reads a bit like 'this reference model is wrong' without acknowledging that skills in this repo are explicitly designed to delegate to reference files. The reference-file architecture is a deliberate pattern across the repo. Criticizing the skill for following the established pattern is fair, but the Contrarian doesn't acknowledge that consistency with other skills (django, htmx) has its own value.

What All Missed

Nobody addressed the eval file. There's an evals.json that presumably tests this skill — nobody asked whether the evals are any good, whether they cover the common failure modes, or whether they need updating alongside any skill changes. Also, nobody mentioned the evals when discussing what patterns the skill should cover. If the evals are sparse or test the wrong things, the skill has no feedback loop.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The Expansionist. The point about behaviors being massively undercovered hits hard. Behaviors are arguably hyperscript's killer feature for real applications — reusable behavioral mixins are a genuinely unique abstraction. The suggestion to show a full HTMX lifecycle pattern (disable → loading → swap → toast) is the kind of end-to-end example that turns a reference doc into a practical skill. The Executor's patterns section is close but the Expansionist goes further by identifying WHERE the unique value is.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider. While the outsider perspective is useful, most of the confusion points are about things that any hyperscript user would face on first encounter — that's expected for a skill that presumably gets loaded when the user is already working with hyperscript. The real outsider concern should be: does the description trigger correctly? And nobody, including the Outsider, deeply analyzed the description's triggering accuracy.

What All Missed

The description's scope claim and the actual skill coverage don't match. The description mentions 'worker' and 'socket' and 'eventsource' as triggers, but the SKILL.md explicitly says these require separate bundles and barely covers them. So the description promises coverage the skill can't deliver. Nobody flagged this trigger-to-content mismatch, which is a real problem — an agent will load this skill for worker-specific questions and find almost nothing useful.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Executor, specifically the point about anti-patterns and inline examples. The Executor correctly identifies that the 'When NOT to Use' section is the weakest structural element — it gives abstract warnings without showing what the wrong approach looks like. A side-by-side comparison (bad hyperscript vs. good JavaScript) would make each bullet immediately actionable. The Executor focuses on what prevents real-world failures, which is exactly what a skill should do.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker. The suggestion to reorganize by task category instead of technical category sounds good in theory but ignores the reality that the reference files already exist and are organized by technical domain. Reorganizing the routing layer while keeping the reference files as-is would create a mismatch between the task-oriented entry points and the domain-oriented reference material. This would make the skill harder to maintain, not easier.

What All Missed

Nobody discussed the `REFERENCE.md` index file's role in this skill. The Task Routing section says 'unsure where to start → references/REFERENCE.md' but nobody evaluated whether that landing page is effective. If someone is lost, is REFERENCE.md actually helpful? Is it a good index? This is the safety net of the skill and nobody checked if it works.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Contrarian's point about the version pinning is the most concretely actionable single-issue in the entire debate. The CDN example hardcodes `@0.9.14` which will go stale. A stale version pin in a skill that ships to agents is worse than no pin at all because it creates false confidence. The fix is a one-line change. This should be the first thing fixed.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker says 'extract the single most important gotcha from each reference file and surface it inline.' This would create maintenance nightmares — every time a reference file changes, the SKILL.md needs updating. It also bloats the SKILL.md. The whole point of the reference-file architecture is to avoid this duplication. The suggestion contradicts the repo's own design pattern documented in AGENTS.md.

What All Missed

Nobody addressed the `scope: hyperscript` frontmatter field. Other skills like django have `scope: project` — what does `scope: hyperscript` actually mean for the pi agent system? Is it a user-level or project-level skill? This ambiguity could affect when and how it gets loaded, and the skill should clarify this or the frontmatter should be corrected. Also, nobody asked whether the `htmx` skill cross-references are actually present in the htmx skill, creating a bidirectional link.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Outsider's critique of the CDN vs. build/import confusion exposes a real structural problem. The Setup Essentials section should use two clearly labeled paths: 'I have an HTML file' vs. 'I use npm/vite/webpack.' This framing matches how users actually think. The Outsider's perspective is valuable because it identifies where the skill assumes knowledge that a first-time user doesn't have. The Executor's debugging recommendation is important but the Outsider's readability fixes will impact every single skill invocation, not just the debugging ones.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist's enthusiasm for behaviors, workers, and end-to-end patterns is exciting but risks scope creep. The skill's description explicitly defines a focused scope (local DOM/event behavior). Adding worker examples and full reactive patterns would bloat the skill and potentially make it trigger for tasks that are better served by JavaScript. The Expansionist is effectively arguing for a different skill, not an improved version of this one.

What All Missed

Critical Rules #6 and #7 both address the same boundary — when to stop using hyperscript. Rule 6 says 'prefer local behavior, not global sprawl' and Rule 7 says 'HTMX ownership stays separate.' These overlap. Rule 6 is about architectural scope (how many elements should one piece of hyperscript touch?) while Rule 7 is about framework boundary (when does HTMX handle this instead?). They should either be merged or given clearer differentiation with an example that shows the distinction. Nobody caught this redundancy.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council strongly agrees on three points: (1) The skill needs a debugging/troubleshooting section — this is the single biggest gap causing real user pain. (2) The skill needs concrete, copy-pasteable code examples inline in the SKILL.md, not just links to reference files. The HTMX companion example with `on htmx:beforeRequest add @disabled` is the right model but there's only one of them. (3) The 'When NOT to Use' section is ineffective as written — it needs side-by-side anti-pattern examples showing bad hyperscript and its JavaScript replacement. There is also agreement that version pinning (`@0.9.14`) is a problem, behaviors are undercovered, and the setup essentials need clearer beginner-friendly framing.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether to reorganize the skill around tasks vs. keeping the technical domain structure. The First Principles Thinker and Expansionist want task-based organization with patterns and end-to-end examples baked into the top-level skill. The Contrarian and parts of the Executor suggest surgically adding specific sections without restructuring. Reviewer 3 and Reviewer 4 also pushed back on the First Principles approach, arguing it contradicts the repo's established reference-file architecture. On scope: the Expansionist wants to expand into workers, behaviors, and full reactive patterns, while Reviewer 5 warns this would bloat the skill against its defined scope. On reference-file coupling: the First Principles Thinker wants gotchas extracted inline, but Reviewer 4 argues this creates maintenance debt that contradicts the repo design pattern.

Blind Spots Caught

The evals.json file was completely ignored by all five advisors and most reviewers. Only Reviewer 1 mentioned it, noting that without good evals the skill has no feedback loop for quality. The `scope: hyperscript` frontmatter field was also unexamined — its meaning and correctness relative to other skills' scope values is unknown. Critical Rules #6 and #7 overlap without clear differentiation — nobody flagged this redundancy. The REFERENCE.md index file, which serves as the skill's 'I'm lost' landing page, was never evaluated for effectiveness. Finally, the description-to-content mismatch (description triggers on worker/socket/eventsource but the skill can't deliver on those) was only caught by Reviewer 2.

The Recommendation

Make three surgical changes now: (1) Add a Debugging & Troubleshooting section covering the top 5 parse errors, what errors look like in the console, and how to inspect compiled behaviors. (2) Expand the HTMX Companion section into a full Patterns section with 5-8 inline code examples covering the most common real-world tasks. (3) Rewrite each 'When NOT to Use' bullet with a concrete anti-pattern example showing the bad hyperscript version and the better alternative. Then make two structural improvements: (4) Move the CDN example's version pin to a variable/comment noting to use the latest 0.9.x, and (5) Rewrite Setup Essentials with two clear paths: 'I have an HTML file' vs. 'I use a build tool.' Do NOT reorganize the reference architecture or expand into workers/full reactivity — that's a separate skill or v2. Evaluate evals.json coverage after these changes and add eval cases for the new patterns.

First Step

Add the Debugging & Troubleshooting section. Open the reference files, identify the top 5 most common errors (parse failures, silent no-fires, queue surprises, math parenthesization, and js interop type issues), and write a concise section with each error's symptoms, cause, and fix. This is the highest-impact change because it addresses pain that every hyperscript user hits, and it requires no structural changes to the skill.

16 / 26

improve-codebase-architecture

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/improve-codebase-architecture/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the improve-codebase-architecture skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a serious identity crisis. It bills itself as an architecture review tool but is really a report-generation exercise dressed up in deep-module vocabulary. The entire value proposition hinges on producing a flashy HTML file — but the HTML is a delivery mechanism, not the skill. The actual architectural reasoning is outsourced to the codebase-design skill, meaning this skill is a thin wrapper around another skill plus a template. That's not a skill, it's a script.

The grilling loop (step 3) is embarrassingly vague. It says 'use court-jester for adversarial review OR conduct a collaborative specification interview.' Pick one. Giving the agent two contradictory modes with no routing logic means it'll default to whichever is easier — probably the collaborative interview, which is just a friendly chat that won't surface real risks. The skill needs to mandate the adversarial pass first, then offer collaboration as a follow-up.

The HTML report requirement is over-engineered. Mandating Tailwind CDN, Mermaid CDN, hand-built SVG, cross-sections, mass diagrams — for what? The user asked for architectural advice, not a design portfolio piece. This is busywork that inflates token usage and execution time without proportional value. A structured text report with clear before/after descriptions would be faster, more accessible, and equally actionable. The skill should offer HTML as an option, not mandate it.

The evals are toothless. Eval 1 checks that the agent 'proposes writing an HTML report' — that's a checkbox, not a quality bar. There's no eval for whether the architectural analysis is actually correct, whether the deletion test is applied properly, or whether the before/after diagrams are accurate. You're testing the process, not the output.

Finally, the skill has no termination criteria. When does the grilling loop end? How many candidates should be surfaced? What if the codebase is 10 lines? What if it's 500K lines? There's no scoping guidance, no time/effort budget, no 'this skill is not appropriate for X' section.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the vocabulary and the HTML scaffolding. What is this skill actually trying to do? It wants to help a developer find the highest-leverage refactoring in their codebase — the one change that makes everything else easier. That's the core job.

From first principles, that requires three things: (1) a way to identify candidates, (2) a way to evaluate their impact, and (3) a way to validate the proposed change before committing. The skill has #1 (explore for friction) and a weak #2 (deletion test, depth vocabulary), but #3 is where it falls apart. The grilling loop is underspecified — it name-drops court-jester but doesn't define what a 'good' grilling looks like. What questions must be answered? What's the minimum viable specification before a deepening is approved?

The skill also assumes the codebase has seams to find. But what about greenfield code? Or code that's uniformly shallow with no clear consolidation targets? The skill needs an explicit 'no candidates found' path — a way to say 'your architecture is either already deep or the problems are at a different layer (data model, deployment topology) that this skill doesn't address.'

The dependency on codebase-design is architecturally sound (don't duplicate the vocabulary), but the skill never tells the agent to actually load that skill. It says 'use the codebase-design skill' in prose but doesn't include a `skill: codebase-design` directive or any mechanism to ensure the vocabulary is loaded. In practice, the agent will either already have it (lucky) or won't (silent vocabulary drift).

The HTML report is a presentation layer concern. The real artifact should be the architectural decision — the before/after model, the test strategy, the risk assessment. The HTML is just how you show it. The skill should separate the analysis from the presentation, making the analysis the primary artifact and the report a secondary rendering.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a much bigger opportunity than it realizes. Right now it's a one-shot architecture review. But the real value is in making it a living practice — a recurring architectural health check that tracks deepening over time.

Imagine: the skill stores a snapshot of the architecture review (the candidates, the chosen deepening, the before/after state) in a project-level file or ADR. Next time it runs, it compares against the last snapshot. Did the deepening actually happen? Did it create new shallow modules? Are there new candidates that emerged from the last refactor? This turns a point-in-time review into an architectural evolution tracker.

The HTML report could be versioned and diffed. Show the user: 'Last time, you had 3 shallow modules in the Order pipeline. Now you have 1 deep module and 2 remaining candidates.' That's a progress bar for architecture quality — incredibly motivating and useful for team leads.

The skill could also integrate with CI. A lightweight mode that runs on every PR, checks for new shallow modules being introduced, and flags them in the PR review. Not the full HTML report — just a 'hey, this new module has a 1:1 interface-to-implementation ratio, consider deepening' comment.

The grilling loop could produce a structured decision record — not just a conversation, but a mini-ADR with the thesis, the counter-arguments, the decision, and the expected outcomes. This feeds directly into the project's ADR log and gives the skill lasting impact beyond the session.

And the vocabulary enforcement — don't use 'component,' use 'module' — this is actually a linting opportunity. The skill could include a vocabulary checker that scans the codebase for inconsistent terminology and suggests alignment. That's a whole adjacent skill hiding inside this one.

🟡 The Outsider

I just read this skill and I'm confused about when I'm supposed to use it. The description says 'scan a codebase for deepening opportunities' — but what does 'deepening' mean to someone who hasn't read the codebase-design skill? The description doesn't explain the concept. It name-drops 'deep modules' and 'shallow modules' without defining them. A user who doesn't already know this vocabulary will read the description and think 'sure, I want to improve my architecture' without understanding what specific thing this skill does versus, say, a general code review.

The process section jumps straight into 'Explore' with no setup. What codebase? The current directory? A path I specify? A git repo URL? The skill doesn't tell me what input it expects. I'm an agent — I need to know what to read, where to look, what the scope is.

The HTML report section is detailed about styling (Tailwind CDN, Mermaid, hand-built SVG) but vague about content. What makes a good candidate? How many should I surface? What if I find 20 candidates — do I list all 20? The skill says 'present candidates' but doesn't define the expected count or prioritization method.

The grilling loop says 'once the user picks a candidate, conduct a specification interview to walk the design tree.' What's a design tree? What questions do I ask? The skill references court-jester but doesn't say whether court-jester replaces the interview or supplements it. I'm left guessing at the workflow.

The reference index at the bottom is just a table pointing to html-report.md. That's it. No examples of what a good output looks like, no sample candidate card, no before/after of a real (even toy) architecture review. I'd need to reverse-engineer the expected quality from the HTML scaffold, which is a styling guide, not a content guide.

Also: the skill says 'Do NOT propose interfaces yet' in step 2, but doesn't say when to propose them. Step 3 mentions 'the shape of the deepened module' but doesn't give a template or checklist for what a good interface proposal looks like.

🟠 The Executor

The fastest path to making this skill actually useful is to fix three things:

**1. Add a concrete example.** The single biggest gap is that there's no worked example of a candidate card — not even a toy one. The HTML report reference has scaffold code and diagram patterns, but no example of a filled-in card with real (even fictional) module names, a real problem statement, and a real before/after. Add one complete example card to the html-report.md reference. That alone will improve agent output quality by 50%.

**2. Define the grilling loop as a concrete checklist.** Replace 'conduct a specification interview to walk the design tree' with 5-7 specific questions the agent must answer: What's behind the seam? What tests exist today? What tests would exist after? What breaks if this deepening is wrong? What's the rollback plan? This turns vague guidance into executable steps.

**3. Add scope boundaries.** One line: 'For codebases under 500 lines, skip the HTML report and present candidates inline. For codebases over 100K lines, focus on one package/module per run.' This prevents the agent from either over-engineering a tiny review or drowning in a massive codebase.

Everything else — the HTML styling, the Mermaid vs. hand-built diagram guidance, the vocabulary enforcement — is fine as-is. The skill's structure is sound. It just needs concrete examples and executable checklists to go from 'sounds good in theory' to 'works in practice.'

The evals also need one more case: an eval where the codebase has no good deepening candidates, to test that the skill can say 'nothing to do here' gracefully. That's the second-fastest win.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor — it identifies the highest-leverage fixes with minimal fuss. The 'add a concrete example' recommendation is the single most impactful change possible. Agents learn from examples ten times faster than from abstract guidance. The checklist for the grilling loop is equally practical.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist — the CI integration and architectural evolution tracker ideas are exciting but completely out of scope for a skill file. You're describing a product feature, not a skill improvement. The vocabulary linting idea is interesting but belongs in a separate skill.

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed the skill's description field — the YAML frontmatter `description` that controls triggering. It says 'Scan a codebase for deepening opportunities, present them as a visual HTML report, then grill through whichever one you pick.' This is vague and doesn't mention the vocabulary dependency, the deletion test, or the 3-phase process. A better description would improve triggering accuracy significantly.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker — correctly identifies that the skill's core job is finding the highest-leverage refactoring, and that the three required capabilities are identification, evaluation, and validation. The observation that the skill never actually instructs the agent to load the codebase-design skill is a critical catch that nobody else made.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian — calling the HTML report 'busywork' is wrong. The visual format is the skill's differentiator. Text-only architectural reviews are a dime a dozen; the HTML report with before/after diagrams is what makes this skill worth having. Dumbing it down to 'structured text' would make it generic.

What All Missed

The skill has no guidance on what to do when the user's codebase uses a different architectural pattern than deep modules. Event-driven architectures, microservices, and functional pipelines may not map cleanly to the deep-module model. The skill needs a 'when not to use this' section or at least guidance on adapting the vocabulary to different paradigms.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider — the observation that the skill doesn't explain what input it expects (what codebase? what path?) is fundamental. A skill that doesn't tell the agent what to read is like a function with no parameters. The point about 'no worked example' reinforces The Executor's recommendation but from a different angle — the Outsider shows that even understanding the expected output is impossible without an example.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist — versioning HTML reports and comparing snapshots is a nice idea but practically infeasible. HTML files in /tmp are ephemeral by design. The skill explicitly writes to temp directories to avoid repo pollution. You can't diff files that get deleted on reboot. This suggestion contradicts the skill's own file hygiene rules.

What All Missed

The skill references `codebase-design/SKILL.md` and `court-jester/SKILL.md` using relative paths (`../codebase-design/SKILL.md`). If this skill is ever invoked from a different directory structure (e.g., a user's local skills directory that doesn't have these siblings), the references break. The skill should either use absolute paths or include a fallback instruction.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Executor — the recommendation to add scope boundaries (under 500 lines → inline, over 100K lines → one package per run) is the kind of practical guardrail that separates skills that work from skills that flounder. The eval for 'no candidates found' is equally important — every skill needs a graceful negative path.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian — dismissing the entire skill as 'a thin wrapper around another skill plus a template' is reductive. Many valuable skills are thin wrappers that orchestrate other skills. The value is in the process (explore → present → grill), not in the individual components. The skill's orchestration is its contribution.

What All Missed

The skill says to use `$TMPDIR` falling back to `/tmp` (or `%TEMP%` on Windows), but the HTML report reference doesn't mention this at all — it just says 'write to the OS temp directory.' The two references should be consistent. Also, the skill doesn't address what happens if `xdg-open`/`open`/`start` fails — there should be a fallback instruction like 'if the open command fails, print the path and instruct the user to open it manually.'

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker — the identification of the missing 'no candidates found' path is the most architecturally significant gap in the skill. A tool that always finds problems is a tool that can't be trusted. The skill needs to be able to say 'your architecture is already deep' or 'the problems are at a different layer' with the same confidence it says 'here's what to fix.'

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider — while the 'what codebase?' question is valid, it's also the kind of thing that's typically handled by the agent's context (current working directory, project root). Most skills don't explicitly state 'read the current directory' — it's implied. This is a minor clarity issue, not a fundamental gap.

What All Missed

The skill's grilling loop says to 'use the court-jester skill for adversarial review of the proposed deepening, or conduct a collaborative specification interview.' The word 'or' creates ambiguity about when to use which. The skill should define a decision rule: use court-jester when the user is confident in their choice and needs stress-testing; use the interview when the user is uncertain and needs to explore. Without this routing logic, the agent will guess.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council broadly agrees on three points: (1) the skill needs a concrete worked example — at least one complete candidate card with fictional but realistic content — to give the agent a quality target; (2) the grilling loop is underspecified and needs a concrete checklist of questions or a defined interview structure; and (3) the skill needs a 'no candidates found' / negative path so it can gracefully handle codebases where deepening isn't the right move. There's also agreement that the skill should explicitly instruct the agent to load the codebase-design skill rather than assuming the vocabulary is available.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes sharply on the HTML report. The Contrarian sees it as over-engineered busywork; the Expansionist sees it as a foundation for a larger architectural tracking system; Reviewer 2 argues it's the skill's key differentiator. The Contrarian also dismisses the skill as a 'thin wrapper,' while Reviewer 4 argues that orchestration is the skill's actual value. On scope: the Executor wants hard line-count boundaries; the Outsider thinks the input scope question is minor and implied by context.

Blind Spots Caught

The entire council missed the frontmatter `description` field's impact on triggering accuracy — it's vague and doesn't mention the vocabulary dependency or the 3-phase process. Nobody addressed the relative path fragility for skill references (`../codebase-design/SKILL.md`). The fallback for failed `xdg-open`/`start` commands was also universally overlooked. Additionally, no advisor discussed what happens when the codebase's architectural paradigm (event-driven, microservices) doesn't map cleanly to the deep-module model.

The Recommendation

Prioritize these changes in order: (1) Add one complete worked example to html-report.md — a fictional candidate card with filled-in problem, solution, before/after diagram, and wins. (2) Replace the vague grilling loop with a 5-7 question checklist that constitutes a minimum viable specification. (3) Add a negative path: explicit guidance for when no candidates are found or when the architecture is already deep. (4) Add an explicit instruction to load the codebase-design skill at the start of step 1. (5) Add scope boundaries (inline for small codebases, single-package for large ones). (6) Add an eval for the negative path. (7) Update the frontmatter description to mention the 3-phase process and vocabulary dependency.

First Step

Add a complete worked example candidate card to references/html-report.md. This is the single highest-leverage change — it clarifies the expected output quality, gives the agent a concrete template to follow, and resolves the 'no example' gap identified by both The Executor and The Outsider. Everything else builds on having a clear target.

17 / 26

langchain

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/langchain/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can this skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill is dangerously overconfident about a moving target. LangChain's API surface has been in near-constant flux since 2024 — the `create_agent` function, LCEL patterns, and even package boundaries shift between minor versions. The skill's `last_verified` is 2026-04-24, but there's no mechanism to keep it current. The critical rules section reads like it was written by someone who read the docs once and assumed they're permanent. Rule 4 says 'Prefer main langchain for current agent/app work' — but the main `langchain` package's role has been shrinking as the ecosystem splits into `langchain-core` + provider packages. Rule 5's advice about `create_agent` vs LangGraph is already showing signs of age as LangGraph's prebuilt agents mature. The skill also has zero guidance on version pinning or how to handle the fact that `langchain` 0.x and 1.x have breaking changes. There's no mention of the `langchain-community` package, which is a real source of confusion in practice. The reference map is clean but the references themselves likely contain code examples that will rot. The skill needs a version-awareness section, explicit 'check the installed version' guidance, and a deprecation policy for its own content. Without that, it will actively mislead users within 6 months.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

What problem does this skill actually solve? It's trying to be a compressed reference for an entire ecosystem — but the ecosystem already has official docs. The real value should be: (1) preventing the most common mistakes agents make with LangChain, (2) providing authoritative package-boundary resolution when imports are ambiguous, and (3) giving task-routing guidance so agents read the right reference file first. Currently, the skill does (2) well and (3) adequately, but (1) is underspecified. The 'Critical Rules' section is the highest-value part — rules about `langchain-core` having no integrations, `InMemorySaver` not being for production, and `configurable_fields="any"` being a security risk are exactly the kind of things an agent would get wrong. But these rules are buried in a numbered list without prioritization. The skill should be restructured around failure modes: 'Here are the 10 things agents get wrong with LangChain, and here's the correct pattern for each.' The reference files are good but the SKILL.md itself should be a decision tree, not a table of contents. The 'Quick Start' section is the right instinct but it's too generic — 'Identify the owning package first' is obvious; what's not obvious is HOW to identify it when you see `from langchain_openai import ChatOpenAI` and don't know if that's `langchain-openai` or `langchain`.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is leaving enormous value on the table by being purely reactive. It tells agents what NOT to do and where to look, but it doesn't provide any opinionated patterns for the 80% use cases. Where are the copy-paste-ready patterns for: a standard RAG pipeline with Chroma, a tool-calling agent with error handling, a LangGraph workflow with human-in-the-loop? The skill should include 'Recommended Patterns' sections — opinionated, tested starting points that an agent can adapt. The references could include a `references/patterns/` directory with canonical implementations. Additionally, the skill has nothing on testing LangChain applications — how do you unit test a LangGraph graph? How do you mock `init_chat_model`? How do you test RAG retrieval quality? This is a massive gap. The skill also ignores the LangChain CLI and `langchain serve` / LangServe deployment story entirely. And there's no guidance on cost management — token counting, streaming vs non-streaming cost implications, or when to use which model tier. Finally, the skill should address the LangChain → LangGraph migration path more explicitly, since many teams are being pushed toward LangGraph whether they need it or not, and the skill should help agents resist premature adoption.

🟡 The Outsider

Reading this as someone unfamiliar with the project: I have no idea what a 'skill' is supposed to do in this context. The SKILL.md opens with 'Use this skill for Python LangChain ecosystem work' but doesn't explain how it interacts with the agent's workflow. The 'scope: langchain-python' field is meaningless to me. The 'source_basis' field is nice but I don't know what 'official docs + source code + README' means practically — which README? The reference files have names like 'models-prompts-runnables.md' which is clear, but 'migration-classic-gotchas.md' is jargon-heavy. The 'When Not To Use This Section' is actually the most useful section because it sets boundaries — more of that energy should be in the rest of the skill. The 'Task Routing' section is good but reads like a router config file, not guidance. A concrete example would help: 'If the user says "I want to build a chatbot with memory and document search", here's which two reference files to read and in what order.' The skill also assumes I know what LCEL, RAG, and 'tool-loop agents' are — it should either define these terms or link to definitions. The 'Output Expectations' section tells me what to output but not why anyone would want that output — what's the consumer of this information?

🟠 The Executor

The fastest improvement: add a 'Decision Tree' section at the top of SKILL.md that routes agents in 3 steps or fewer. Currently, the routing table at the bottom requires reading 7 entries to find the right one. A simple flowchart — 'Is it about packages/imports? → package-map. Is it about agents? → agents-tools. Is it about workflows? → langgraph.' — would cut the time-to-answer in half. Second: add version-check guidance. Every reference file should have a 'Last verified against' version number, and SKILL.md should tell agents to run `pip show langchain langchain-core langgraph` before trusting any import advice. Third: the 'Critical Rules' section needs a 'DO NOT' summary box at the top — the 3-4 rules that cause the most real-world bugs. Currently they're buried in a 12-item list. Fourth: add one concrete code example per major section. The skill is all prose and no code, which means agents still have to guess at syntax. A single `init_chat_model` call, a single `create_agent` call, and a single `StateGraph` compilation would prevent more errors than all the prose warnings combined. Fifth: remove the 'Content Ownership' section at the bottom — it's meta-information that doesn't help the agent write better code and takes up tokens.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor — the decision tree and version-check guidance are immediately actionable and address the two biggest practical problems: slow routing and stale advice. The call for concrete code examples is the single highest-impact change.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian correctly identifies version rot as a risk but offers no practical solution beyond 'add version awareness.' The Executor's concrete `pip show` command is better, but neither addresses how to handle the case where the skill itself is the stale source — who updates it and when?

What All Missed

None of the reviewers addressed the skill's interaction with the broader skill ecosystem. How does this skill relate to the `python-async` skill (LangChain is heavily async)? How does it interact with `django` or `litestar` skills when LangChain is used inside a web framework? Cross-skill references are completely absent.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker — reframing the skill around failure modes rather than reference navigation is the right architectural instinct. The current SKILL.md is organized like a library catalog; it should be organized like a troubleshooting guide. The observation that the Critical Rules are the highest-value content and should be restructured is spot-on.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist's call for copy-paste patterns is appealing but dangerous. LangChain patterns are version-sensitive; a 'recommended pattern' that works in 0.3.0 might break in 0.4.0. The Expansionist doesn't address how to keep patterns current, and the Contrarian's version-rot concern applies directly here.

What All Missed

No reviewer mentioned the complete absence of async/await guidance. LangChain's Python API has both sync and async paths (`ainvoke` vs `invoke`, `astream` vs `stream`), and agents will get this wrong constantly. The `python-async` skill exists but there's zero cross-reference.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Contrarian — the version-rot concern is the most important observation in the entire debate. A skill about a fast-moving library that has no freshness mechanism is a liability, not an asset. The specific callouts (no `langchain-community` mention, Rule 4 aging, no version pinning guidance) are precise and actionable.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider's confusion about what a 'skill' is suggests the SKILL.md needs a preamble, but the Outsider's other suggestions (defining LCEL, RAG, etc.) would bloat the skill with information the target audience already knows. The skill is for agents working on LangChain tasks — they know what RAG is. The Outsider's feedback is useful for the meta-layer but not for the content.

What All Missed

No one addressed the LangSmith cost and privacy implications. LangSmith is an observability SaaS — sending traces, prompts, and data to a third party has real cost and privacy implications. The skill treats LangSmith as purely beneficial with zero caveats about data exposure or pricing.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Executor's call to remove the 'Content Ownership' section is the most efficient token-saving suggestion. Every token spent on meta-information is a token not spent on actual guidance. The decision tree idea is good but the Executor's '3 steps or fewer' framing is what makes it actionable — it's not just 'add a tree,' it's 'make it fast.'

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist wants to add patterns, testing guidance, deployment advice, cost management, and migration resistance. This would triple the skill's size. The skill's current value is its focus — it's a routing document, not a comprehensive guide. The Expansionist is solving a different problem (building a LangChain tutorial) than the skill is designed to solve (helping agents navigate the existing references).

What All Missed

No reviewer evaluated the quality of the reference files themselves. The SKILL.md is the routing layer, but the references are the actual content. If the references are thin or outdated, the best routing in the world won't help. The skill needs a reference-quality audit.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker's observation that the skill should be organized around failure modes is the most structurally sound recommendation. The current organization mirrors the ecosystem's package structure (models, agents, retrieval, LangGraph, LangSmith) — which is logical for a human browsing a reference but terrible for an agent trying to fix a specific problem. A failure-mode organization ('Import not found,' 'Agent loops forever,' 'State not persisting,' 'Tracing not showing up') would be far more useful.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor wants to add code examples but doesn't address where they live. Inline in SKILL.md? In the references? In a separate `examples/` directory? The 'just add examples' advice without an implementation plan is incomplete. The Expansionist's `references/patterns/` idea is actually the better structural solution.

What All Missed

No one discussed the skill's triggering mechanism. The description says 'Use when tasks involve...' but doesn't address false positives. When does an agent incorrectly trigger this skill? What about tasks that use LangChain indirectly through another library? The triggering accuracy is unexamined.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees on three things: (1) the skill needs version-awareness and freshness mechanisms — the Contrarian's rot concern is real and the Executor's `pip show` check is the minimum viable response; (2) the Critical Rules section is the highest-value content and should be more prominent and better prioritized; (3) the skill should include at least minimal code examples — prose-only guidance for a code library is insufficient. There is also broad agreement that the 'Content Ownership' section at the bottom should be removed or minimized.

Where the Council Clashes

The Expansionist and the Contrarian clash on the patterns question — the Expansionist wants opinionated copy-paste patterns while the Contrarian warns that patterns will rot. The resolution is: patterns should live in reference files (not SKILL.md) with explicit version markers, and should be limited to stable APIs. The Outsider and the Executor clash on audience assumptions — the Outsider wants more definitions, the Executor wants less bloat. The resolution: define terms on first use in reference files, not in SKILL.md. The First Principles Thinker and the Executor clash on structure — failure-mode vs decision-tree. The resolution: use the decision tree for routing (Executor) but organize the Critical Rules as failure-mode entries (First Principles).

Blind Spots Caught

The council missed three things: (1) async/await guidance — LangChain has dual sync/async APIs and the skill has zero mention of `ainvoke`, `astream`, or async patterns; (2) cross-skill references — no mention of `python-async`, `django`, or `litestar` skills that commonly co-occur with LangChain usage; (3) LangSmith cost/privacy — the skill treats LangSmith as purely beneficial with no caveats about data exposure, pricing, or self-hosting alternatives.

The Recommendation

Restructure SKILL.md in three phases. Phase 1 (immediate): Add a 3-step decision tree at the top, add version-check guidance (`pip show langchain langchain-core langgraph`), promote the top 5 Critical Rules into a highlighted summary box, and remove the 'Content Ownership' section. Phase 2 (short-term): Add one minimal code example per major section (model init, agent creation, graph compilation), add async/await guidance noting the dual API, and add cross-references to `python-async` skill. Phase 3 (medium-term): Audit reference files for accuracy against current package versions, add a `last_verified` field to each reference file, and add a LangSmith cost/privacy caveat.

First Step

Add the 3-step decision tree and version-check guidance to the top of SKILL.md. This is the Executor's highest-impact, lowest-effort change: it addresses the Contrarian's version-rot concern and the First Principles Thinker's routing-clarity concern in a single edit. The decision tree should read: (1) Package/import question → `references/package-map.md`; (2) Model/LCEL/agent/retrieval → read the matching reference; (3) Workflow/persistence → `references/langgraph-stateful-workflows.md`. Add above it: 'Run `pip show langchain langchain-core langgraph` before trusting any import advice in this skill.'
