litestar

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/litestar/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can this Litestar skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill is a well-organized reference router, but it has several fatal flaws. First, the 'Output Expectations' section is pure process noise — telling the agent to 'name the reference files used' and 'state verification steps' adds boilerplate to every answer without improving correctness. Agents either do this naturally or they won't; mandating it just inflates token count. Second, the skill NEVER shows actual code. Every rule is prose describing behavior. Rule 2 says 'annotate everything' but shows zero handler examples. Rule 9 says 'annotate data with Body(media_type=...)' — for what? A form? A CSV upload? Show me. Third, the version staleness risk is basically unaddressed. The skill mentions '2.x vs 3.0 transition' in one bullet but gives no version pinning, no feature matrix, no deprecation timeline. If a user is on Litestar 2.8 and the skill casually references 3.0 patterns, they get runtime errors. Fourth, the 'When Not To Use' section pushes the user toward other skills but never says WHICH one — 'use the relevant integration docs' is a dead end. Fifth, the Task Routing section has duplicate entries with subtly different phrasing that could confuse routing logic (e.g., the single-item list vs. multi-item list — they overlap badly). The skill works as a library card catalog but fails as a practical guide.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the structure and ask: what is an agent actually trying to do when it loads this skill? It has a task involving Litestar and needs the right pattern applied correctly. The skill's mental model is 'route to a reference file, read the reference, apply the pattern.' But this assumes the agent can correctly identify which domain the task belongs to — and many real tasks are ambiguous. A handler that validates request body, applies middleware for rate-limiting, AND returns a DTO crosses three domains. The skill acknowledges this only in the 'Task Routing' cross-cutting section, but gives no guidance on READING ORDER when multiple references apply. Should the agent read architecture-layers first or dependencies-dto first? This matters because Litestar's DI system interacts with DTOs which interact with handlers — they're not independent. The skill also treats Litestar as a static target, but the framework is evolving rapidly (2.x → 3.0). The core Litestar contract — type-annotated handler signatures, layered config, DI resolution — is stable, but the surface API changes. The skill should anchor on the stable contracts and treat surface APIs as version-qualified snapshots. Finally, the skill has no concept of 'progressive loading': the agent loads ALL 13 rules, the reference map, the task routing, the output expectations — before it even knows if the task is about websockets or handlers. A tiered loading approach (rules first, then only the needed reference) would be more efficient.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is playing defense when it could be playing offense. It routes and references — fine. But the real value an agent needs is pattern recognition for COMMON LITESTAR MISTAKES. Consider adding an 'Anti-Patterns & Pitfalls' section with entries like: 'Agent used `request.body()` instead of `data: MyDTO` for JSON parsing,' 'Agent forgot `async def` on a handler doing I/O and blocked the event loop,' 'Agent placed a `Cookie` in `Response(headers=...)` instead of using `response.set_cookie()`,' 'Agent used `Depends()` from FastAPI instead of Litestar's `Provide`.' These are the real-world failure modes. Second, the skill should add COMMON RECIPES — small, copy-pasteable code patterns for the 80% cases: a standard CRUD controller, a file upload handler, a websocket listener with auth, a DTO with validation. The reference files presumably have these, but the SKILL.md should surface them. Third, the skill could integrate with the skill-creator skill for benchmarking itself — have evals that test whether the agent correctly routes common Litestar tasks. Fourth, the 'integrations.md' reference gets barely any attention in the SKILL.md — HTMX + Litestar is a genuinely powerful combo that deserves explicit promotion, not a footnote. Fifth, consider a 'Quick Patterns' appendix at the SKILL.md level (not in references) showing the 5 most common handler signatures, so the agent gets instant pattern-matching context before diving into references.

🟡 The Outsider

Loading this skill as a newcomer, I'm overwhelmed before I'm helped. The first thing I see after the description is 'Quick Start' — but step 1 says 'Identify the primary task domain' from a list of 9 options I don't understand yet. I came here because I need to add an endpoint; I don't know if my problem is 'handlers,' 'requests,' or 'DI.' The skill should start with a SIMPLE EXAMPLE — a complete `Litestar(...)` app with one route — before the reference map. Second, terms like 'layer precedence,' 'yield dependencies,' and 'connection-scoped' are used without any introductory context. I need at least parenthetical hints: 'layer precedence (closer = higher priority).' Third, the 'Critical Rules' section mixes beginner-critical rules (annotate everything) with expert-critical ones (router-generated 404s bypass middleware). These should be tiered: 'Essential Rules' vs. 'Advanced Rules' so beginners don't get paralyzed and experts don't skim past the advanced ones. Fourth, the Task Routing section says 'Add one secondary reference only when the task clearly crosses domains' — but 'clearly' is doing heavy lifting. A task like 'add rate limiting to my upload endpoint' DOES cross domains (middleware + handlers), but that's not obvious to someone who just learned what middleware is. Better: give explicit 'if X and also Y, also read Z' heuristics. Fifth, the closing line about 'References used: ...' format — is this mandatory? Recommended? A suggestion? The tone wobbles between prescription and suggestion throughout.

🟠 The Executor

The highest-impact improvement is also the simplest: add 3-5 COMPLETE CODE EXAMPLES inline. Not in reference files — right in the SKILL.md. Show a full handler with DTO, validation, dependency injection, and error handling in one block. This alone would prevent 60% of agent mistakes because Litestar's handler signature contract is fussy and seeing it working end-to-end is worth 1000 words of rules. Second, fix the Task Routing section — merge the single-domain and multi-domain sections into ONE table with a 'Primary' and optional 'Secondary' column. The current two-list format with overlapping entries creates confusion about which to consult. Third, add a visible DEPRECATED/EXPERIMENTAL marker system for the rules and references. Rules 4 and 12 already hint at version sensitivity (`sync_to_thread` warnings, `StaticFilesConfig` deprecation). Make this systematic: tag each critical rule with the Litestar version range it applies to. Fourth, prune the 'Output Expectations' section down to 2 lines. The 'name your references' and 'state verification steps' mandates are agent-process rules that belong in AGENTS.md, not in a framework skill. Fifth, add a single 'Common Gotcha' callout box at the top — the one thing that trips up every Litestar beginner: 'Litestar will NOT start if any handler parameter lacks a type annotation. Always annotate. This is not optional.' Front-load the pain.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

Response E (The Executor) is strongest because it identifies the single highest-leverage fix — inline code examples — and pairs it with concrete, immediately actionable structural changes (merge the routing table, add version tags, prune Output Expectations). Every recommendation is shippable in one session.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response B (The First Principles Thinker) has the biggest blind spot: it proposes 'tiered loading' and 'progressive disclosure' as if the agent has control over what it reads. In practice, the SKILL.md loads in full. The suggestion to split rules by tier is good for READERS but doesn't address the actual loading mechanism.

What All Missed

All responses missed OpenAPI/schema generation. Litestar auto-generates OpenAPI schemas from handler signatures — this is a major selling point and a major source of agent mistakes (wrong return type annotations producing broken schemas). None of the advisors mentioned it as something the skill should explicitly address.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

Response C (The Expansionist) sees the biggest picture. Anti-patterns and recipes are genuinely what differentiate a reference doc from a skill. The suggestion to add eval/benchmarking via skill-creator is strategically smart — it creates a feedback loop for improvement.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response D (The Outsider) assumes the audience is a human beginner, but this skill is for AI agents. Suggesting 'parenthetical hints' and 'introductory context' adds tokens without proportional value — agents don't need 'layer precedence (closer = higher priority)' because they either know it from the reference or they don't.

What All Missed

All responses missed the testing gap. Litestar has a specific test client (`TestClient`) and testing patterns that differ from pytest-django or FastAPI's test client. The SKILL.md has `websockets-testing.md` as a reference but the SKILL.md body says almost nothing about WHEN or WHY to test, or what Litestar-specific test pitfalls exist. None of the advisors flagged this.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

Response A (The Contrarian) is strongest on specific,Enumerated defects. The point about Task Routing having overlapping two-list entries is concrete and verifiable. The critique of Output Expectations as token-inflating boilerplate is sharp and correct. The dead-end critique of 'use the relevant integration docs' is fair.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response E (The Executor) suggests tagging each critical rule with version ranges. This is operationally impractical — the skill would need constant updating with every Litestar release, creating maintenance burden. A single 'Last verified against Litestar X.Y' header plus a deprecation-watch rule would be more sustainable.

What All Missed

All responses missed the controller pattern. Litestar's controller class system (`Controller` base class, `controllers=` param on routers) is a primary organizational unit, yet the SKILL.md's critical rules section doesn't mention controllers at all. No advisor flagged this omission.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

Response B (First Principles Thinker) correctly identifies that the core value is 'route to correct pattern' and that the skill assumes better domain-classification ability than agents actually have. The observation about reading order mattering for interacting subsystems (DI → DTOs → handlers) is the most architecturally sophisticated insight in the council.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response C (Expansionist) suggests'benchmarking via skill-creator evals' which is a meta-improvement that costs significant effort for uncertain ROI. Good idea in theory, but it's a second-order optimization — fix the content first, then measure. It shouldn't be in the top recommendations.

What All Missed

All responses missed the guards gap. Guards are Litestar's auth/permission mechanism and are mentioned in the description but get only a passing mention in Critical Rules (Rule 5: 'they accumulate'). No advisor said the skill should have explicit guard examples or common guard anti-patterns. For a framework skill, auth patterns are table stakes.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

Response D (Outsider) is strongest on structural clarity. The tiered rules suggestion (Essential vs. Advanced) directly addresses the signal-to-noise problem. The critique of 'clearly crosses domains' as doing heavy lifting is precise — replacing vague adjectives with explicit cross-domain heuristics would materially improve routing accuracy.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response A (Contrarian) argues that 'never showing actual code' is a fatal flaw, but the skill is explicitly designed as a REFERENCE ROUTER, not a reference book. Delegating code to reference files is intentional architecture, not a bug. The real question is whether the reference files themselves contain adequate code — which the Contrarian didn't verify.

What All Missed

All responses missed the response model surface. Litestar has rich response customization: `Response` type, status codes, headers, cookies, background tasks, and Redirect/response caching. None of the advisors mentioned whether the skill adequately covers response construction patterns — which is the other half of the request/response cycle that the focus on handlers leaves incomplete.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council broadly agrees on three points: (1) the Output Expectations section is bloated with agent-process boilerplate that should be pruned or moved to AGENTS.md; (2) the Task Routing section's two-list structure with overlapping entries is confusing and should be merged into a single table; and (3) the skill needs more concrete, practical guidance — whether through inline code examples (Executor), anti-patterns (Expansionist), or better cross-domain routing heuristics (Outsider). Every advisor identified some form of the 'too abstract, not actionable enough' problem from a different angle.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether this skill should contain inline code examples (Contrarian: yes, fatal flaw without them; First Principles: wrong level of abstraction for a routing skill) and on whether the audience is beginners needing context (Outsider) or agents that either know the reference content or don't (Reviewer 2's critique of Outsider). There's also tension between the Expansionist's 'add recipes, anti-patterns, evals' vision and the Executor's 'prune, tighten, ship now' pragmatics — they're both right but imply different scopes of revision.

Blind Spots Caught

The entire council missed four significant gaps: (1) OpenAPI/schema generation from handler signatures — a core Litestar feature and common agent mistake source; (2) testing patterns specific to Litestar's TestClient and Litestar-specific test pitfalls; (3) the Controller class pattern, which is Litestar's primary organizational unit but barely mentioned in SKILL.md-level rules; and (4) guards/auth patterns, which are named in the trigger description but under-served by only one passing rule mention. These are all high-value, practical gaps that would cause real agent failures.

The Recommendation

Execute a two-phase improvement. Phase 1 (immediate, high-ROI): merge the Task Routing lists into a single table with Primary/Secondary columns; prune Output Expectations to 2 lines; add a Tier-1 'Essential Rules' subset (annotate everything, semantic decorators, layer precedence, sync_to_thread) separated from advanced rules; add a version-verified-against header. Phase 2 (follow-up): add one inline code example showing a complete handler with DTO + DI; add an Anti-Patterns section with 5-7 common Litestar mistakes; verify and improve coverage of guards, controllers, OpenAPI generation, and TestClient patterns in the references. Both phases should check reference files for code example adequacy before assuming the gap is the SKILL.md's fault.

First Step

Merge the Task Routing section's two bullet lists into a single table with columns: Task Pattern | Primary Reference | Secondary Reference (if any). This is a 30-minute edit that eliminates the most concrete structural defect every advisor touched on.

19 / 26

prestashop

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/prestashop/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can this PrestaShop 9 module development skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a serious credibility problem: it's fighting a battle it already lost. The entire premise is 'stop AI from inventing PrestaShop classes' — but the skill itself is a hallucination risk factory. Look at the reference map: 11 markdown files, each with external doc links that may 404 or redirect. The skill has no mechanism to verify those links are alive. If a link is dead, the agent will do exactly what the skill warns against: guess. The evals are equally weak — three prompts with soft 'expectations' that can't be mechanically scored. An agent could write a fluent, confident, completely wrong module and pass all three evals. The 'Critical Rules' section is the strongest part, but it's undermined by the reference architecture: critical rules live in SKILL.md, but the actual implementation guidance is scattered across 11 files. An agent routing to the wrong reference file gets no guardrail. The task routing table is naive — real tasks don't fall into neat single-category buckets. 'Build a module that syncs orders via API and exposes a BO config page' hits five reference files. Which one takes priority? The skill doesn't say. The 'last_verified' date is March 2026 — already stale for a living framework. There's no versioning strategy, no changelog, no mechanism to detect when PrestaShop 9.x changes break the guidance. The skill is a well-intentioned paperweight.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip this down. What problem are we actually solving? The core failure mode is: an AI agent tasked with PrestaShop module work will confidently produce code that looks right but uses wrong class names, wrong folder layouts, wrong hook signatures, and wrong service definitions. The skill exists to prevent confident wrongness. That's it. Everything else is ornament. So let's rebuild from that. The skill needs three things: (1) a closed-world assertion — 'these are the only valid patterns, anything else is wrong', (2) a lookup mechanism — 'when you need X, here is the exact pattern', and (3) a verification step — 'before you ship, check these things'. The current SKILL.md has (1) and (2) but (3) is weak. The testing checklist exists but is buried in a reference file. The critical rules are good but negative-only — they say 'don't' but rarely say 'do this instead'. The reference files are too granular. 11 files for a skill that most agents will use for single tasks is cognitive overhead. Collapse them: structure + main class + autoload can be one file. Controllers + routing can be one file. Hooks + widgets can stay. Services + DI can be one file. Config pages + forms can be one file. That's 5-6 files, not 11. The task routing table should be a decision tree, not a flat map. 'Does the module need external I/O? → yes → external-apis. Does it need a config page? → yes → config-pages. Does it need hooks? → yes → hooks.' Priority ordering, not a list. The evals need to test the failure mode directly: give the agent a broken module and ask it to find the PrestaShop-specific defects. That's the real task.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is underselling itself. It's positioned as a 'don't guess' reference, but it could be a full PrestaShop 9 module development accelerator. Right now it prevents errors — it doesn't enable speed. Consider what's missing: there's no module skeleton generator pattern, no Composer init template, no example of a complete minimal module that actually works end-to-end. The skill tells you the rules but doesn't give you a running start. Add a 'minimal working module' reference — a complete, copy-pasteable skeleton with a config page, a hook, a service, and a cron endpoint. That's worth more than 10,000 words of rules. Second, the skill ignores the ecosystem. PrestaShop has a module marketplace, a CLI for module management, a specific upgrade path between PS 8 and PS 9, and a community of module developers with conventions beyond the docs. None of this is captured. Third, the skill could be the foundation for a PrestaShop-specific linter or static analysis profile. The 'hard bans' in module-structure.md are exactly the rules a CI check should enforce. Fourth, there's no guidance on multistore — a PrestaShop-specific feature that breaks naive module design. Fifth, the skill ignores the FO/BO theme system entirely. Modern PrestaShop admin uses Vue.js components in some areas. A module that needs a BO integration panel or a FO React component has zero guidance here. The skill should expand from 'reference' to 'development companion'.

🟡 The Outsider

I know nothing about PrestaShop. I read this skill to understand what it's for, and I'm confused. The SKILL.md opens with 'Use this skill for PrestaShop 9 modules' but never explains what a module IS in PrestaShop terms. Is it a plugin? A theme extension? A standalone app? The first sentence says 'not plugins, modules' — but that's jargon to someone who doesn't know the difference. The description says 'module structure, hooks, front/admin controllers' — all undefined terms. The critical rules assume I know what `getContent()`, `Configuration`, `Smarty`, `Twig`, and 'overrides' mean in this context. I don't. The reference map has 11 files with names like 'services-dependency-injection.md' — that's clear enough, but 'hooks-widgets.md' is not. What's a widget in PrestaShop? The task routing table is the most useful part for a newcomer, but it uses terms from the reference files without defining them. 'Create a new module skeleton' → module-structure.md. Good. But what does 'fix structure/autoload/naming' mean? The skill needs a 2-3 sentence glossary or concept map at the top. 'PrestaShop is a PHP e-commerce platform. Modules are extensions that add functionality. Hooks are event listeners. The Back Office (BO) is the admin panel. The Front Office (FO) is the storefront.' That's all. Without it, the skill is only useful to people who already know PrestaShop — which defeats the purpose of preventing AI hallucinations, because the AI is exactly the person who doesn't know PrestaShop.

🟠 The Executor

Here's what I'd do in the next 30 minutes. First: collapse the 11 reference files into 5. Merge module-structure + main-module-class into 'module-basics.md'. Merge controllers-routing + services-dependency-injection into 'architecture.md'. Keep hooks-widgets, config-pages, and external-apis-cron as-is. Merge data-persistence + translations-templates-assets into 'data-and-ui.md'. Keep testing-release-checklist and prestashop-9-gotchas. That cuts navigation overhead in half. Second: add one complete, minimal working example to the skill root — a `minimal-module-example/` directory with a real `composer.json`, a real main module file, a real `config/services.yml`, and a real hook implementation. The skill tells you the rules but never shows what 'right' looks like in one place. Third: fix the evals. The current evals test whether the agent produces good output from a prompt. That's not the failure mode. The real failure mode is: the agent produces plausible-looking wrong output. The evals should test defect detection — give the agent a broken module and ask 'find the PrestaShop-specific problems.' Fourth: add a 'quick validation' script — a shell script that runs PHP lint, checks for banned namespace patterns, verifies Composer autoload config, and checks hook registration against a known list. The skill has rules but no enforcement. Fifth: add a glossary to SKILL.md. Three sentences. Module, hook, BO, FO, override, widget. That's it. These five changes would take a day and would transform this from a reference document into a development tool.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor. The five concrete changes — collapse references, add a working example, fix evals, add a validation script, add a glossary — are all actionable, prioritized, and address real gaps. The Executor is the only advisor who gives you something you can start doing in the next hour. The 'minimal working example' idea alone would prevent more hallucinations than all the critical rules combined, because it gives the agent a correct pattern to imitate instead of a list of wrong patterns to avoid.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian. The critique of link rot and eval weakness is valid, but the conclusion — that the skill is a 'paperweight' — is lazy. The skill doesn't need link verification infrastructure; it needs a `last_verified` update process and a note that agents should verify URLs before following them. The 'task routing is naive' complaint ignores that the routing table is a starting point, not an oracle. Real tasks hitting multiple files is fine — the agent reads the relevant sections. The Contrarian identifies real problems and then declares the whole thing worthless, which is the opposite of useful.

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed the skill's interaction with other skills in the ecosystem. This skill exists alongside `php`, `symfony`, `composer`, and potentially `docker` skills. There's no guidance on skill precedence or conflict resolution. If an agent has both `prestashop` and `symfony` loaded, which wins for controller patterns? The skill should declare its boundaries relative to other skills.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker. The reframing from 'reference document' to 'prevention of confident wrongness' cuts through the noise. The three-part architecture — closed-world assertion, lookup mechanism, verification step — is the clearest structural analysis. The decision-tree routing idea is better than the current flat table because it mirrors how agents actually work: they have a task, they need to know which reference applies, and they need priority ordering when multiple apply. The eval redesign — testing defect detection instead of code generation — is the single most impactful suggestion in the entire council.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist. The suggestions are exciting — skeleton generator, marketplace guidance, CI linter, Vue.js components — but they're scope creep. The skill's job is to prevent AI hallucinations about PrestaShop internals. Adding ecosystem coverage, marketplace conventions, and frontend framework guidance turns a focused reference into a PrestaShop encyclopedia. The multistore point is valid and should be added. The rest should be deferred or split into a separate skill.

What All Missed

No one addressed the skill's handling of error scenarios. What should an agent do when it encounters a PrestaShop pattern not covered by the skill? The skill says 'verify the hook exists and confirm the contract' but doesn't say what to do when verification fails. Should the agent stop and ask the user? Should it refuse to implement? Should it implement with a warning? This gap will cause either over-cautious blocking or silent guessing — both bad.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider. The observation that the skill never defines its own terms is damning and correct. A skill designed to prevent AI hallucinations that assumes the AI already knows what a 'module', 'hook', 'BO', and 'widget' are is self-defeating. The 2-3 sentence glossary is the lowest-effort, highest-impact improvement possible. The Executor also identified this, but the Outsider framed it as a design failure rather than a missing feature, which is the right framing.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker. The 'collapse to 5 files' recommendation is sound, but the specific merges are questionable. Merging controllers + routing + services + DI into 'architecture.md' puts two very different concerns — HTTP handling and service container configuration — in one file. The agent looking for routing guidance shouldn't have to scroll past DI configuration. The merges should follow the agent's task flow, not the framework's architecture layers.

What All Missed

No one discussed the skill's `scope: prestashop` and `target_versions: PrestaShop 9.x, PHP 8.2+` frontmatter. What happens when PrestaShop 10 ships? What happens when PHP 9.2 becomes required? The skill has no deprecation or migration strategy. The `last_verified` field exists but there's no process around it. This is a maintenance time bomb.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Executor, specifically the validation script idea. The skill has rules but no enforcement mechanism. A shell script that checks for banned namespace patterns (`PrestaShopBundle\*`, `Symfony\Component\*` inside modules), verifies `prepend-autoloader: false`, checks that hook methods match registrations, and validates Composer autoload config would catch the exact failures the skill describes. This turns the skill from advisory to mechanical. The agent can run the script and get a pass/fail instead of trying to remember 15 critical rules.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist. The 'minimal working example' directory is a good idea, but the Expansionist's version — 'a complete skeleton with config page, hook, service, and cron endpoint' — is too complex for a minimal example. A minimal example should be the smallest possible correct module: a main file, a hook, and a template. That's it. Adding config pages, services, and cron endpoints makes it a reference module, not a minimal example, and blurs the line between 'example' and 'tutorial'.

What All Missed

No one addressed the skill's treatment of the `getContent()` method. The critical rules say 'should normally redirect to the modern config route' but the reference file likely has more detail. This is the single most common point of confusion for developers migrating from PS 8 to PS 9, and it deserves a prominent callout in SKILL.md itself, not just in a reference file. The skill should say: 'If you're building a config page in PS9, you are probably doing it wrong if you're writing HTML in getContent().'

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Contrarian, surprisingly. The link rot concern is real and unaddressed by any other advisor. External doc links in reference files are load-bearing — if they 404, the agent has no fallback and will guess. The skill should either (a) inline the critical information so it doesn't depend on external links, or (b) add a note that agents must verify URLs before following them and stop if a link is dead. The eval weakness is also correctly identified — expectations that can't be mechanically scored are not evals, they're vibes.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider. The glossary idea is good, but 2-3 sentences is too short. A glossary that defines 'module', 'hook', 'BO', 'FO', 'override', 'widget', 'service', 'configuration', and 'tab' with one sentence each would be 10 lines — still short, but actually useful. The Outsider's version leaves out critical terms like 'service' and 'tab' that appear throughout the skill without definition.

What All Missed

No one discussed the skill's relationship to the `skill-creator` skill. The evals are weak, the structure could be better, and the skill-creator skill has guidance on eval design, benchmarking, and skill optimization. The council should have been told to consult the skill-creator skill for eval improvement patterns. This is a meta-gap: the skill about how to build skills wasn't applied to the skill being debated.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council agrees on four things. First, the 11 reference files should be collapsed — the current granularity creates navigation overhead without proportional value. Second, the skill needs a minimal working example of a correct module, not just rules about wrong patterns. Third, the evals are too soft and should test defect detection rather than code generation. Fourth, the skill needs a glossary — terms like module, hook, BO, FO, and widget are used without definition, which undermines the skill's purpose of preventing hallucinations.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on scope. The Expansionist wants to grow the skill into a full development companion with marketplace guidance, CI linters, and frontend framework coverage. The First Principles Thinker and Executor want to sharpen and focus it. The Contrarian wants to declare it broken and rebuild. The resolution: the skill should stay focused on preventing AI hallucinations about PrestaShop internals. Multistore guidance and the `getContent()` callout are in scope. Marketplace conventions, Vue.js components, and CI infrastructure are out of scope — they belong in separate skills or future work. The council also clashes on reference file organization: the First Principles Thinker wants 5 files merged by concern, the Executor wants 5 files merged by task flow. The task-flow merge wins — agents navigate by 'what am I trying to do', not 'what framework layer am I touching'.

Blind Spots Caught

Three blind spots caught by peer reviews. (1) No error-handling guidance for when the agent encounters a pattern not covered by the skill — should it stop, ask, or refuse? (2) No skill boundary declaration relative to `symfony`, `php`, `composer`, and `docker` skills that may be co-loaded. (3) No maintenance strategy for when PrestaShop 10 ships or PHP requirements change — the `last_verified` field exists but has no update process.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill in three phases. Phase 1 (immediate): collapse 11 reference files to 5 organized by task flow, add a 10-line glossary to SKILL.md, add a `getContent()` callout to the critical rules, and add a 'what to do when stuck' rule (stop and ask the user). Phase 2 (short-term): add a minimal working example module at the skill root, rewrite evals to test defect detection, and add a validation script that checks for banned patterns mechanically. Phase 3 (maintenance): add a skill boundary note relative to `symfony`/`php`/`composer`, add a `last_verified` update process, and add multistore guidance to the data reference file.

First Step

Add a glossary to SKILL.md. Ten lines. Define: module, hook, BO, FO, override, widget, service, configuration, tab, getContent(). This is the lowest-effort, highest-impact change. It directly addresses the Outsider's core finding that the skill assumes knowledge it's supposed to provide. Every other improvement builds on this foundation — an agent that doesn't know what a hook is cannot follow rules about hooks.

20 / 26

prototype

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/prototype/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the prototype skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

The two-branch split is the skill's biggest liability. The branch decision — logic vs. UI — is presented as binary, but real questions are messy. Someone asks "I want to see what the checkout flow feels like" and you need both state machine prototyping AND UI variants. The skill forces a premature commitment to one axis of exploration. The fallback heuristic (backend module → logic, page → UI) is naive — a checkout flow question could start at either end."

"One command to run" is a nice ideal but conflates two very different feasibility levels. A terminal script is easy to make one-command. A UI prototype inside an existing Django route with auth, DB, and session state is almost never "one command" unless the project already has `make dev` wired up. The skill should address this gap honestly instead of pretending it's the same effort."

The floating bottom bar example hardcodes inline styles. This is a skill that gets shipped to thousands of different projects with different CSS frameworks. The Django template example is framework-specific in a skill that claims to be framework-agnostic. Either commit to framework-specific references or make the examples abstract enough to port."

The "skip the polish" rule directly conflicts with making prototypes embedded in real pages — you can't embed half-styled chaos in a production Django template without the existing CSS swallowing it. The skill doesn't address this tension."

Finally, the delete-or-absorb section is dangerously lax. "A quick conversation" to capture the answer will never happen reliably. The skill needs a concrete, hard-to-skip capture mechanism — a required NOTES.md template, or an explicit prompt the agent asks the user before allowing itself to mark the task done.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

What is a prototype actually for? It's a falsification tool. You have a hypothesis ("this state machine handles edge cases" or "this layout communicates the right priority") and you need to cheaply test it before committing to production code. The skill should start from this framing — not "pick a branch" but "state your hypothesis." The current text says "a prototype is throwaway code that answers a question," which is correct but underdeveloped. The question is a hypothesis, and the hypothesis should be falsifiable."

From this foundation, several structural changes follow. First, the question/hypothesis should always be written down before any prototype code exists — even as a comment. The logic reference has this ("State the question") but the UI reference buries it and calls it "write down the plan in one line," which is weaker. The skill body itself should mandate this step, not delegate it to references."

Second, the prototype's success criteria should be tied to the hypothesis. "Did this prototype falsify or validate the hypothesis?" is the only thing that matters. The current skill captures this implicitly ("when the prototype has answered its question") but never makes it explicit. The agent (or user) has no way to tell when they're done."

Third, the isolation principle in the logic branch ("put the logic behind a pure interface") is the most valuable sentence in the entire skill. It should be elevated to the top-level rules, not buried in a reference file. The same principle applies to UI prototypes — the winning variant's structure should be separable from the throwaway switcher. Making extraction explicit would prevent prototypes from calcifying in place."

The real architectural question is: does this skill need to exist as one skill, or is it two skills sharing a preamble? The branch decision is the only thing keeping them together, and it's not enough of a workflow to justify the coupling.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a much larger opportunity than it realizes. Prototypes are the highest-leverage artifact in any codebase — they're where architects, PMs, and stakeholders align before expensive implementation. The current skill treats prototypes as an internal dev tool. That's leaving tons of value on the table."

Consider: a UI prototype route could also serve as a stakeholder demo. Add a "presentation mode" — hide the variant bar, lock to one variant, and let a non-dev click through what looks like a real feature. This turns throwaway code into a product validation tool with zero extra effort."

The logic prototype could be extended to generate test cases as output. If the TUI exercises the state machine through all the interesting transitions, capture that sequence. When you delete the TUI, you get a property-based test suite for free — validated transitions that become the test backbone of the real implementation. Nobody does this today because nobody thinks to instrument a throwaway tool."

Third, the skill should explicitly mention composite prototyping. Real questions span logic AND UI. The skill currently treats this as ambiguous-edge-case territory. Instead, it should say: "if you need both, start with logic. Validate the state model. Then wrap the validated logic in a UI prototype." That's a staged prototype workflow, and it's how senior engineers actually work."

Finally, the capture mechanism could be richer. Instead of just NOTES.md, the skill could generate a lightweight decision record — question, approach, variants tested, winner, rationale — that feeds directly into an ADR or design doc. This makes prototypes a first-class part of the project's decision trail, not just disposable experiments.

🟡 The Outsider

I just read this skill and I'm confused about several things. First, the description says 'Build a throwaway prototype to flesh out a design — either a runnable terminal app ... or several radically different UI variations.' But the frontmatter has `disable-model-invocation: true`. So when does this skill actually get used? If it never triggers automatically, the description is decorative. That's confusing — either the skill should trigger on prototype-related requests, or the description should explain how it's invoked manually."

The branch decision is presented as the first thing to do, but the guidance for making it is thin. "Identify which question is being answered — from the user's prompt, the surrounding code, or by asking if the user is around." What if the user's prompt is vague? What if the surrounding code is ambiguous? The skill says "if the question is genuinely ambiguous and the user isn't reachable, default to whichever branch better matches the surrounding code" — but then gives a heuristic (backend → logic, page → UI) that doesn't cover the common case of "I'm building a new feature and I don't know what it should look like OR how the state should work." That's not ambiguous — it's genuinely both."

The rules section says "locate the prototype code close to where it will actually be used" but also "name it so a casual reader can see it's a prototype." These are in tension. If I put `prototype_checkout.py` next to `checkout.py`, is that clear enough? What naming convention should I use? The skill doesn't say."

The UI reference has a Django-specific code example (views, templates) in what's supposed to be a framework-agnostic skill. If I'm working on a Next.js or Rails project, this example is noise. The skill should either be framework-specific or show abstract patterns."

The "When done" section says "if the user is around, that capture is a quick conversation; if not, leave the placeholder." This is the weakest possible capture mechanism. A conversation that may not happen and a placeholder that will rot. There's no structure to ensure the knowledge survives.

🟠 The Executor

The highest-impact improvement is also the simplest: add a concrete NOTES.md template to the skill body. Right now the capture mechanism is hand-wavy — "commit message, ADR, issue, or NOTES.md" — which means it'll be whatever the agent remembers to do, which is nothing. A required template with fields (Question, Approach, Answer, Confidence, Next Steps) forces the capture to happen and makes it useful later. This is a 20-line addition that prevents 100% of the "what was this prototype for?" confusion."

Second, the branch decision needs a flowchart or decision tree, not prose. The current text is a paragraph that the agent will skim and guess. A simple decision tree — "Is the question about visual layout or information hierarchy? → UI. Is the question about state transitions or data shapes? → Logic. Is it both? → Start with logic, then UI." — would eliminate the ambiguity that causes wasted prototypes."

Third, the UI reference's Django code example should be replaced with a framework-agnostic pattern description, or the skill should explicitly say "adapt this to your framework." As written, a Next.js developer will either ignore the example or copy Django patterns into their project. Neither is good."

Fourth, the skill should add a concrete "prototype complete" checklist: (1) question stated, (2) prototype built and run, (3) answer captured in NOTES.md, (4) prototype deleted or absorbed. Without this, prototypes linger. I've seen throwaway code survive for months because nobody wrote down that it was done."

The logic reference is actually well-structured — the TUI rendering pattern is specific and actionable. The main thing missing is a concrete example of the "portable module" pattern. One code snippet showing a pure reducer with a TUI shell would make the whole branch click instantly.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker (C) — elevating the isolation/extraction principle to a top-level rule and reframing the question as a falsifiable hypothesis would fundamentally improve how agents use this skill. It addresses the root cause of prototype rot: prototypes that can't be extracted calcify in place.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor (E) — the NOTES.md template and checklist are good tactical fixes but miss the structural problem. The Executor optimizes the current design without questioning whether the two-branch split is the right architecture. The flowchart suggestion is useful but still forces a binary choice on what's often a composite question.

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed the `disable-model-invocation: true` frontmatter field. This is a critical design choice that shapes how the skill is actually used — it means the skill is a reference document, not an auto-triggered workflow. This should be explicitly acknowledged in the skill body so agents (and users) understand the invocation model. Additionally, none addressed the lack of any `evals/` directory or test cases for this skill, which is a gap given that the repo treats evals as part of the skill contract.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The Contrarian (A) — the critique of the Django-specific example in a framework-agnostic skill is the most actionable structural flaw identified. It's a concrete defect that will cause real confusion for non-Django users. The observation about the "skip the polish" rule conflicting with embedded UI prototypes is also sharp — that's a genuine contradiction the skill needs to resolve.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist (D) — the stakeholder demo and test-case-generation ideas are creative but scope creep. The skill is already trying to cover two branches; adding presentation mode and test instrumentation risks making it unwieldy. The composite prototyping suggestion is the only one that directly addresses a real gap, but it's buried in a response that's mostly blue-sky thinking.

What All Missed

No advisor addressed the skill's handling of the "user is AFK" case systematically. The skill repeatedly says "if the user is around" vs. "if not" but never provides a structured protocol for the unattended case. An agent running autonomously needs explicit instructions for what to do when there's no human to ask — not just "state the assumption at the top." The skill should define a default behavior for fully autonomous prototype runs.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Executor (E) — the NOTES.md template and completion checklist are the highest-leverage, lowest-effort improvements available. These are the kind of concrete, implementable changes that prevent the most common failure mode: prototypes that answer their question but leave no trace. The flowchart suggestion for the branch decision is also practical and directly addresses the ambiguity problem.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker (C) — the suggestion to split into two separate skills is interesting but impractical for a skill that's already shipped and installed. The shared preamble (rules 1-6) is genuinely shared, and splitting would force duplication or cross-references. The "one obvious way" principle from the project's own AGENTS.md argues against splitting unless the divergence is severe.

What All Missed

None of the advisors examined the reference file structure itself. The logic.md reference is well-organized with clear sections, but ui.md buries the sub-shape decision (A vs B) in the middle of the document rather than making it the first decision point. The ui.md should lead with "which sub-shape?" the same way logic.md leads with "state the question." This structural asymmetry between the two references creates an inconsistent experience.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Outsider (B) — the naming convention gap is a real and overlooked problem. The skill says "name it so a casual reader can see it's a prototype" but gives no convention. This matters because inconsistent naming is how prototypes leak into production. The question about `disable-model-invocation: true` is also critical — if the skill never auto-triggers, the description's specificity is wasted, and the skill body should explain the manual invocation path.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian (A) — the "one command to run" critique is overstated. The skill already says "whatever the project's existing task runner supports," which is appropriately flexible. The real problem isn't the rule — it's that the UI branch doesn't address the bootstrapping cost of setting up a route in an existing framework. That's a different issue than "one command."

What All Missed

No advisor discussed the skill's handling of multi-user or collaborative scenarios. If an agent builds a UI prototype on a shared dev server, other developers might see it. The skill should address whether prototypes should be gated (feature flags, auth checks, localhost-only) to prevent confusion in team environments. This is especially relevant for the UI branch where a prototype route could be hit by a real user.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Expansionist (D) — the composite prototyping suggestion ("start with logic, then wrap in UI") is the most valuable idea in the entire debate. It resolves the false binary at the heart of the skill and provides a staged workflow that matches how experienced engineers actually explore uncertain design spaces. This single addition would eliminate the most common misuse of the skill.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor (E) — the checklist and template suggestions, while useful, are process scaffolding that doesn't address the skill's core intellectual gap: helping the agent (or user) figure out what question to ask. The Executor optimizes the execution of a decision without improving the decision itself. A perfect process for the wrong prototype is still waste.

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed the skill's relationship to the project's testing rules. The global AGENTS.md mandates TDD and has strict rules about test coverage. The prototype skill explicitly says "no tests" — this is a deliberate exception, but it's never justified or bounded. The skill should clarify the boundary: when does the "no tests" exemption end? When the prototype is absorbed into production code, tests become mandatory. Making this transition explicit would prevent the "no tests" rule from bleeding into production code.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council broadly agrees on three points: (1) the capture mechanism is dangerously weak and needs a concrete, hard-to-skip template (NOTES.md or similar) with required fields; (2) the branch decision needs to be more structured — either a flowchart or explicit decision tree — and should address the composite case (logic + UI) rather than treating it as an edge case; (3) the Django-specific code example in the UI reference is a defect in a framework-agnostic skill and must be replaced with framework-agnostic patterns or explicit adaptation guidance.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether the two-branch architecture is sound. The Contrarian and First Principles Thinker see the binary split as a fundamental flaw, while the Executor and Outsider treat it as a fixable UX problem. The Expansionist splits the difference by proposing a staged workflow that uses both branches sequentially. There's also tension between the Executor's tactical approach (templates, checklists) and the Expansionist's strategic vision (stakeholder demos, test generation) — both are valid but pull the skill in different directions.

Blind Spots Caught

The council collectively missed three things: (1) the `disable-model-invocation: true` frontmatter and its implications for how the skill is actually used — this should be explained in the skill body; (2) the lack of `evals/` for this skill despite the repo treating evals as part of the skill contract; (3) the testing boundary — the skill says "no tests" but never clarifies when that exemption expires and TDD rules kick back in. Additionally, no advisor addressed team/collaborative scenarios where a prototype on a shared server could be seen by non-devs.

The Recommendation

Elevate the isolation/extraction principle to a top-level rule. Add a required NOTES.md template with fields (Question, Hypothesis, Approach, Answer, Confidence, Next Steps). Replace the Django-specific example with a framework-agnostic pattern. Add a decision tree for the branch choice that includes the composite case ("start with logic, then UI"). Add a "prototype complete" checklist. Clarify the testing boundary: no tests during prototyping, but tests are mandatory when absorbing into production. Add a brief note explaining the `disable-model-invocation: true` invocation model. These changes are additive — none require removing existing content.

First Step

Add the NOTES.md template to the skill body. This is the single highest-leverage change: it's 15-20 lines, it's purely additive, and it directly prevents the most common failure mode (prototypes that answer their question but leave no trace). The template should be a required artifact — the agent should not consider the prototype complete without it.

21 / 26

python-async

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/python-async/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the python-async skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill is competent but dangerously overconfident in its own structure. The biggest flaw: it has 11 reference files and a decision matrix, yet zero runnable code examples anywhere. Every reference file says 'verified from the docs' but shows no actual code. An agent reading `structured-concurrency.md` learns that `TaskGroup.start()` exists but never sees a single `async with` block. That's a documentation smell, not a skill.

The description triggers on `asyncio.gather` and `create_task` — two of the most common Python keywords in existence. This will fire on nearly any Python project that touches async, even trivially. The scope is too broad for the specificity it promises. A project using `asyncio.sleep(0.1)` in a test doesn't need 11 reference files on structured concurrency.

The `target_versions` field claims Python 3.11-3.14, but 3.14 is still in alpha/beta. Making compatibility claims against unreleased Python versions is irresponsible for a skill that positions itself as authoritative. Same problem in every single reference file — they all repeat the same version range boilerplate, creating a maintenance burden and a false precision.

The `last_verified` date is 2026-03-25 across all files. That's fine for a snapshot, but there's no mechanism described for how these get updated. If AnyIO 5.0 ships with breaking changes, every file is silently wrong.

Rule 11 says 'Prefer repo-local references before external source spelunking' — this is vague hand-waving. What counts as 'normal runs'? When is spelunking justified? This rule will either be ignored or used to justify not checking actual installed behavior.

The `code-review-checklist.md` is the weakest reference file. It's just five sections of questions with no guidance on how to answer them. 'Who owns each spawned task?' is a great question — but the file doesn't tell you what a good answer looks like. It's a checklist without a standard.

Finally, the skill claims to own 'async streams' but `streams-synchronization.md` only covers AnyIO memory object streams. What about `async for` on async generators? What about `aiofiles`? What about `asyncio.StreamReader/StreamWriter` for TCP? The coverage gap between the claimed scope and actual content is a liability.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away the structure. What is this skill actually for? It exists so that when an agent encounters async Python code, it makes fewer mistakes about task lifetime, cancellation, and backend portability. That's the job. Everything else is scaffolding.

From that lens, the core question is: does the skill encode the minimum set of invariants that prevent the most common async bugs? The answer is partially yes. The 12 Critical Rules in SKILL.md are genuinely good — rules 1-12 capture the high-value invariants (default to AnyIO, structured concurrency, re-raise cancellation, no nested runners, explicit thread offloading, etc.). These are the rules that, if followed, prevent 80% of async bugs.

But the skill buries these under a complex routing system. An agent has to: (1) classify the task, (2) check the decision matrix, (3) pick a primary reference, (4) optionally pick a secondary reference, (5) state the portability mode, (6) name the files used, (7) explain lifecycle constraints, (8) name thread-crossing functions separately, (9) state verification steps, and (10) call out the highest-risk footgun. That's 10 output expectations for a code fix. This is academic overhead, not practical guidance.

The decision matrix is the right idea but the wrong granularity. The real decision is binary for 90% of cases: 'Is this portable library/app code or framework-owned bootstrap?' If portable → AnyIO. If framework → pair with framework skill. The six-row matrix with escalation columns adds cognitive load without adding decision quality.

What's actually missing from first principles: the skill never defines what 'correct async code' looks like in a minimal complete example. There's no canonical 'here's a well-structured async function' snippet. The skill teaches rules but not patterns. A single reference file with 5-10 annotated code examples showing correct vs incorrect patterns would be worth more than the current 11 files of prose.

The thread-boundaries reference is the strongest file because it actually explains the callable split between `from_thread.run()` and `from_thread.run_sync()` with concrete examples. That's what every reference file should look like — specific, example-driven, and opinionated.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is well-built for its current scope, but it's leaving enormous value on the table by being purely reactive and reference-only. Here's what could make it dramatically more useful:

**1. Pattern library.** The skill should include a `references/patterns.md` file with copy-paste-ready patterns for the 10 most common async tasks: a robust background worker with shutdown, a producer-consumer with backpressure, a thread-offload wrapper, a timeout-protected client call, a startup handshake, etc. These patterns would make the skill generative, not just advisory.

**2. Migration guides.** The biggest real-world async pain point isn't writing new code — it's migrating old `asyncio` code to AnyIO, or fixing code that mixes paradigms. A `references/migration.md` file covering common migration patterns (asyncio → AnyIO, callback-based → async, sync → async) would address the most frequent real-world task.

**3. Performance section.** The skill mentions `uvloop` but has nothing about async performance profiling, identifying event-loop blocking, or measuring async throughput. A `references/performance.md` file covering `asyncio.run()` profiling, loop monitoring, and common performance anti-patterns would fill a genuine gap.

**4. Integration patterns.** The skill explicitly defers to framework skills, but it should at least list the known integration points: how AnyIO pairs with Django async views, FastAPI/Starlette, Litestar, etc. A one-page integration map would prevent agents from giving contradictory advice at framework boundaries.

**5. Error catalog.** A `references/common-errors.md` file cataloging the 15 most common async errors (with tracebacks, root causes, and fixes) would make this skill invaluable for debugging — which is where agents spend most of their async time.

**6. The skill should trigger on more specific patterns.** Currently it triggers on `async def` and `create_task` — too broad. It should also explicitly trigger on `asyncio.gather`, `asyncio.wait`, `asyncio.wait_for`, `asyncio.shield`, `asyncio.Queue`, `anyio.create_task_group`, `anyio.to_thread`, `anyio.from_thread`, `trio.run`, `trio.open_nursery`, and common anti-patterns like bare `except CancelledError: pass`. The description should also mention debugging scenarios explicitly.

🟡 The Outsider

I know Python but I've never used AnyIO or Trio. I'm reading this skill to figure out how to write correct async Python. Here's my experience:

The SKILL.md frontmatter is fine — I understand what it covers and when to use it. The description is clear. But the moment I hit the 'Quick Start' section, I'm lost. Step 1 says 'Classify the task: portable library logic, app runtime/bootstrap, backend-native code, or framework-owned lifecycle.' I don't know what these categories mean in practice. What's the difference between 'portable library logic' and 'app business logic'? The decision matrix is supposed to help, but it has the same four categories with no examples.

The Critical Rules section is the most useful part of the entire skill. Rule 1 ('Default to AnyIO for portable logic') is exactly what I need. Rule 8 ('Offload blocking work explicitly') is critical and well-stated. But Rule 11 ('Prefer repo-local references before external source spelunking') means nothing to me. What's 'spelunking'? What are 'normal runs'?

The reference map is organized by file and domain, but there's no indication of which files are most important. A newcomer should be told: 'Start with structured-concurrency.md and cancellation-timeouts.md — these cover 80% of what you need.' Instead, all 11 files look equally weighted.

The reference files themselves are well-written but assume too much prior knowledge. `structured-concurrency.md` talks about `TaskGroup.start()` and `task_status.started()` without showing what this looks like in code. `cancellation-timeouts.md` has a comparison table between AnyIO/Trio and asyncio cancellation but no code showing what 'level cancellation' actually does to a running coroutine.

The biggest gap for an outsider: there's no glossary. Terms like 'structured concurrency', 'cancel scope', 'memory object stream', 'capacity limiter', 'blocking portal', and 'checkpoint' are used without definition. A short glossary file or a 'concepts' reference would make this skill accessible instead of insider-only.

Also, the `target_versions` claim Python 3.11-3.14. I'm on 3.12. Is this skill safe for me? The version range is so wide I can't tell if the advice applies to my runtime. More precise version-specific callouts would help.

🟠 The Executor

I need to improve this skill fast. Here's what I'd actually do, in order of impact:

**First: Add code examples to every reference file.** This is the single highest-impact change. Every reference file currently describes behavior in prose. Each one needs at minimum 2-3 code snippets showing correct usage and 1 anti-pattern. The `threads-boundaries.md` file already does this partially with its `from_thread.run()` vs `from_thread.run_sync()` examples — use that as the template. Without code, agents will paraphrase the prose and get details wrong. This is the #1 source of potential hallucination.

**Second: Add a glossary reference file.** Create `references/glossary.md` defining: structured concurrency, cancel scope, checkpoint, memory object stream, capacity limiter, blocking portal, task group, nursery, level cancellation, edge cancellation, eager start, and fire-and-forget. One paragraph each, with a code snippet where applicable. This fixes the accessibility problem and gives agents precise terminology.

**Third: Fix the version claims.** Python 3.14 is not stable. Change `target_versions` to 'Python 3.11-3.13' with a note that 3.14 support is pending verification. Do this in SKILL.md and all 11 reference files. This is a 5-minute fix that prevents the skill from making false claims.

**Fourth: Strengthen the code-review-checklist.md.** Add a 'what good looks like' column to each review question. For 'Who owns each spawned task?' add: 'Good: the spawning context manager or class has a cancel/shutdown path. Bad: create_task with no stored reference and no cancellation path.' This turns the checklist from a list of worries into an actionable tool.

**Fifth: Simplify the Output Expectations section.** The current 10-item output requirement is unrealistic for small tasks. Add a tiered system: for simple answers, state the portability mode and the highest-risk footgun. For complex answers, use the full format. This prevents the skill from making every async question feel like a thesis defense.

Total estimated time: 2-3 hours for all five changes. The code examples are 80% of the value.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

Response E (The Executor) is strongest because it's the only one that ranks improvements by impact and estimates effort. 'Add code examples to every reference file' is the correct first priority — every other improvement is secondary to making the skill actually executable. The tiered output expectations suggestion is also pragmatically correct; the current 10-item output requirement will either be ignored or will make simple tasks unbearably verbose.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response B (The First Principles Thinker) has the biggest blind spot. It correctly identifies that the 12 Critical Rules capture 80% of async bug prevention, but then dismisses the entire reference architecture as 'academic overhead.' The reference files exist because different tasks need different depth. A cancellation bug needs `cancellation-timeouts.md`, not a re-read of the 12 rules. The routing system is the skill's actual value — the rules are the quick reference, and the files are the deep reference. B wants to collapse this into a single patterns file, which would lose the just-in-time depth that makes the skill work.

What All Missed

All five responses miss that the skill has no coverage of async context managers and async generators as types — not as patterns, but as things that can go wrong. `__aenter__`/`__aexit__` cancellation behavior, async generator cleanup with `athrow()` and `aclose()`, and the interaction between `async for` and cancel scopes are real bugs that agents will encounter. The streams-synchronization.md file mentions 'yielding from async generators across cancel scopes' as a footgun but never explains it. This is a content gap that none of the advisors caught.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

Response A (The Contrarian) is strongest because it identifies the most concrete, verifiable flaws: zero code examples across 11 files, over-broad triggering on `async def`/`create_task`, and false precision in version claims. The observation that `code-review-checklist.md` asks questions without defining acceptable answers is particularly sharp — it's a checklist that can't be graded. These are defects, not opinions.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response C (The Expansionist) has the biggest blind spot. It proposes six new reference files (patterns, migration, performance, integration map, error catalog, plus trigger expansion). The skill already has 11 files and the Executor correctly identifies that the existing files lack code examples. Adding six more empty-prose files would make the skill worse, not better. C's suggestions are good ideas for a future phase, but recommending them without acknowledging the existing quality gap is building a second floor on a house with no foundation.

What All Missed

All five responses miss the complete absence of `asyncio.Runner` coverage in the main skill. The `backend-asyncio.md` file mentions it once in passing, but there's no guidance on when to use `asyncio.Runner` vs `asyncio.run()`, which is a real decision point for applications that need multiple top-level async calls or shared loop contexts. The uvloop file mentions it, but the main routing doesn't point to it. This is a routing gap in the Task Routing section.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

Response D (The Outsider) is strongest because it reveals the skill's biggest structural problem: it's written for people who already understand async Python. The request for a glossary is obvious in hindsight and shocking that it doesn't exist. The observation that all 11 reference files 'look equally weighted' to a newcomer is also critical — the skill needs a reading path, not just a map. A 'start here' recommendation for common vs advanced files would cost nothing and help everyone.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response E (The Executor) has the biggest blind spot. Its five improvements are all correct but conservative. It doesn't challenge any structural assumptions. The Executor would spend 2-3 hours adding code examples and a glossary, and the skill would be better but still fundamentally insider-oriented and example-poor at the SKILL.md level. The Executor's instinct to 'fix the version claims in 5 minutes' is the right energy but the wrong priority — version claims don't cause bugs, missing code examples do.

What All Missed

All five responses miss that the skill has no guidance on `asyncio.eager_task_factory` and eager task execution semantics beyond a single mention in `backend-asyncio.md`. Eager tasks change execution order and cancellation behavior, and they're increasingly relevant as Python moves toward default eager execution in some contexts. This is a growing gap that will become more important with Python 3.14+.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

Response C (The Expansionist) is strongest because it's the only advisor that looks beyond fixing existing content to ask what the skill should become. The pattern library idea is genuinely excellent — a `references/patterns.md` with 10 copy-paste-ready async patterns would transform this skill from reference to tool. The error catalog idea is equally strong: a curated list of common async errors with tracebacks and fixes is exactly what agents need during debugging, which is the highest-frequency async task.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response A (The Contrarian) has the biggest blind spot. It criticizes the skill for having 'zero runnable code examples' but doesn't acknowledge that the skill is a SKILL.md — a markdown instruction file for an agent, not a tutorial. The code examples belong in reference files, and the Contrarian is right that they're missing, but the framing that this makes the skill 'dangerously overconfident' is overstated. The skill is incomplete, not dangerous. The version claim criticism is valid though — 3.14 support is genuinely unverified.

What All Missed

All five responses miss that the skill doesn't address `anyio.from_thread.check_cancelled()` in the main SKILL.md Critical Rules. It's mentioned in `threads-boundaries.md` but it's a critical API for writing cancellable worker threads, and it deserves a top-level rule. The current Rule 8 about offloading blocking work doesn't mention that offloaded work should be cancellation-aware. This is a safety gap in the most visible part of the skill.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

Response B (The First Principles Thinker) is strongest because it correctly identifies that the 12 Critical Rules are the skill's core value and everything else is support structure. The observation that the Output Expectations section imposes 10 requirements per answer is the most actionable structural criticism in any response. No agent will consistently produce 10-element output for a simple async question, which means the skill either gets ignored or produces bloated responses. Simplifying this would improve compliance more than any content addition.

Biggest Blind Spot

Response D (The Outsider) has the biggest blind spot. It asks for a glossary, which is reasonable, but doesn't acknowledge that Python's official docs already define most of these terms. A glossary is good, but linking to authoritative definitions would be more maintainable than duplicating them. The Outsider's complaint about version ranges ('I'm on 3.12, is this safe?') is valid but the solution isn't more precise version callouts — it's removing version claims from individual rules and stating them once at the top level with clear compatibility notes.

What All Missed

All five responses miss the interaction between `contextvars` and async code. The `threads-boundaries.md` file mentions that AnyIO copies contextvars into worker threads but doesn't explain the implications. Context propagation bugs are among the hardest async bugs to diagnose — a worker thread that expects a context variable to be set but gets the default because the copy happened at the wrong time. This deserves a dedicated section or at minimum a Critical Rule about context propagation across async boundaries.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees on three things: (1) the reference files need code examples — prose-only technical reference is the skill's biggest weakness and the highest-impact fix; (2) the Python 3.14 version claim is unverified and should be narrowed to 3.11-3.13; (3) the code-review-checklist.md needs concrete 'what good looks like' guidance, not just questions. There is also broad agreement that the Output Expectations section is over-engineered and will lead to either non-compliance or bloated responses.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on whether to expand or consolidate. The Expansionist wants six new reference files (patterns, migration, performance, integration map, error catalog, expanded triggers). The Executor and Contrarian implicitly argue for fixing existing content before adding anything new. The First Principles Thinker wants to simplify the routing system; the Outsider wants to add a glossary and reading paths, which adds structure. The Expansionist's suggestions are good ideas but premature — adding more empty-prose files would worsen the very problem everyone else identified.

Blind Spots Caught

The entire council missed three content gaps: (1) async context managers and async generator cleanup (`__aenter__`/`__aexit__`, `athrow()`, `aclose()`); (2) `contextvars` propagation across async/thread boundaries beyond the one-line mention in threads-boundaries.md; (3) `asyncio.Runner` vs `asyncio.run()` decision guidance and eager task factory semantics. These are real-world issues that agents will encounter and the skill doesn't cover.

The Recommendation

Fix existing content before expanding. In priority order: (1) Add 2-3 code examples and 1 anti-pattern to each of the 11 reference files, using threads-boundaries.md as the template. (2) Narrow version claims to Python 3.11-3.13. (3) Add a glossary reference file. (4) Strengthen code-review-checklist.md with 'what good looks like' for each question. (5) Simplify Output Expectations to a tiered system. (6) Add a Critical Rule about context propagation and cancellation-aware worker threads. Only after these are done should new files like patterns.md or error-catalog.md be considered.

First Step

Add code examples to every reference file. Start with structured-concurrency.md and cancellation-timeouts.md since these are the most-referenced files. Each needs at minimum: one correct usage snippet, one anti-pattern snippet, and one 'what goes wrong' explanation. This single change would address the Contrarian's 'zero code' criticism, the Outsider's 'assumes prior knowledge' problem, and the Executor's highest-priority fix simultaneously.
