skill-creator

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/skill-creator/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the skill-creator skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill is a bloated 665-line monster that violates its own progressive-disclosure principle. It tells you to keep SKILL.md under 500 lines, then ships at 665. The hypocrisy is staggering.

The 'Communicating with the user' section is patronizing filler — 'plumbers opening terminals, parents googling npm' — that wastes tokens every single time the skill loads. Nobody needs a sociological essay on the user base. Cut it entirely.

The description-optimization workflow is dangerously over-engineered. It requires `opencode run` via subprocess, a 20-query eval set, an HTML review template, and a 5-iteration loop. Most users will never run this. It's a research project masquerading as a practical step. The skill should offer a simple heuristic-based description review instead, with the heavy automation as an optional advanced path.

The 'Single-agent environment instructions' and 'Cowork-Specific Instructions' sections are near-duplicates that could be one conditional section. Same information, slightly different wording, twice the tokens.

The pressure-testing section is only relevant for discipline skills, yet it takes up enormous space in a skill that's supposed to serve all skill types. It should be a reference file, not inline content.

The Skill Quality Checklist at the end is redundant with the phased workflow described earlier. It repeats RED/GREEN/REFACTOR steps that were already covered. Either integrate it into the workflow or cut it.

The evals.json has only 3 test cases with vague expected_output descriptions like 'The response drives the full skill-creation loop.' That's not measurable. Where are the concrete, binary assertions?

Bottom line: this skill needs a brutal edit. Cut 40% of the content, move pressure testing and advanced workflows to reference files, merge the environment-specific sections, and add real assertions to the evals.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip away everything and ask: what is this skill actually for? It's a meta-skill — a skill about making skills. Its job is to guide an agent through the lifecycle of skill creation: intent → draft → test → iterate → deploy.

The core loop is actually well-defined: interview, baseline (RED), draft (GREEN), evaluate, iterate. That's solid. But everything around it is noise that obscures this loop.

The fundamental problem is that this skill tries to be simultaneously: (1) a tutorial on skill authoring concepts, (2) a step-by-step workflow guide, (3) a testing framework manual, (4) a description optimization tool, and (5) a packaging utility. These are five different concerns crammed into one document.

From first principles, the skill should do one thing: guide the agent through creating a skill. Everything else — the benchmarking infrastructure, the description optimizer, the packaging script — should be tooling that the skill calls, not content the skill contains.

The skill should be a thin orchestration layer: 'Here's the process, here are the tools, here's when to use each tool.' The 45KB of reference files already exist. Reference them. Don't inline their content.

The triggering description is also problematic. It says 'Use when creating new skills, editing existing skills, measuring skill performance, or verifying skills work before deployment.' But 'measuring skill performance' and 'verifying skills work' are vague enough to trigger on almost any eval task. The description should be tighter: focus on the act of authoring and iterating on skill files, not on general evaluation.

The real question this skill should answer in 50 lines or less: 'What is the process for making a skill, and what tools do I use at each step?' Everything else is reference material.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a massive opportunity it completely ignores: it could be the foundation for an entire skill ecosystem. Right now it's a workflow guide. It should be a platform.

First, there's no mention of skill versioning or changelog practices. Skills evolve, but the skill-creator has no guidance on how to version skills, document breaking changes, or manage backward compatibility. As the skill library grows, this becomes critical.

Second, there's no skill dependency or composition model. Real-world skills build on each other — a 'django-testing' skill might depend on both 'django' and 'tdd' skills. The skill-creator should define how to declare skill dependencies, how to compose skills, and how to avoid circular skill references.

Third, the eval framework is primitive. Three test cases with subjective expected outputs. There's no concept of regression testing — when you improve skill A, how do you know you didn't break its compatibility with skill B that references it? A skill graph with cross-skill regression tests would be transformative.

Fourth, the packaging section is an afterthought ('only if present_files tool is available'). Packaging should be a first-class concern with versioning, metadata validation, dependency resolution, and a publish workflow.

Fifth, there's no skill discovery or search guidance. When creating a new skill, how do you check if a similar skill already exists? How do you browse the skill library? The skill-creator should include a 'check before you create' step.

Sixth, the blind comparison system is buried as 'advanced' and 'optional.' A/B testing between skill versions should be a standard part of the iteration loop, not a niche feature.

This skill could orchestrate an entire skill economy. Right now it's just a workflow document.

🟡 The Outsider

I just read this skill from scratch. Here's what confused me:

The very first thing I see after the frontmatter is a high-level process summary, then immediately 'Communicating with the user' which talks about plumbers and grandparents. I have no idea why this is here or who it's for. It feels like an internal memo accidentally left in.

The skill uses terms like 'RED,' 'GREEN,' and 'REFACTOR' as phase labels without ever explicitly stating this is a TDD-inspired pattern. If I don't already know what RED/GREEN/REFACTOR means in this context, I'm lost. The first mention of RED is in parentheses: 'Run baseline scenarios WITHOUT any skill to see what agents get wrong (RED).' That's the definition, but it's buried inside a sentence.

The 'Classify Skill Type' section has a table with four types, but the differences between 'Technique' and 'Pattern' are subtle and the examples don't help much. 'Concrete method with steps' vs 'Mental model for problems' — in practice, how do I tell them apart? I need a decision flowchart or at least a 'if X, then Technique; if Y, then Pattern' guide.

The eval workflow has 5 steps, but steps 1 and 2 happen simultaneously ('spawn all runs' then 'while runs are in progress, draft assertions'). This is confusing because the document presents them linearly. A timeline diagram or explicit 'IN PARALLEL' markers would help.

The 'Cowork-Specific Instructions' section says 'for whatever reason, some environments seem to disincline the model from generating the eval viewer.' This is the writer admitting the skill doesn't work reliably in a specific environment, then just shrugging. That's not a fix.

The reference files are listed at the end, but I already passed sections that referenced them (like `references/testing-pressure-scenarios.md`). I didn't know to read them when they were first mentioned. The skill should say 'READ THIS NOW' when a reference is first needed, not just list everything at the end.

Finally, the skill says 'Use when creating new skills, editing existing skills, measuring skill performance, or verifying skills work before deployment.' I'm a user who wants to benchmark an existing skill. Does this skill apply? 'Measuring skill performance' suggests yes, but the content is overwhelmingly about creation. The scope is unclear.

🟠 The Executor

Here's what I'd fix first, in priority order:

**1. Fix the 665-line violation.** The skill tells you to keep SKILL.md under 500 lines. It's 665. This is the most visible credibility problem. Move the pressure-testing section (which only applies to discipline skills) to a reference file. Merge the single-agent and Cowork sections into one 'Environment Adaptations' section with conditional bullets. Cut the 'Communicating with the user' section entirely — it's fluff. That gets you under 500.

**2. Fix the evals.json assertions.** The expected_output fields are paragraphs of prose. The expectations arrays are better but still vague ('It captures the workflow clearly' — how do you measure 'clearly'?). Write binary, checkable assertions: 'The draft SKILL.md contains a YAML frontmatter with name and description fields' — that's pass/fail. 'evals.json exists and contains at least 2 eval entries' — pass/fail. Make the evals actually evaluable.

**3. Add a 'Quick Start' path.** Most users don't need the full RED/GREEN/REFACTOR/optimize loop. They need: write skill → test it → fix it → ship it. Put a condensed 5-step quick start at the top for experienced users, with a link to the full workflow for those who need it.

**4. Fix the description-optimization section.** It requires `opencode run`, an HTML template, a downloads folder check, and a background process. That's too many failure points. Add a fallback: if `opencode run` isn't available, use a heuristic checklist (contains 'Use when', starts with trigger conditions, no workflow summary, under 1024 chars, includes keywords).

**5. Add concrete examples to the skill type classification.** The Technique vs Pattern distinction is too abstract. Show me a concrete example of each type of skill and walk through the classification decision.

Do these five things and the skill goes from 'comprehensive but unwieldy' to 'practical and trustworthy.' Everything else is polish.

The suggestion to add binary assertions to evals.json is the single most impactful improvement listed across all responses.

You can't declare skill dependencies if the platform doesn't support them. This is building a blueprint for a city that has no ground to stand on.

What All Missed

All responses missed the problem of skill rot. Skills become outdated as the platform evolves, but there's no guidance on maintenance cycles, deprecation, or how to know when a skill needs updating. The skill-creator teaches you how to birth a skill but not how to keep it alive.

Strongest

The prescription — make the skill a thin orchestration layer that references tools rather than containing them — would solve the length problem, the progressive-disclosure violation, and the maintainability problem in one architectural change.

Biggest Blind Spot

All responses missed the complete absence of collaboration guidance. Skills are often created by teams, but there's no mention of how to co-author a skill, how to review someone else's skill draft, or how to handle conflicting feedback from multiple stakeholders. The skill-creator assumes a single agent and a single user.

Strongest

What All Missed

The skill presents a happy path but gives no guidance on what to do when things go wrong, which is exactly when users need the skill most.

A perfectly designed process in an unreadable document is still a failed skill.

A 50-line document can't cover skill authoring, testing, benchmarking, description optimization, and packaging. The right answer is structured progressive disclosure, not radical compression.

What All Missed

All responses missed that the skill has no 'anti-skills' guidance — when should you NOT create a skill? Not every workflow needs to be a skill. Some things are better as prompt templates, some as scripts, some as one-off instructions. The skill-creator should help you decide whether to create a skill at all, not just how to create one.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council overwhelmingly agrees on three things: (1) The 665-line length violates the skill's own guidance and must be reduced, primarily by moving pressure-testing and environment-specific content to reference files. (2) The evals.json has weak, non-binary assertions that undermine the skill's credibility as a teaching tool. (3) The skill conflates multiple concerns (tutorial, workflow, testing manual, optimization tool, packaging guide) that should be separated into an orchestration layer plus reference files.

There is also strong agreement that the 'Communicating with the user' section is filler, the reference-file linking structure is broken (listed at end, needed throughout), and the description-optimization workflow is over-engineered for most users.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on scope. The Expansionist wants to turn this into a skill ecosystem platform with dependency graphs, versioning, and cross-skill regression testing. The Executor wants to fix the top 5 practical problems and ship. The First Principles Thinker wants to radically simplify to a 50-line orchestration layer. These are fundamentally different visions: platform vs. tool vs. manifesto.

The council also clashes on the evals. The Contrarian says the evals are broken and should have binary assertions. The Expansionist says the evals should include cross-skill regression tests. The Executor says make them binary and checkable. The Outsider doesn't mention evals at all, focusing instead on document structure.

Blind Spots Caught

The entire council missed four important issues: (1) No guidance on skill maintenance, deprecation, or versioning over time (skill rot). (2) No guidance on when NOT to create a skill — the skill-creator assumes creation is always the answer. (3) No error-handling or troubleshooting guidance for when the workflow itself fails. (4) No collaboration or co-authoring guidance for team-based skill creation.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill into a thin orchestration document (~300 lines) with clear phase gates, and move all specialized content (pressure testing, environment adaptations, description optimization internals, packaging) to reference files. Fix the evals.json with binary, checkable assertions. Add a decision point at the start: 'Should this be a skill?' with criteria for when the answer is no. Add a maintenance section. Merge single-agent and Cowork into one conditional section. Cut the 'Communicating with the user' section. Add a Quick Start path for experienced users. Fix the reference linking so files are referenced inline when first needed, not just listed at the end.

First Step

Reduce the SKILL.md from 665 lines to under 500 by moving the Pressure Testing section (which only applies to discipline skills) into a reference file, merging the Single-agent and Cowork sections into one 'Environment Adaptations' section, and cutting the 'Communicating with the user' paragraph. This single edit addresses the most credibility-damaging violation (the skill doesn't follow its own rules) and creates space for the structural improvements that follow.

tabler

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/tabler/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the Tabler skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill is a reference dump, not a skill. It's 15 markdown files of raw HTML snippets with almost zero decision guidance. An agent reading this still won't know WHEN to use a card vs a panel, WHEN to reach for a modal vs an offcanvas, or HOW to choose between similar patterns. The 'Component Routing' section is just a file listing — it doesn't route anything. There's no prioritization, no 'start here,' no anti-patterns section telling agents what NOT to do. The color reference in SKILL.md duplicates what's in colors-typography.md, and the size reference duplicates component-level docs. The Bootstrap integration section is a liability — it gives agents license to use any Bootstrap class without understanding Tabler's overrides, which is how you get broken UIs that look almost right. The evals test whether agents use 'real Tabler classes' but the skill itself doesn't define what 'real' means beyond showing examples. There's no versioning strategy — Tabler 2.x is on the horizon and this skill has no migration guidance or even awareness. The 'last_verified' field is theater — it says 2026-03-19 but there's no verification process documented. The skill is structurally complete but functionally shallow: it tells you what the pieces look like but never how to compose them or what can go wrong.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

What problem does this skill actually solve? The agent needs to produce correct Tabler HTML. But 'correct' means three things: (1) valid class names that exist in the framework, (2) proper structural hierarchy so components render correctly, and (3) appropriate component choice for the UX context. This skill only addresses #1, and barely — it lists examples but doesn't define the boundaries of the class namespace. An agent can't tell if `.card-body-lg` exists or not from this skill. The fundamental architecture is wrong: a skill should be a decision tree, not an encyclopedia. The agent's real question is 'I need to show X to the user — what do I build?' not 'show me 400 lines about modals.' The skill should be structured as: (a) a component selection guide (when to use what), (b) a minimal correct template per component, (c) common composition patterns, and (d) a gotchas/anti-patterns section. Everything else — the exhaustive variant lists — should be secondary reference material that the agent only pulls when needed. The current structure front-loads reference material and buries the decision logic. The page-patterns file is the closest thing to useful, but it's buried at the bottom of the routing table. The skill should be inverted: patterns first, reference second.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is leaving enormous value on the table. First, it has zero integration guidance for the actual stack this repo targets — Django, HTMX, and Tabler are used together everywhere, but this skill treats Tabler as a standalone static HTML framework. There's no mention of how Tabler components work with HTMX swap targets, how Django form rendering maps to Tabler's form structure, or how to handle CSRF tokens in Tabler modals. Second, the skill has no accessibility guidance — Tabler has specific ARIA patterns, focus management requirements for modals, and screen-reader considerations that are completely absent. Third, there's no dark mode coverage — Tabler supports it natively and the skill doesn't mention it once. Fourth, the skill could include a 'Tabler class existence checker' — a definitive list of valid classes so agents can validate their output instead of guessing. Fifth, the patterns file covers 8 page types but misses critical ones: login/auth flows, error pages, onboarding wizards, notification centers, and data-heavy admin tables with filtering. Sixth, there's no mention of Tabler's JavaScript API — when to use `data-bs-toggle` vs custom JS initialization, how to programmatically open modals, how to handle toast notifications. The skill could be the definitive bridge between design and implementation for the entire stack, but right now it's just a HTML snippet library.

🟡 The Outsider

I have no context about this project. Reading SKILL.md, I can't tell: (1) what this skill is FOR — is it a reference? A tutorial? A cheat sheet? The description says 'comprehensive reference guide' but the quick start says 'identify the component you need' which implies it's a lookup tool, yet there's no lookup mechanism beyond reading file names. (2) The 'scope: tabler' and 'target_versions: Tabler 1.x' frontmatter means nothing to me — I don't know what Tabler is, and the skill doesn't explain. (3) The routing table lists 14 reference files but gives me no sense of which ones I'll actually need for a typical task. (4) The color reference section lists color names but doesn't show me what they look like or when to use semantic vs theme colors. (5) The Bootstrap integration section is confusing — is Tabler built on Bootstrap or is it a separate framework? The relationship is never explained. (6) There are no links to external documentation, no 'see also' to the official Tabler docs, no way for me to verify if the information is current. (7) The evals test whether the agent uses 'real Tabler classes' but I can't tell from the skill which classes are real vs invented. The skill assumes I already know Tabler well enough to use it, which defeats the purpose of a skill.

🟠 The Executor

The fastest improvement: restructure SKILL.md as a decision guide, not a table of contents. Current SKILL.md is ~120 lines of overview + color/size reference + Bootstrap integration. Replace it with: (1) a 10-line 'if you need X, use Y' component selection table, (2) a 'start with these 3 files' section pointing to page-patterns.md, cards.md, and form-elements.md since those cover 80% of real tasks, (3) a 15-line anti-patterns section covering the most common mistakes (wrong card nesting, missing role attributes, mixing Bootstrap JS with Tabler's API), (4) remove the duplicated color/size reference from SKILL.md — it's already in the reference files. Second: add a 'Tabler + HTMX' integration note since this repo is Django+HTMX+Tabler — even 5 lines about swap targets and modal triggers would prevent the most common integration bugs. Third: add dark mode to colors-typography.md — it's a real feature that agents will need. Fourth: add a 'valid classes' validation section or regex so agents can self-check. Fifth: fix the 'last_verified' process — either automate it or remove it. The current approach of stamping a date with no verification process is worse than no date at all because it creates false confidence. Do these five things and the skill goes from reference dump to actually useful.

Biggest Blind Spot

What All Missed

The skill mixes them without clearly distinguishing which components require JS initialization and which don't. Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees the skill is structurally complete but functionally shallow. B fears bloat; C fears gaps. The resolution is a two-layer structure: a lean decision guide on top with comprehensive reference below. A second clash: C and D want more content (explanations, external links, stack integration) while E wants to focus on the highest-ROI structural changes first. The council should do E's five steps as phase one, then address C and D's suggestions as phase two.

Blind Spots Caught

(2) no mention of responsive design patterns beyond 'use Bootstrap classes' — this causes real UI bugs. (3) no discussion of JS vs CSS component distinction — agents need to know which components require JS initialization. Additionally, no advisor addressed triggering accuracy or the skill's description language, which Reviewer 5 identified as a real problem.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill in two phases. Phase one (immediate): (1) Rewrite SKILL.md as a decision guide with component selection table, anti-patterns section, and 'start with these 3 files' onboarding. (2) Remove duplicated color/size reference from SKILL.md. (3) Add a 15-line Tabler + HTMX integration note. (4) Fix or remove 'last_verified.' (5) Add dark mode coverage to colors-typography.md. Phase two (follow-up): (1) Add accessibility guidance (ARIA patterns, focus management). (2) Add a troubleshooting/common mistakes section. (3) Clarify the JS vs CSS component distinction. (4) Add external doc links and explain Bootstrap relationship in the first paragraph. (5) Expand page patterns to include login/auth flows and error pages. Throughout, maintain the two-layer structure: decision guide on top, comprehensive reference below.

Rewrite SKILL.md as a decision guide. tdd

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/tdd/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can this TDD skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill is dangerously incomplete for anyone who isn't already a TDD practitioner. The 'vertical slice via tracer bullet' advice is correct but presented without any acknowledgment of how hard this is in practice. Where's the guidance on what to do when you can't figure out how to write a test without knowing the implementation? That's the actual hard part of TDD, and it's completely glossed over. The skill says 'write ONE test that confirms ONE thing' but never addresses the paralysis of not knowing what that one thing is when facing a blank file. The anti-pattern section on horizontal slices is the strongest part — it's concrete and visual. But the rest reads like a summary of Kent Beck's book, not a practical guide. The references to AGENTS.md sections (§2, §11, §9.3) are leaky — a skill should be self-contained or explicitly state it's a companion, not scatter section numbers that may change. The 'ask the user what behaviours to test' step is naive — users often don't know what's testable. The skill needs a section on how to decompose a feature into testable units when the user can't articulate the plan themselves. Also, the skill completely ignores the emotional/psychological dimension: TDD is hard, people abandon it when tests feel pointless. Where's the motivation maintenance?

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

What is this skill actually trying to solve? It's trying to change agent behavior from 'write code then tests' to 'write tests then code.' That's a behavioral intervention, not a knowledge transfer problem. The current approach is to explain the philosophy and show examples. But agents don't have habits to break — they follow instructions. So the real question is: what instruction set reliably produces TDD behavior? The skill should be structured as a decision procedure, not a manifesto. Step 1: Given a task, enumerate observable behaviors. Step 2: Pick the simplest. Step 3: Write a failing test for it. Step 4: Write minimal code. Step 5: Commit. Step 6: Repeat. The current skill has this buried in prose. It should be an algorithm. The 'philosophy' section is noise for an agent — agents don't need convincing, they need a procedure. The references to other files (tests.md, mocking.md, refactoring.md) are good but the skill should inline the critical rules rather than requiring file reads. Every context switch is a chance to lose the thread. The skill should also address the 'what if the test is hard to write' escape hatch — that's the moment TDD breaks down, and the skill is silent on it.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a much bigger opportunity than it realizes. TDD isn't just a coding practice — it's a design feedback loop. When tests are hard to write, that's signal that the design is bad. The skill should make this explicit: 'if you can't write a test for it, your interface needs work.' That turns TDD into a design tool, not just a testing technique. The skill could also cover TDD for different contexts: bug fixes (write a test that reproduces the bug first), refactoring (characterization tests before touching legacy code), and exploration (spiking then throwing away the spike, keeping the tests). The 'tracer bullet' concept is great but underdeveloped — it should explain when to use it (new feature in unfamiliar codebase) vs. when to skip it (well-understood domain). The skill should also address test data strategy: fixtures vs. factories vs. inline setup, and when each is appropriate. The mocking reference is solid but the skill should mention test doubles beyond mocks — fakes, stubs, and when each is appropriate. Finally, the skill could include a 'TDD checklist' that agents can literally tick through, making it a tool rather than a document.

🟡 The Outsider

Reading this as someone who doesn't know TDD deeply: I'm confused about when this skill triggers. The description says 'use when the user wants to build features or fix bugs test-first' — but that's almost every task. Does this skill apply every time the agent writes code? If so, it should say that explicitly. If not, what's the boundary? The 'horizontal slices' diagram is helpful but I don't understand why it's wrong just from reading it — the explanation is too abstract. I need a concrete example of a horizontal slice going wrong: 'I wrote 5 tests for a payment flow, then implemented it, and all tests passed but the real flow failed because I misunderstood the API contract.' The references to 'deep modules' and 'codebase-design skill' are confusing — I don't know what a deep module is, and I'm not going to read another skill to understand this one. The skill should define its own terms. Also, the 'Planning' checklist has 7 items before writing any test — that feels like a lot of friction. Is all of that really necessary for a 10-line bug fix? The skill should scale its process to the task size. Finally, the commit-after-every-cycle advice contradicts what most developers do (they commit after a feature is done). I need to understand why committing after each micro-cycle is better, not just be told to do it.

🟠 The Executor

The fastest improvement: make the workflow a numbered checklist, not prose. Agents execute checklists better than they follow philosophical arguments. Current structure: Philosophy → Anti-Pattern → Workflow (4 steps) → Environment → References. Better structure: 1. Pre-flight (3 yes/no checks), 2. Loop (RED→GREEN→COMMIT, repeat), 3. Done (refactor checklist). Cut the philosophy section entirely or move it to a 'Why this works' footnote. The anti-pattern section is good — keep it, but add the positive pattern next to each negative (side-by-side comparison). The 'ask the user to approve the plan' step is a bottleneck — instead, write the first test, show the user, and ask 'is this the right direction?' That's faster and gives the user something concrete to react to. The skill should specify exact commands for running tests (e.g., `pytest tests/test_foo.py::test_bar -xvs`) rather than saying 'run the test.' The reference files are a problem — if the agent has to read 3 files to understand the skill, the skill isn't done. Inline the critical content. The 'commit after each cycle' rule needs a concrete command: `git add -A && git commit -m 'test: add failing test for checkout with valid cart'` — with a template.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor — correctly identifies that agents need procedures, not philosophy. The concrete suggestions (numbered checklist, exact commands, commit templates) are immediately actionable and would measurably improve agent compliance.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist — lots of interesting ideas (TDD as design feedback, bug-fix TDD, characterization tests) but no prioritization. If we add all of this, the skill becomes bloated and loses focus. The skill needs to be opinionated about scope.

What All Missed

None of the advisors addressed test naming as a failure mode. Bad test names (`test_checkout_method_returns_true`) are everywhere in real codebases and the skill's naming advice is buried in a reference file. Also, none addressed what to do when tests are slow — TDD grinds to a halt if test feedback takes >10 seconds, and the skill is silent on test suite performance.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker — reframing the problem as 'behavioral intervention, not knowledge transfer' is the most insightful observation in the entire debate. It explains why the current skill doesn't work: it's trying to convince when it should be instructing.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian — correctly identifies the 'blank file paralysis' problem but offers no solution beyond 'the skill needs a section on decomposition.' That's a diagnosis, not a fix. The emotional/psychological dimension is overstated for agents — they don't get frustrated or lose motivation.

What All Missed

No advisor discussed the skill's triggering mechanism. The description says 'use when the user wants to build features test-first' but this is vague. Should this skill always be active? Only on explicit request? The skill's utility depends entirely on when it triggers, and nobody questioned this.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider — the 'when does this trigger' question is the most important observation. If the skill triggers on every coding task, it's noise. If it triggers only on explicit request, it's underused. The side-by-side comparison suggestion for anti-patterns would make the skill dramatically more useful.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor — the 'cut philosophy entirely' suggestion is too aggressive. Some context on why vertical slices work better than horizontal ones is necessary for the agent to make good decisions when the situation doesn't match the checklist. Checklists without understanding produce cargo-cult behavior.

What All Missed

Nobody addressed the skill's relationship to the project's existing test infrastructure. The skill assumes a `tests/unit/` and `tests/integration/` structure but doesn't mention what to do if the project has a different layout. Also, no advisor mentioned parameterized tests — a critical TDD accelerator for testing multiple inputs against the same behavior.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Contrarian — the 'blank file paralysis' problem is real and the current skill has zero guidance for it. The decomposition gap (user can't articulate testable behaviors) is the #1 reason TDD fails in practice, and the skill's 'ask the user to approve the plan' step assumes the user has this knowledge.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker — the 'agents don't need convincing' argument is partially wrong. Agents do need context for edge cases. A pure algorithm works for standard cases but fails when the situation is novel. The skill needs both: a default procedure AND the reasoning to deviate from it when appropriate.

What All Missed

No advisor discussed the skill's reference architecture. Three reference files is a lot — are they all necessary? Could the mocking content be inlined since it's the most actionable? The refactoring content is the least critical and could be a separate skill. The skill should be self-contained with references as optional deep-dives.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Expansionist — the 'TDD as design feedback' angle is genuinely missing from the skill and would elevate it from a testing technique to a design methodology. The suggestion to include a literal checklist that agents tick through is practical and aligns with how agents actually work.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider — the 'this triggers on every task' concern is valid but the solution isn't to narrow the trigger, it's to make the skill's workflow lightweight enough that it doesn't matter. A 3-step loop (RED→GREEN→COMMIT) is cheap enough to apply everywhere. The friction concern is about the current 7-item planning checklist, not the concept of TDD itself.

What All Missed

Nobody addressed what happens when TDD doesn't make sense. Sometimes you're exploring an unfamiliar API and need to spike first. Sometimes the behavior is purely visual (CSS changes) and a test is overhead. The skill presents TDD as universally applicable — it needs a 'when NOT to use TDD' section to be credible.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council agrees on three things: (1) The skill needs a concrete, step-by-step workflow rather than philosophical prose. (2) The reference files should be consolidated or inlined — requiring 3 file reads to understand one skill is too much. (3) The anti-pattern section (horizontal slices) is the strongest part and should be expanded with concrete examples. There is also broad agreement that the 'blank file paralysis' problem — not knowing how to decompose a feature into testable units — is the biggest gap in the current skill.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on how much philosophy to keep. The Executor and First Principles Thinker want to cut it entirely; the Contrarian and Expansionist argue context matters for edge cases. The Outsider wants more concrete examples; the Expansionist wants to add more content (TDD for bugs, characterization tests, design feedback). The Executor wants to minimize scope; the Expansionist wants to maximize it. There's also disagreement on whether the skill should always trigger or only on explicit request.

Blind Spots Caught

The council collectively missed: (1) test suite performance as a TDD enabler — if tests take >10 seconds, TDD is impractical; (2) the skill's triggering mechanism needs clarification; (3) 'when NOT to use TDD' is absent and the skill's credibility suffers for it; (4) parameterized tests as a TDD accelerator; (5) the skill assumes a specific project test directory structure without stating it.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill as a decision procedure with three sections: (1) WHEN TO USE — explicit trigger conditions and a 'when NOT to use' escape hatch, (2) THE LOOP — a numbered RED→GREEN→COMMIT cycle with exact commands, inline the critical mocking and test-strategy rules from the reference files, (3) TROUBLESHOOTING — blank file paralysis, slow tests, and 'test won't go red' scenarios. Keep the anti-pattern section but add concrete before/after examples. Cut the philosophy section to a two-sentence preamble. Add a 'TDD for bug fixes' variant (reproduce → fix → commit). Make the planning checklist lightweight: 3 items max, with a 'just start' escape for small tasks.

First Step

Inline the mocking rules from references/mocking.md into the main SKILL.md (the 'mock at system boundaries only' rule and the httpx.MockTransport pattern are the most critical), then restructure the Workflow section as a numbered loop with exact pytest commands. This is the highest-impact, lowest-effort change that addresses the Executor's and First Principles Thinker's concerns while preserving the skill's depth.

25 / 26

ui-design-direction

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/ui-design-direction/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can this skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a fatal dependency on a Python search script that may not run in the user's environment, and the skill itself admits this with a quiet 'if the script cannot run, say so and continue with manual reasoning.' That's a design failure, not a fallback — it means the core workflow silently degrades to the same generic design advice the skill claims to replace. The bundled data (67 styles, 96 palettes, etc.) is useless if the retrieval mechanism is fragile. The skill also has no versioning or update strategy for the bundled references; design trends age fast, and stale data is worse than no data because it gives false confidence. The 'persist' feature writes files to the user's project without explaining cleanup, collision handling, or what happens when the user runs it twice with different inputs. The stack list includes 'nuxtjs' and 'nuxt-ui' as separate entries, which is confusing — are these distinct guidance sets or overlapping? The skill also claims to support 'healthcare, fintech, security' but provides no domain-specific validation or compliance awareness (HIPAA, WCAG AA, PCI) beyond generic 'trust signals.' The pre-delivery checklist is a good idea but placed at the very end where it's easy to skip. Overall, the skill is a promising concept wrapped in a fragile execution model with no graceful degradation path that actually works.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

What problem does this skill actually solve? It claims to provide 'concrete UI direction grounded in bundled local reference data.' But the real user need is: 'I have a design decision to make and I need a well-reasoned recommendation with tradeoffs.' The bundled data approach assumes that pre-curated style/palette/typography catalogs are the right abstraction layer. But design decisions are contextual — they depend on brand, audience, content density, and existing design language. The skill's architecture treats design as a lookup problem when it's actually a reasoning problem. The search script is a database query, not a design consultant. What's missing is a framework for reasoning about design tradeoffs: when to prioritize conversion vs. accessibility, when a trendy style undermines trust, how to balance brand personality with usability heuristics. The skill should lead with decision frameworks, not data retrieval. The 'high-confidence guidance vs optional taste' section is the most valuable part of the entire skill — it's where actual design reasoning lives. That section should be the core, not an afterthought. The search script should be a supplement for inspiration, not the primary workflow. Restructure around: (1) identify constraints, (2) reason about tradeoffs, (3) recommend with justification, (4) validate against anti-patterns. The bundled data becomes a reference for step 3, not the entry point.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a goldmine it hasn't fully exploited. The persist feature creates design system files — that's the seed of a much larger workflow. Imagine: the skill generates a living design system that evolves with the project, tracks decisions in a design-tokens.json, and can be consumed by both the agent and the codebase (CSS custom properties, Tailwind config). The skill could integrate with the repo's actual CSS or component files to audit the existing design against the recommendations. It could detect when the project's actual colors or fonts diverge from the chosen system. The stack-specific guidance could go beyond tips — it could generate starter component code for the detected stack. The skill could also support design critique mode: point it at an existing codebase and it evaluates the UI against the bundled UX guidelines, flagging real anti-patterns in the actual code. Another missed opportunity: the skill could maintain a changelog of design decisions (why we chose this palette, why we rejected glassmorphism) that becomes documentation for future contributors. The 'hostile design lead review' mentioned in the description is never defined as a distinct mode — it should be, with its own adversarial checklist. Finally, the skill could support A/B testing guidance: when the user is torn between two directions, generate both with measurable criteria for choosing.

🟡 The Outsider

Reading this skill for the first time, I'm confused about several things. First, the description is enormous — it's a wall of keywords that tries to cover every possible UI scenario. A fresh reader won't know if this skill is for them or not until they've read the entire paragraph. The skill says 'resolve the installed skill directory first' but doesn't explain how — what does 'resolve' mean? Find it? Verify it exists? The search script is referenced as `<skill-dir>/scripts/search.py` but there's no instruction for what `<skill-dir>` resolves to in practice. The 'common mistakes' section lists six items but they're all written from the perspective of someone who already understands the skill — they don't help a new user avoid mistakes they haven't made yet. The example commands are helpful but there's no example of what the output looks like, so the user doesn't know what success looks like. The 'response format' section prescribes a five-part structure but doesn't explain when to deviate — what if the user only wants a color palette? The skill also never explains what the bundled data actually contains in human terms. '67 styles' — what does that mean? Are these CSS frameworks? Visual movements? Without a one-line explanation, the numbers are meaningless. The skill would benefit from a 30-second 'here's what this skill does' summary at the top, before the execution details.

🟠 The Executor

The fastest improvement: move the pre-delivery checklist to the top of the skill and make it the execution gate. Right now it's buried at the end where no one reads it. Second: add a one-paragraph 'quick start' at the very top — what to type, what comes out, what to do next. Third: the search script dependency is a single point of failure. Add a two-line fallback that doesn't require Python: 'If the script fails, use the reference tables in the bundled data directory directly.' Fourth: the 'common mistakes' section should become inline guards in the workflow steps, not a separate section. For example, step 1 should say 'Do not guess the stack — verify from repo or prompt' directly, not as a mistake listed later. Fifth: the persist feature needs a collision policy — what happens when `design-system/<project-slug>/MASTER.md` already exists? Overwrite? Append? Prompt? This is unspecified and will cause real user confusion. Sixth: the skill should detect the stack automatically from the repo (look for package.json, tailwind.config, etc.) rather than requiring the user to specify it. All of these are small, concrete changes that improve the skill without restructuring it.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker. The insight that design is a reasoning problem, not a lookup problem, is the most fundamental critique here. The observation that the 'high-confidence guidance vs optional taste' section is the most valuable part — and should be the core — is a structural insight that would genuinely improve the skill. This response identifies the architectural flaw rather than just surface issues.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian. While the critique of the fragile search script is valid, the response doesn't acknowledge that the skill already has a fallback ('continue with manual design reasoning'). The fallback may be weak, but it exists. The Contrarian also misses that the persist feature's collision handling could be a deliberate design choice (let the user manage it) rather than an oversight.

What All Missed

None of the reviewers addressed the skill's description field — the YAML frontmatter description that controls triggering. It's 150+ words, which is far too long for reliable triggering, and mixes use cases with non-use cases in a way that could cause false positives or missed triggers. The description should be a concise, scannable summary, not an exhaustive enumeration.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The Executor. Every suggestion is concrete, actionable, and small. Moving the checklist to the top, adding a quick start, detecting the stack automatically — these are improvements that could be implemented in under an hour with outsized impact. The collision policy for persist is a real gap that would cause user-facing bugs.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist. The ideas are exciting (living design systems, A/B testing guidance, design critique mode) but most of them would triple the scope of the skill. The skill is already trying to cover 13 stacks and 9 domains — adding automated repo auditing and design-token generation is a different project entirely. The Expansionist doesn't grapple with the skill's actual scope constraints.

What All Missed

No one evaluated the quality or currency of the bundled data itself. 67 styles, 96 palettes — are these curated or auto-generated? How often are they updated? Is there a mechanism for the community to contribute new entries? The skill's value is entirely dependent on the quality of this data, and no advisor examined it.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider. The observation that the skill lacks a 30-second summary is the most important usability issue. If a user can't understand what the skill does in 30 seconds, they won't use it. The confusion about `<skill-dir>` resolution is a real onboarding blocker. The point about example output being missing is also strong — without knowing what success looks like, users can't evaluate whether the skill worked.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker. While the 'reasoning over lookup' argument is intellectually sound, it undervalues the practical utility of curated data. Not every design decision needs a full tradeoff analysis — sometimes a developer building a side project just needs a good color palette fast. The skill's data-driven approach serves a real audience that the First Principles Thinker's framework-first approach might slow down.

What All Missed

No one addressed the skill's relationship to the skill-creator skill. This skill has evals and bundled data — there should be a process for evaluating whether the search script actually returns good results for common queries, and whether the bundled data covers real-world use cases. The skill-creator skill exists for exactly this kind of validation.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Contrarian. The point about stale data being worse than no data is genuinely important and underappreciated by the other reviewers. The observation that the skill claims domain awareness (healthcare, fintech) without compliance-specific guidance is a real gap that could lead users astray. The 'nuxtjs' vs 'nuxt-ui' confusion is a concrete ambiguity that should be resolved.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor. While the suggestions are practical, they're all incremental. The Executor doesn't question whether the skill's fundamental approach (bundled data + search script) is the right architecture. Sometimes the fastest improvement isn't the most important improvement. The Executor would optimize a flawed foundation rather than question the foundation.

What All Missed

No one discussed the skill's error handling for the search script. What happens when the script returns zero results? What if the query is nonsensical? What if the bundled data files are corrupted or missing? The skill says 'if the script cannot run, say so' but doesn't address partial failures, empty results, or malformed data. This is a real gap in the execution model.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Expansionist. The idea of making the persist feature generate machine-readable design tokens (CSS custom properties, Tailwind config) is the single highest-leverage suggestion in the entire debate. It would bridge the gap between design direction and implementation, making the skill's output directly consumable by code. The design critique mode (audit existing code against UX guidelines) would also be a genuinely differentiated feature.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Outsider. While the usability issues are real, the Outsider's suggestions (add a summary, explain `<skill-dir>`) are things that should be obvious to the skill author. They're table stakes, not insights. The Outsider also doesn't engage with the skill's actual design content — the bundled data, the workflow, the reasoning quality. The usability fixes are necessary but not sufficient.

What All Missed

No one addressed internationalization or localization. The skill mentions 'healthcare, fintech, security' across different stacks but never considers RTL layouts, cultural color associations, or locale-specific design patterns. For a skill that claims broad domain coverage, this is a significant blind spot.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council agrees on three things. First, the search script is a fragile single point of failure and the fallback to 'manual reasoning' is inadequate — the skill needs a more robust degradation path or should restructure around reasoning-first with data as supplement. Second, the pre-delivery checklist should be moved to the top of the skill as an execution gate, not buried at the bottom. Third, the YAML description is too long and needs to be concise for reliable triggering. There is also broad agreement that the 'high-confidence guidance vs optional taste' section is the most valuable part of the skill and should be elevated in the workflow.

Where the Council Clashes

The First Principles Thinker and the Expansionist clash on architecture: the former wants to restructure around design reasoning frameworks with data as a supplement, while the latter wants to expand the data-driven approach into living design systems and code generation. The Executor and the Contrarian clash on priority: the Executor wants incremental fixes now, while the Contrarian argues the fundamental approach has fatal flaws that incremental fixes won't resolve. The Outsider and the Expansionist clash on scope: the Outsider wants a simpler, more approachable skill, while the Expansionist wants to add critique mode, A/B testing, and token generation.

Blind Spots Caught

The council collectively missed three issues: (1) the quality, currency, and update mechanism for the bundled data — the entire skill depends on this and no one examined it; (2) error handling for partial script failures, empty results, or corrupted data; and (3) internationalization/localization considerations for a skill that claims broad domain coverage. Additionally, no one evaluated the skill's triggering mechanism against real-world usage patterns or referenced the skill-creator skill for validation methodology.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill in three phases. Phase 1 (immediate): Move the pre-delivery checklist to the top, add a 30-second summary, shorten the YAML description, add explicit error handling for script failures and empty results, and add a collision policy for the persist feature. Phase 2 (short-term): Elevate the 'high-confidence guidance vs optional taste' section to become the core reasoning framework, with the search script as a supplement for inspiration rather than the primary entry point. Add automatic stack detection from repo files. Phase 3 (medium-term): Add a design critique mode that audits existing code against bundled UX guidelines, and make persist output machine-readable design tokens (CSS custom properties, Tailwind config) alongside the markdown documentation. Throughout all phases, establish a data update process for the bundled references.

First Step

Move the pre-delivery checklist to the top of the skill as an execution gate, and add a one-paragraph quick-start summary that explains what the skill does, what the user types, and what output to expect. This is the highest-impact, lowest-effort change that addresses the most common failure mode: users (and agents) not knowing what success looks like.

26 / 26

webapp-testing

/home/minder/projekty/oh-my-slop/skills/webapp-testing/SKILL.md

Framed Question

How can the webapp-testing skill be improved? What's missing, weak, redundant, or could be better structured? Consider: clarity, completeness, triggering accuracy, examples, edge cases, anti-patterns, organization, and real-world effectiveness.

5 Advisors

🔴 The Contrarian

This skill has a serious identity crisis. It claims to be about 'web application testing' but is really just a 'how to write Playwright scripts' tutorial wearing a testing costume. There's zero mention of test assertions, test frameworks (pytest-playwright), fixtures, CI integration, or what constitutes a passing vs failing test. The examples don't use pytest at all — they're standalone scripts. Nobody runs tests this way in production.

The video recording section is bloated to the point of distraction. Nearly a full page of the skill is devoted to orange-dot pointer overlays and ripple effects. This is demo/presentation polish, not testing. It inflates the skill's surface area and competes with the actual testing guidance. If a user needs a marketing video, that's a different job.

The decision tree is theatre. The static vs. dynamic split is obvious to anyone who has used a browser. The 'Don't start a second server' rule appears three times (pitfalls, decision tree, evals) — the skill doesn't trust the reader.

The evals.json has gaps: no eval for the console_logging example, no eval testing the `with_server.py` multi-server case in a realistic scenario, and nothing testing failure modes (what happens when the server doesn't come up, when selectors are ambiguous, when Playwright itself isn't installed).

The `scripts/with_server.py` uses `subprocess.Popen` with `shell=True` and pipes stdout/stderr but never reads them, meaning server errors are silently swallowed. The skill never tells the user it might want to check those streams. That's a trap.

The API gotchas section mixes trivial trivia (triple-click) with genuinely important warnings (HTMX swap waits) at the same level. A reader doesn't know which to prioritize. The HTMX section is good — it's the most valuable part of the whole skill — but it's buried at the bottom instead of being front and center as a core testing pattern.

🔵 The First Principles Thinker

Strip this down. What problem does the skill actually solve? The answer: 'The user wants to programmatically interact with a local web application using Playwright and get back useful evidence.' That's it. Everything else is ornamentation.

From that core, the skill should answer exactly these questions:
1. How do I set up a Playwright sync script for local use? (boilerplate)
2. How do I make sure the server is running and reachable? (with_server.py)
3. How do I find the right elements on the page? (reconnaissance pattern)
4. How do I wait properly for dynamic content so my script doesn't flake? (wait strategies)
5. How do I capture evidence — logs, DOM, screenshots, video? (artifacts)
6. How do I use that evidence to answer a testing question? (assertions)

The current skill answers 1-5 passably but completely drops the ball on #6. 'Testing' implies a pass/fail judgment. The skill never shows how to make an assertion or report a test result. Without that, these are browser automation scripts, not tests.

The video section tries to solve a separate problem: 'How do I make a shareable visual record?' That should be a sub-pattern, not a first-class section. Right now it's ~30% of the skill's weight.

The API gotchas are good but they're curated trivia, not a principle. The underlying principle is: 'Use the current Playwright sync API, verify in the Playwright docs if you see a method you're not sure exists, and prefer explicit waits over implicit ones.' Gotchas are just illustrations of that principle.

Restructure around the six questions above. Cut the bloat. Make assertions first-class citizens. Move video to an 'advanced artifacts' subsection.

🟢 The Expansionist

This skill is sitting on a much bigger opportunity than it realizes. It's positioned as a narrow Playwright helper, but it could be the agent's entire local webapp verification surface. Here's what's adjacent and missing:

**Network interception and API assertion.** Real webapp testing often means verifying that the right API calls were made, with the right payloads. Playwright's `page.route()` and `page.on('request')` / `page.on('response')` are incredibly powerful. The skill could show how to intercept a POST, assert the request body, and verify the response — all without leaving the browser context. This is the single biggest gap.

**Authentication state management.** Testing any real app means logging in. The skill should show `context.storage_state()` to save auth state and reuse it across scripts, avoiding repeated login flows. This is a massive time-saver and a real-world necessity.

**Multi-page / tab testing.** Many webapps open popups, new tabs, or OAuth flows. The skill has zero guidance on `context.expect_page()` or handling popup windows.

**Accessibility assertions.** Playwright can integrate with axe-core. A single example showing `page.accessibility.snapshot()` or an axe-core check would make this skill dramatically more valuable for teams that care about a11y.

**Performance timing.** `page.evaluate(() => performance.getEntriesByType('navigation'))` gives real load metrics. For a skill about testing webapps, ignoring performance is a missed opportunity.

**Parallel script execution.** The `with_server.py` helper could support running multiple Playwright scripts against the same server. The skill hints at this but never explores it.

The video overlay system is genuinely clever — it could be extracted into a reusable library or even a separate skill. The orange-dot pattern is the kind of thing every team reinvents. Package it properly and it becomes a product of its own.

🟡 The Outsider

I just read this skill cold. Here's what confused me:

The description says 'testing, debugging, reproducing, or demonstrating' — that's four different activities with different goals. A test needs assertions and a pass/fail. A repro needs minimal steps to trigger a bug. A demo needs to look good. These have different shapes, but the skill mostly treats them as the same thing: write a Playwright script, maybe record a video.

The 'Core workflow' section says 'report both the observed UI result and browser-side evidence' but never defines what 'observed UI result' means in practice. Am I supposed to print the page text? Return a boolean? Write a markdown report? There's no output format, no template, no example of what a completed 'test run' looks like from the user's perspective.

The examples directory has four files, but the SKILL.md examples section just lists their names with one-line descriptions. I have to go read them separately to understand what they teach. At minimum, each example should have a 'when to use this' note inline in the skill.

The `with_server.py` helper is referenced heavily but its interface is only discoverable by running `--help`. The skill should show the help output or at minimum document the key flags inline. Right now I'd have to leave the skill context to figure out how to use a core dependency.

The 'Common pitfalls' section has four items, all prefixed with ❌. But the 'Best practices' section has seven items with no corresponding ❌. The asymmetry makes it feel like the pitfalls are an afterthought rather than a structured anti-pattern catalog. Why not pair each pitfall with its corresponding best practice?

The video recording code example is 80 lines long. That's more than a third of the entire skill's code blocks. A reader who doesn't need video has to scroll past a wall of overlay JavaScript to find the next section. This should be collapsed behind a reference to the example file.

Finally: the skill says 'Prefer small, task-specific scripts over framework-heavy test harnesses unless the project already has one.' But it never tells me what 'already has one' looks like. How do I check? What's the signal?

🟠 The Executor

The fastest path to making this skill actually useful: add a complete, copy-pasteable test script template that includes assertions. Right now every example is a standalone script with no test structure. Give me a template that looks like this:

```python
def test_homepage_loads():
with sync_playwright() as pw:
browser = pw.chromium.launch(headless=True, args=[...])
context = browser.new_context(viewport={...})
page = context.new_page()
page.goto(url)
page.wait_for_load_state('networkidle')
assert page.locator('h1').inner_text() == 'Expected Title'
assert page.locator('.error').count() == 0
context.close()
browser.close()
```

That's what's missing. Everything else is refinement.

Second priority: fix the `with_server.py` stdout/stderr problem. Add a `--verbose` flag or at minimum document that server output goes to pipes and how to surface it. Right now server failures are invisible.

Third: add one eval that tests a failure scenario — server doesn't start, selector not found, assertion fails. The current evals only test happy paths. A skill about testing must show what failure looks like.

Fourth: move the video recording section to the end as an 'Advanced: Video Artifacts' appendix. It's too prominent for how rarely it's needed. The core skill should be about testing, not cinematography.

Fifth: add a one-line 'check if Playwright is installed' guard at the top of the skill. Something like `python -c "from playwright.sync_api import sync_playwright; print('ok')"`. The global AGENTS.md has a whole section on Playwright detection — this skill should reference that checklist, not pretend Playwright is always available.

Do these five things and the skill goes from 'Playwright tutorial' to 'actually useful testing guide.' Everything else is polish.

5 Peer Reviews

Reviewer 1
Strongest

The Executor nails it. The absence of a test script template with assertions is the skill's most embarrassing gap. A testing skill without a single `assert` statement in its core guidance is a browser automation skill mislabeled. The Executor's five-step improvement plan is concrete, ordered by impact, and immediately actionable. The suggestion to reference the global AGENTS.md Playwright detection checklist is especially good — it respects the existing rule system instead of ignoring it.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist. Network interception, auth state, accessibility, performance — all valid points, but this is scope creep masquerading as vision. The skill is 8.9KB. Adding all of these would triple its size and dilute its focus. The Expansionist doesn't acknowledge the tradeoff: every adjacent feature added makes the core workflow harder to find. Some of these (auth state, network interception) genuinely belong. Others (performance timing) are a different skill entirely.

What All Missed

Nobody addressed the skill's triggering description. The description says 'Use when testing, debugging, reproducing, or demonstrating a local web application with Playwright' — but it doesn't say when NOT to use it. Should the agent use this for a 5-minute smoke check? For a full E2E suite? The description should set scope boundaries. Also, nobody mentioned that the examples use hardcoded paths like `/tmp/` and `/mnt/user-data/outputs/` without explaining when to use which — that's confusing and inconsistent.

Reviewer 2
Strongest

The First Principles Thinker provides the clearest structural diagnosis. Reducing the skill to six questions is elegant and immediately actionable. The observation that the skill answers 1-5 but 'completely drops the ball on #6 — assertions' is the single most important critique in the entire debate. The call to restructure around workflow questions rather than feature categories (video, gotchas, pitfalls) would make the skill dramatically more navigable.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Contrarian. Calling the decision tree 'theatre' is clever but wrong. For an LLM agent, explicit decision trees are genuinely useful — they reduce ambiguity in branching logic. The agent doesn't 'obviously' know whether to start a server or not; it needs to be told. The Contrarian's critique of the video section is valid, but dismissing the entire skill as a 'tutorial wearing a testing costume' is uncharitable and doesn't offer a constructive path forward.

What All Missed

Nobody discussed the relationship between this skill and the global AGENTS.md rules. The global file mandates Playwright for webapp E2E testing, requires Docker-based test environments for integration/E2E, and specifies TDD as the only allowed strategy. This skill doesn't reference any of that. It shows standalone scripts, not Docker-based execution. It doesn't mention TDD. There's a real risk that following this skill in isolation produces code that violates the project's testing rules. The skill should at minimum link to or acknowledge the global testing requirements.

Reviewer 3
Strongest

The Outsider asks the questions a first-time reader actually has. 'What does the output of a completed test run look like?' is something every user will wonder and nobody else caught. The observation about asymmetric pitfalls vs. best practices formatting is a small but real UX issue — paired ❌/✅ items would be scannable; the current structure forces the reader to mentally match them up. The point about examples needing 'when to use this' labels is also spot-on and trivially fixable.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Executor. The five-step plan is solid but the suggestion to 'add a one-line Playwright guard' misunderstands the skill's role. The skill is guidance for the agent, not a runtime script. Playwright detection is handled by the global AGENTS.md (section 10). Duplicating that logic here creates a maintenance burden when the global rules change. A reference would be fine; a guard would be redundant.

What All Missed

Nobody evaluated the quality of the existing examples on their own terms. The `static_html_automation.py` example uses `page.wait_for_timeout(500)` after form submission — that's an anti-pattern the skill itself warns against (prefer explicit waits). The `console_logging.py` example saves logs to `/mnt/user-data/outputs/` which is a pi-specific path that won't exist in most environments. These are concrete bugs in the skill's own examples that undermine its credibility.

Reviewer 4
Strongest

The Contrarian's critique of the `with_server.py` helper is the most technically precise observation in the entire debate. The script pipes stdout/stderr but never reads them, meaning server errors are silently swallowed. This is a real bug that will cause confusing failures in practice. The Contrarian is also right that the HTMX wait guidance is the most valuable content in the skill and deserves to be prominent, not buried in a gotcha section. Elevating it to a core pattern would improve the skill's signal-to-noise ratio immediately.

Biggest Blind Spot

The Expansionist's suggestion to add accessibility testing via axe-core is good in isolation, but the suggestion to add performance timing, multi-page handling, network interception, AND auth state management all at once is a recipe for an unfocused skill. The Expansionist doesn't prioritize — everything is 'the single biggest gap.' In practice, you'd pick one or two of these and do them well. The lack of prioritization makes this advice hard to act on.

What All Missed

Nobody mentioned the skill's license field. It says 'Complete terms in LICENSE.txt' but the skill content itself is instructional markdown — the license is for the code (examples, scripts). This should be clarified. More importantly, nobody discussed whether the evals are actually runnable. Eval 4 asks the agent to 'reproduce the disappearing toast after login' — but there's no app provided. How is this eval supposed to execute? The evals need either a fixture app or a mock server to be meaningful.

Reviewer 5
Strongest

The Executor's critique is the most actionable. The absence of assertions is the skill's fatal flaw, and the Executor is the only advisor who says so with a concrete code template. The suggestion to add a failure-path eval is also critical — a testing skill that only tests happy paths is like a smoke detector that only works when there's no fire. The Executor's advice to move video to an appendix is pragmatic and would immediately improve the skill's focus.

Biggest Blind Spot

The First Principles Thinker. Reducing the skill to six questions is intellectually clean but practically incomplete. The framework ignores the 'how do I set up Playwright in the first place?' question, which is a real barrier. The global AGENTS.md has a 5-minute timeout rule, sudo restrictions, and Docker-based test environments — none of which are 'principles,' but all of which are constraints that shape how the skill gets used. A principles-only framework that ignores operational constraints is incomplete.

What All Missed

Nobody addressed the skill's name vs. its content. It's called 'webapp-testing' but it's really 'playwright-scripting-for-agents.' The name sets an expectation of test methodology, coverage, assertions, and reporting. The content delivers browser automation scripts. This mismatch means the skill will disappoint users who expect actual testing guidance and will be ignored by users who want Playwright help but don't think to look under 'testing.' Either rename the skill or add the testing methodology content that the name promises.

Chairman's Verdict

Where the Council Agrees

The council unanimously agrees on three things: (1) The skill lacks assertions and test structure — it's browser automation guidance mislabeled as testing. (2) The video recording section is disproportionately large and should be demoted to an appendix or advanced section. (3) The HTMX wait guidance is the skill's most valuable content and deserves prominent placement, not burial in a gotcha section. There is also broad agreement that the examples need better contextual labels and that the evals only cover happy paths.

Where the Council Clashes

The council clashes on the decision tree's value — the Contrarian calls it theatre while the Outsider and First Principles Thinker see it as useful scaffolding for agent behavior. They also clash on scope: the Expansionist wants to add network interception, auth state, accessibility, and performance testing, while the Executor and First Principles Thinker want to cut scope and focus on the core workflow. The Contrarian and Expansionist disagree on whether the video overlay system is bloat or a product opportunity.

Blind Spots Caught

The entire council missed three critical issues: (1) The skill doesn't reference or integrate with the global AGENTS.md testing rules (Docker-based environments, TDD mandate, Playwright detection checklist), creating a real risk of contradiction. (2) The examples contain anti-patterns (hardcoded `wait_for_timeout`, pi-specific paths) that undermine the skill's own guidance. (3) The evals aren't actually runnable without a fixture app — eval 4 asks the agent to reproduce a bug in an app that doesn't exist in the eval context.

The Recommendation

Restructure the skill around a six-step workflow (setup → server → reconnaissance → interaction → assertion → evidence). Add a complete test script template with real assertions as the primary example. Demote video recording to an 'Advanced Artifacts' appendix. Elevate the HTMX wait pattern to a core section. Fix the `with_server.py` stdout/stderr swallowing bug. Add paired ❌/✅ formatting to pitfalls and best practices. Add at least one eval with a failure scenario. Reference the global AGENTS.md Playwright detection and Docker testing rules. Fix the example anti-patterns (replace `wait_for_timeout` with explicit waits, use `/tmp/` consistently). Consider renaming the skill to 'playwright-automation' or adding genuine test methodology content to justify the 'testing' name.

First Step

Add a complete, copy-pasteable test script template with assertions to the Core Workflow section. This is the single highest-impact change: it transforms the skill from a Playwright tutorial into an actual testing guide, and it takes less than 20 lines of code. Everything else — restructuring, appendix reorganization, eval fixes — builds on having a proper test template as the skill's anchor.
